**Title:** The Psychology of Leadership
**Authors:** Sébastien Page
**Language:** en
**Publisher:** Harriman House
**Date:** 2025-03-05T16:40:28+00:00
**Description:** <i>The Psychology of Leadership</i> offers a revelatory fresh take on business leadership through the lens of groundbreaking research in positive, sports, and personality psychology. Witty, conversational, and personal, <i>The Psychology of Leadership</i> blends research, fascinating true stories, humor, and self-improvement advice to deliver simple yet powerful principles to master the mental game of leadership. Leaders will develop what feels like mind-reading abilities for interpreting workplace personalities, hidden motivations, and group dynamics. They will learn how to inspire their organization to move mountains, improve their ability to listen, communicate and, when necessary, persuade. Along the way they will dramatically improve their own mindset and resilience.

Principles to Perfect Your Management of Individuals, Teams, and… Yourself

Sébastien Page

To Anne, the love of my life, and our wonderful children, Olivia and Charlie.

Foreword by H. Lawrence Culp Jr., Chairman and CEO of General Electric

When the board of GE asked me to take over as Chairman and CEO, I said no twice. As a money manager, Sébastien Page often talks about diversification. After leaving Danaher, I built a life portfolio diversified between family, teaching, fishing, skiing, company boards, and private equity investing.

Yet, in the fall of 2018, on a flight back from visiting a company, my decision to turn down the job gnawed at me. My wife Wendy saw it in my eye. She saw the pressure and uncertainty, of course. But she also realized—before I did—that this was a challenge I needed to meet.

This wonderful book discusses “meaning,” an elusive but all-important aspect of positive psychology and corporate leadership. When I joined the GE team as Chairman and CEO, it was with a deep sense of meaning. This is a company with a history that goes back 130 years to Thomas Edison and holds one of the world’s most iconic brands. GE’s people develop innovative products that change and improve the lives of billions. We take great pride and purpose in our mission to build a world that works. As Sébastien says in one of his principles, when we talk about meaning, we mean it.

Back in 2018, there were a lot of people who thought our “why” at GE really didn’t matter. I thought it mattered a lot, and I appreciate that even more today.

Our company has evolved rapidly over the last five and a half years. We reduced our debt burden by over $100 billion between 2018 and 2023, all the while improving our operations through lean, focusing on delivering customer value and eliminating waste. Our operational and cultural transformation has enabled us to take our next historic step—creating three industry-leading, global public companies focused on vital sectors of healthcare, energy, and aerospace.

Lean goes beyond process. It requires teamwork, transparency and candor, and respect for people. Leaders must listen to those closest to the work—whether on the factory floor, out with the customer, or processing a transaction. Everyone should feel empowered to make suggestions to reduce waste and improve quality. At GE, we ask, how can we learn so we can do better next time? It’s one thing to talk about continuous improvement, but another entirely to operationalize it across an organization at scale and sustain it. Sébastien distills the latest research in positive, sports, and personality psychology into simple principles to master these crucial aspects of leadership. He lays the foundations for high personal and organizational performance.

In his book, Sébastien also talks about strong leadership and having “thick skin.” I recall when Sébastien presented to the board of T. Rowe Price several years ago, where I was a director. He submitted a growth target for his multi-asset money management business. Instead of a specific goal, he used a range.

I challenged him: “What’s your precise target, and how are you aligning your strategy and incentives to this target?”

This prompt didn’t rattle Sébastien; instead, we had a candid discussion that was richer in detail and nuance when dealing with specifics. Sébastien and the team needed to feel confident operating with transparency and trust to have that conversation. This is why this book on the psychology of leadership is so important.

Sébastien also explains the power of “core beliefs”—the foundational and deeply ingrained long-term beliefs that shape how an individual and an organization see the world and think about business. My grandfather founded a small welding and machine shop in 1937, just outside Washington, D.C., in the Maryland suburbs. At an early age, I had a window into what it means to run a business—not part of a business, but the whole thing. I learned what’s required to listen to what customers need from you, which is the only way to succeed over time. To this day, I’ve kept my grandfather’s payroll register to remind me of my own core belief—that we exist so that the real people on that payroll can take care of the customers we serve.

Leadership requires humility. It requires making tough decisions. Always remember why your company exists in the first place. It doesn’t exist to boost your ego.

The mental game

On October 14, 2012, Austrian skydiver Felix Baumgartner jumped from a space capsule 24 miles above the earth’s surface to become the first skydiver to break the sound barrier, reaching a top speed of 843 mph. You read that right: he jumped from space. A little-known fact is that a psychologist coached Baumgartner. His psychologist, Michael Gervais, said: “At the elite levels of sports, the game is played above the shoulders. The paper-thin margins between victory and defeat are more often than not determined by how skilled an athlete is at using their mind.”

Sébastien will help you unleash the science and practice of psychology to thrive. Business is not all that different from sports. To be an elite leader, remember that the game is played above the shoulders. This is what this book is all about.

H. Lawrence Culp Jr.

Spring, 2025

Introduction

A few weeks before I started working on this book, I struggled at work. Looking back now with hindsight, the issues I was dealing with weren’t out of the ordinary. Yet at the time they were affecting my well-being, creeping into my state of mind, and impacting the quality of my sleep.

That’s typical, isn’t it? In the fog of daily life, our challenges seem worse than when we look back on them a few months or years later.

My wife, Anne, noticed I was stressed.

“Do something about it!” she said.

I could tell she wouldn’t let this go, so I asked for advice.

Sports psychologists help elite athletes deal with performance stress and are experts in coaching and team dynamics.

I found one nearby. Could he help me in my job as a leader?

As I walked into his office, my outlook on leadership was about to change forever.

About me

But first, let me admit another motivation for this book. I have a writing addiction. I get antsy if I don’t write for a while (the same as if I don’t exercise). Writing soothes my mind and soul. I’ve kept this addiction under control—it’s never interfered too much with my day job. In fact, I’ve almost always made writing part of my job.

To introduce myself, I grew up in the French-speaking province of Québec, Canada. I completed a Master of Science in Finance and earned the Chartered Financial Analysts designation. I began my career in the United States as a Canadian immigrant who could barely speak English. I was lucky to land an unpaid internship at State Street in Boston, Massachusetts.

The rest of my story has been an American dream.

At State Street, I became the youngest associate named Senior Managing Director in the firm’s 200+ year history. Later, at PIMCO, I co-led a transformational initiative to build a global Solutions business.

For the last nine years, I’ve led the division responsible for managing Multi-Asset portfolios (those that combine stocks and bonds) at T. Rowe Price. We’ve launched new products and expanded into new markets, including the UK, China, Hong Kong, Singapore, and Australia. Our business has grown substantially.

My father, a retired finance professor, was puzzled when he found out I was writing a book on the psychology of leadership.

“You should stay in your lane—write another finance book,” he said.

When he realized I was serious, he rhetorically asked: “Are you in HR now?”

By which he meant human resources.

He was discounting my more than two decades of leadership experience. Unlike most self-improvement authors, I have skin in the game. I’m addicted to writing, but I’m not a “writer.” My job is to lead a business. You’re reading this book for self-improvement. I wrote it for the same reason.

![](image/1.jpg)

Anne’s kick in the pants, asking me to deal with my stress, motivated me to meet with the sports psychologist. The story he told me during our first discussion was the spark that started this project. It put me on a path of discovery. I spent four years investigating new ways to apply psychology to leadership. I plowed through hundreds of books and articles in scientific psychology.

What I learned has been life-changing and worth sharing with the world. I became a better leader at work, acquired wisdom, and developed resilience in all aspects of my life.

But to my father’s point, I ventured outside my formal training. I don’t hold a PhD in psychology. An embarrassing number of literary agents refused to represent me for that reason. (I thought money management was the most rough-and-tumble industry… until I met book publishing.)

Yet a surprising number of scientific breakthroughs happen when innovators venture outside their immediate domain of expertise. Besides, psychology research uses some of the same statistical methodologies I’ve employed throughout my career in quantitative economics and finance.

Importantly, I used my role in the C-suite of a Fortune 500 company as my lab, testing principles to evaluate their effectiveness in the real world.

I hired the sports psychologist as a consultant for over 150 hours along the way. To be clear, this book is my project. The content is my own. But I wanted to make sure I didn’t misinterpret any of the research in psychology, especially in areas where I might be the first to apply certain models to leadership. So, the sports psychologist became this book’s scientific referee. And yes, he does hold a PhD in psychology.

Let’s meet him.

This book’s origin story

Dr. Daniel M. Zimet looks to be in his fifties. In top physical shape, he wears a neatly trimmed beard. Don’t all credible male psychologists wear a beard? He has the straight-backed, calm demeanor of someone who’s mastered self-discipline. He seems at peace with himself and with the world. His appearance screams professionalism. Except for one thing: the goofy white tennis shoes.

To be precise, these are handball shoes. By handball, I mean the sport that looks like squash but is played with your hands. Zimet holds 38 national titles. He’s based near where I live in Maryland. He’s an expert in mental performance counselling and has worked with pro soccer players, Olympic athletes, and NFL players.

The first time we met, he told me the story of his best-ever handball match.

By the time the 1997 three-wall Nationals came around, he’d already lost eight times to Kendall Lewis, a ranked professional handballer. “If you were to see us side by side, the results wouldn’t surprise you. Kendall was built like an NFL tight end. I’m scrawny and scrappy,” Zimet said.

The match started at five on Saturday afternoon. By then, many competitors had been eliminated and had moved on to the second tournament attraction: the beer truck.

The first game went the same way as many others between the two players. Zimet ground out bread-and-butter points while Kendall made spectacular shots.

But unexpectedly, Zimet eked out a 21–20 victory after an hour of play.

Lewis found his rhythm in the second game and prevailed 21–7, sending the match into a tiebreaker.

It started to get dark. The crowd was thick when the players entered the court for the final 11-point game.

The venue’s lights came on as the players passed the second hour of grueling play. The crowd swelled. The fans became so boisterous that the event director stopped matches in the adjacent courts. Those fans added to the ones already watching this incredible match. Beer was flying in the stands.

Kendall served for the fourth time to win the match point. Trying something new, he hit an overhand serve.

Zimet’s return was weak and short, a perfect opportunity for Lewis to hit a low kill shot.

More than twenty years later, as we sit in his office, Zimet recalls what happened next like it was yesterday:

I reached deep into my depleted well of strength and sprinted as fast as I could to the front of the court, where Kendall’s match-ending shot seemed to fall in slow motion. At full sprint and stretch, I caught up to the ball an inch from the ground and hit the perfect shot—a flat kill. I rolled over on the concrete to break my speed. On my knees, I squeezed both hands into fists and screamed at the top of my lungs. The crowd’s roar was so overpowering that I couldn’t hear my voice.

His voice is trembling. I could swear his eyes are getting watery. But what happens next is a bit strange. He tells me the rest of the story nonchalantly.

He was down 9–10 and failed to score on his next serve. Then, Kendall served an ace.

“Wait, you lost?” I ask. “How was that your ‘best moment’? What about all your wins?”

How could a world-class athlete be elated by defeat? Zimet explains his rationale: “Measurable goals can impede self-motivation. Those who focus on mastery—rather than on the scoreboard—are the biggest winners in the long run. That time, I played to the absolute best of my abilities.”

This match changed Zimet. From then on, he knew he belonged amongst the best in the game.

“Every victory stands on that match’s shoulders. That match, that point, and that loss solidified my quality as a player, a competitor, and a person, making it the most important moment of my career as an athlete,” he tells me.

Have you ever met anyone who cherishes failure above victory? Zimet has won 38 national titles, but he’s most proud of this match he lost.

His theory became an epiphany for me. I asked myself: What if leaders could develop a similar mindset and apply it to their organizations?

A leader’s job is to inspire others, so you might wonder why I’m starting with a story about a singles handball match. The psychology of leadership requires you to understand yourself so you can understand others. It’s about empathy, leading by example, and personal resilience. You put on your oxygen mask before helping others.

Many lessons from this illustration of Zimet’s mindset apply to the psychology of leadership. The most important takeaway is that intrinsic motivation is found somewhere deeper than on the surface of measurable goals. Intrinsic motivation goes beyond the scoreboard.

Of course, he loves to win, but Zimet defines success in terms only a sports psychologist could, focusing on mastery over ego, the importance of relationships and community (“The crowd’s roar was so overpowering that I couldn’t hear my voice”), the meaning of sport as a meritocracy, and engagement and flow. To him, all these concepts came together that day on the court. This book teaches you how they come together in timeless leadership principles.

The research

Psychology has many branches. The well-known clinical branch addresses mental illnesses such as depression and severe anxiety. Clinical issues are outside the scope of this book.

Psychology’s positive side is underappreciated in our culture. Famed academic psychologist Martin Seligman argues that psychology can help people thrive in all domains of life. In an interview with the LA Times, he said that “psychology is much bigger than just medicine or fixing unhealthy things. It’s about education, work, marriage—it’s even about sports. What I want to do is see psychologists working to help people build strengths in all these domains.”

Over decades of scientific research, academics studying the human mind have published a treasure trove of insights related to well-being and high performance. This research has accelerated in recent years.

In addition to sports psychology, two more branches that offer untapped leadership insights are positive and personality psychology. The common thread across these branches is that they’re not focused on clinical issues. Rather:

* Positive psychology studies what makes people thrive—think of it as the science of long-term happiness.
* Sports psychology focuses on the mental game of high performance for individuals and teams. It overlaps with positive psychology in several areas.
* Personality psychology defines common traits that help us to understand and sometimes predict people’s behavior.

In 2022, 491 academic research articles were published with “positive psychology” in their titles, compared with 428 with “clinical psychology.” Publications ranging from scholarly papers to blogs have touted the importance of relationships and meaning in people’s lives.

Yet, more needs to be done to apply positive psychology to redefine how to lead businesses and other organizations. I will give you tools to use the science of thriving. You will learn how to emphasize relationships and meaning to maximize organizational performance.

The story is the same for the latest research on sports psychology, which abounds with applications for leaders. An increasing number of top athletes and business leaders work with sports psychologists. Mental skills are critical to peak performance, and they are trainable.

The secret of sports psychology is that it’s not about winning. It’s about losing! It’s about how to maximize learning and build mental resilience from losses. Here’s what tennis legend Roger Federer told the class of 2024 at the Dartmouth College commencement:

In the 1,526 singles matches I played in my career, I won almost 80% of those matches. Now, I have a question for all of you… what percentage of the POINTS do you think I won in those matches? Only 54%. In other words, even top-ranked tennis players win barely more than half of the points they play. … The truth is, whatever game you play in life, sometimes you’re going to lose. A point, a match, a season, a job, it’s a roller coaster, with many ups and downs. … You want to become a master at overcoming hard moments. That to me is the sign of a champion.

Every leader knows they can’t always win. In my industry, money managers are acutely aware that there’s no such thing as a crystal ball. Some of their bets will lose money. In 2021, when hedge fund manager Nicolai Tangen took over as CEO of Norway’s Sovereign Wealth Fund—becoming responsible for overseeing the world’s largest investment portfolio, valued at $1.3 trillion—one of his first moves was to hire a sports psychologist as a full-time member of this team.

Personality psychology also offers a powerful toolset to improve the practice of leadership. Susan Cain’s book Quiet: The Power of Introverts in a World That Can’t Stop Talking generated interest in personality psychology from the business community. Cain praises the power of introverts (from the personality trait extraversion). However, four equally important traits are still unexplored in leadership practice. Research in psychology has produced 88 new articles on these personality traits (including extraversion) over the last two years alone. Cain opened the door to applying personality psychology to leadership. I intend to follow her lead.

Here’s a primer on the key well-known and lesser-known models in research-based psychology that we will explore:

* PERMA: A model of positive psychology developed by Martin Seligman. “P” stands for positive emotions. The remaining four letters are considered the formula for long-term well-being: “E” stands for engagement, “R” for relationships, “M” for meaning, and “A” for accomplishment.
* Core beliefs: The filters by which you interpret the world. Core beliefs are your default assumptions. They drive how to react to challenges and opportunities. They are typically established early in life, but they can evolve. Companies also have core beliefs, sometimes called values or “we believe” statements.
* Yerkes-Dodson law or “curves”: A model used in sports (and popular) psychology to map performance to your degree of arousal (activation) or stress. This law assumes that performance increases with stress, but up to an optimal point, after which anxiety hurts performance.
* Control theory: Originally developed for applications in science and engineering, control theory has been used in scientific psychology to explain how people interpret the world around them, separate signal from noise, make multi-period decisions, and adjust to setbacks and feedback.
* The Big Five: A popular model in personality psychology that seeks to explain how people typically behave based on five traits: extraversion, agreeableness, neuroticism, openness, and conscientiousness. These traits are heritable and learned, and they have major implications for collaboration in the workplace.

The principles

I prefer to describe the principles in this book as timeless rather than new or groundbreaking. The same ideas presented in modern leadership books are found in the writings of philosophers, famous religious figures, and prophets throughout history. Leadership is timeless. Humans have designated leaders for as long as we’ve organized ourselves into groups. The best leaders have always shown personal resilience, planned for the long term, pursued goals with meaning, separated signal from noise, and understood personalities.

In addition to fresh examples and applications, I will make new connections with the latest research in psychology. To my knowledge, this is the first book to distill research on these models into simple principles for leadership—for example, using PERMA to set personal and organizational goals, applying Yerkes-Dodson to optimize organizational stress, and deploying the full power of personality psychology in the workplace.

We will toggle between self-improvement and leadership. We will ask: What does this principle mean for you as an individual and a leader?

These principles will give you a fresh perspective on setting goals, executing them, leading (and reading) people, and managing your stress.

Each principle represents an aha! moment when I connected my real-world experience with scientific psychology. Some of them are offbeat and counterintuitive, including:

* Don’t Die on Everest—Beware of the Side Effects of Measurable Goals
* Ignore the Gifted Kids—Don’t Focus So Much on Winning
* Think About Death—Always Work Backwards from Your Goals
* Learn to Love to Worry—Harness the Positive Side of Neuroticism
* Be Disagreeable, Sometimes—Don’t Run Your Business Like a Democracy

Some principles may appear contradictory because they apply to different leadership challenges. I will encourage you to focus on mastery rather than winning and use the power of competition and social comparison. I will ask you to exercise strategic patience and embrace frequent feedback and adjustments. I will resolve these contradictions by explaining when you should apply each principle.

And unlike in traditional self-improvement books, I will address the caveats behind the research. I won’t insult your intelligence. Research in psychology continues to evolve in fascinating directions. The human mind is still largely a mystery.

One more promise: we will cover a lot of ground. Get your highlighter ready. Rather than repeat a single idea ad nauseam—something self-improvement and leadership books tend to do nowadays—we will cover a breadth of stories, research, and advice on the broad theme of the psychology of leadership.

Along the way, I will share my perspective on what Jeff Bezos, Elon Musk, the band Metallica, Snoop Dog, Japanese dancing mice, and TV shows like Game of Thrones and Succession have to do with the psychology of leadership. We’ll even meet a Human Piranha on Steroids.

Let’s start with goals. Buckle up!

Part 1: Setting Long-Term Goals

My friend john dedicated three decades of his life to running a charitable foundation in Baltimore, helping alleviate poverty.

One day, as he was eating cereal in his kitchen, an angel appeared to him.

To thank him for his generosity, the angel said: “I can give you one of the following three presents: infinite beauty, infinite wisdom, or infinite wealth. Pick one.”

(This is where I should admit that this is not a true story.)

Which one would you choose? Infinite beauty seems purposeless. But for most people, the choice between wisdom and wealth may be harder.

John chose wisdom.

Soon, word got out that someone had attained infinite wisdom for the first time in history. The media went bananas. Everyone wanted to know what John would have to say. The pressure became so intense that he decided to call a press conference.

With microphones in his face and cameras flashing and clicking, John looked out at his audience. The room went silent. The world went silent. What unbearable suspense! What was John going to say? What would humanity learn from the man with infinite wisdom? Surely, civilization was about to change.

“I should have taken the money,” John said.

To make an effective choice in this situation, John—or anyone—would need to have clarity on their long-term goals. There’s nothing wrong with pursuing wealth, especially to rise above the poverty level. But when it comes to long-term well-being, having lots of money—even an infinite amount—is vastly overrated.

The principles in Part 1 use positive psychology—the science of happiness—to help you set goals. I ask you to think long-term and inspire your organization with more than trite mission statements.

What’s your life mission? What truly matters to you and the people in your organization?

You’ve heard that employee engagement, culture, and meaning are important. Start with why! has become a corporate cliché. But practical advice on how to start with why has been lacking. How do you integrate these concepts into goals and practices that drive success? And what does the latest research on happiness reveal? Part 1 answers these questions, starting with something about Russian roulette.

Principle 1: Don’t Die on Everest—Beware of the Side Effects of Measurable Goals

“The most successful people aren’t directly pursuing conventional notions of success. They’re working hard and persisting through difficulties because of their internal desire to control their lives, learn about the world, and accomplish something that endures.”

—Daniel H. Pink, bestselling author

Let me ask you an uncomfortable question: What would it take for someone to convince you to put a gun to your head and play Russian roulette? Suppose the hypothetical gun had one hundred chambers and was loaded with four bullets. You’d have a four percent chance of dying. How big would the reward have to be for you to spin the chamber and press the trigger? Would you do it for a million dollars? Ten million? One hundred million?

Click!

On May 10, 2023, Pieter Swart posted a hopeful message on his Instagram. The 63-year-old Canadian anesthesiologist had wanted to reach the summit of Mount Everest since he was nine. “The defining phase of our expedition is approaching,” he wrote. Six days later, he posted a photo of the Khumbu icefall at 17,999 feet above sea level. “Perfect day,” he wrote. His ascent was going well.

And then it wasn’t.

On summit day, Swart fell sick. He decided to forgo his lifelong dream to reach the top of the world. He was so high in altitude that helicopters could not reach him. He was in Everest’s so-called “death zone.” There, oxygen is so sparse that no one can survive for long. With urgency, Swart needed to hike 1.2 miles down the steep and icy path from Camp IV to Camp II.

On his way down, he died. The cause was an “undefined respiratory event,” with symptoms consistent with “high altitude pulmonary edema,” according to a statement by a colleague.

The spring 2023 Everest season was the deadliest in history. If we include five missing climbers presumed dead, the death toll stands at 17. Yet, there were no massive earthquakes or extreme weather events. Experts and Nepali officials blame many factors, including climber errors, inexperienced guides, crowdedness, and climate change. Most Everest deaths aren’t predictable or preventable. Swart’s altitude sickness was sudden and unexpected.

The reality is that climbing Everest is a deadly idea to begin with. If you’ve reached the summit of Everest, I admire and respect you. At the same time, let me ask you: why?

I mean, there’s an area called the DEATH ZONE. You climb over frozen dead bodies to get to the summit. I like to think I have a sense of adventure. Still, I struggle to understand why any rational individual would want to climb Everest—let alone reach the summit 28 times, as world record holder Kami Rita Sherpa has done. Over the last 30 years, four of every 100 climbers who have summited have died—hence my Russian roulette question at the start of the chapter.

Reaching the summit of Everest may be an irresistible goal because it’s a challenging and prestigious achievement. More than anything else, it’s a well-defined, measurable goal. You either reach the summit, or you don’t. This measurability could be the number one factor contributing to deaths on the mountain.

How does this relate to leadership? The leader’s job is to set goals for their organization and individuals. The more measurable the goals, the better.

For example: “Grow revenues in Europe by 10% over the next 12 months.”

We set such goals for my division. Clear, metrics-oriented goals can be powerful. They motivate people, create a sense of accountability, and make performance evaluations more transparent. Also, research shows that sharing goals—and the metrics used to measure progress against them—improves collaboration. It’s easier to collaborate when you know what others are trying to achieve.

However, like medicine, goals can have side effects. Measurability is a powerful active ingredient. It makes a goal more effective and more susceptible to side effects.

In a research article with the cheeky title “Goals Gone Wild,” the authors warn that mismanaged goals can lead to “a narrow focus that neglects non-goal areas, a rise in unethical behavior, distorted risk preferences, corrosion of organizational culture, and reduced intrinsic motivation.”

Traditional leadership advice treats these side effects as fine print. Most leaders ignore them because, like many aspects of human psychology, they’re counterintuitive.

Principle 1, “Don’t Die on Everest—Beware of the Side Effects of Measurable Goals,” helps you manage this pitfall of the psychology of leadership.

The power of measurable goals

High-performing organizations diligently use goals. Individuals, teams, and even entire countries are motivated by setting goals and measuring outcomes.

“We choose to go to the moon,” President John F. Kennedy said in 1962, “… because that goal will serve to organize and measure the best of our energies and skills.”

For years, I’ve evaluated employees’ performance on outcomes against goals. A strong compliment in my performance evaluations would be that you’re “outcome-oriented.” By that, I mean that you focus on results. You don’t let distractions get in the way. You get things done. Some people seem wired that way—it’s a trait I’ve noticed in high performers.

Measurable goals are a subset of goals. In today’s data-driven business world, metrics have become indispensable, and, as an executive at a Fortune 500 company, I couldn’t do without them. The measure can be continuous (a company’s profits, the score at a football game, the number of pounds you want to lose), or discrete, i.e., pass or fail (getting promoted, reaching the top of Everest, landing on the moon).

However, later I’ll argue that the most important goals aren’t easily measurable. These goals relate to positive psychology concepts such as engagement, positive relationships, and meaning. I’ll suggest how to make these goals work for your organization.

For now, we begin with the counterintuitive downsides of measurable goals and how to manage them. Research in psychology has revealed three important side effects: goal-induced blindness, reduced intrinsic motivation, and failure to differentiate between luck and skill. If we are not careful, if we adhere too much to the measurability of goals, then we will encounter these problems.

Let’s look at each problem in turn.

Goal-induced blindness

Goal-induced blindness happens when you’re so obsessed with reaching a goal that you don’t see the negative consequences of your actions. You lose sight of the big picture. Mount Everest deaths are an example of goal-induced blindness cited in the psychology literature. Climbers obsess over the measurable goal of reaching the summit. They sacrifice safety and sometimes die in the process.

There are many other ways in which the focus on outcomes leads to undesirable side effects. Some people decide to bend the rules. Others sacrifice their work-life balance, even their health.

In the early years of my career in finance, I worked in the research and trading division at State Street. I managed a small team that provided asset allocation advice to large institutional clients. Most of the time, we had to crunch numbers and deliver recommendations quickly because our firm was competing to win the related portfolio reallocation mandates. We had clear goals for growing our activities. We counted the studies, the recommendations, and the wins/losses of trading mandates. We covered the globe. Hundreds of studies every year. It was fast-paced, and I loved it.

But I developed goal-induced blindness. I wasn’t taking care of myself. I was traveling non-stop, and I wasn’t getting enough sleep. Most of the time, upon waking up, I would need a few minutes to remind myself which time zone I was in. If a salesperson asked me to fly to Japan for a presentation with one or two-days’ notice, I would be happy to rise to the occasion. It made me feel important. I spent most of my time on the road while remotely managing the rest of the team via BlackBerry (remember those?).

At some point, I developed a head cold that lasted a year. I was stressed. I was exhausted. And I wasn’t spending enough time with my wife, Anne (a saint who has always supported me). It took me a while, but I eventually realized that my frenetic work ethic was dumb. Just plain dumb. Lack of sleep weakened my immune system and made me less productive. I recommend Matt Walker’s book Why We Sleep on the adverse effects of lack of sleep.

I’ve often noticed high performers who aren’t maximizing their potential because they focus on a narrow set of measurable goals at the expense of work-life balance. Most of the time, it’s not full goal-induced blindness, but it’s a blind spot. At a town hall, I told my division that work-life balance makes you more productive. I firmly believe that. Anyone involved in fitness—whether strength or cardio—knows that rest periods between bouts of effort lead to better performance over time. The overall balance of work and life is the same.

Companies can also suffer from goal-induced blindness. Think of Wells Fargo employees opening dummy accounts, presumably to increase the measurable goal related to the number of new accounts opened. Or Volkswagen cheating their carbon dioxide emission numbers. Or trading and investment firms taking too much risk to juice short-term returns. Examples abound.

One of the most powerful aspects of the psychology of leadership, borrowed from economics, is that people respond to incentives. As a leader, you should encourage your team to strive toward ambitious goals and, by all means, attach incentives to them.

However, you should insist on two non-negotiable rules. First, no one should ever compromise their well-being. In the long run, a mentally and physically healthy team will crush any overworked competition. Second, make it crystal clear that everyone should stay miles away from any ethical grey zone.

I don’t like acronyms… but here I can’t help it. To prevent goal-induced blindness, let me propose the SEE model:

* Systematize: Take out the human element by creating indicators to flash red when goal-induced blindness is creeping in. Metrics for well-being can include employee turnover, absenteeism, or HR complaints. For ethics, every major corporation has a whistleblower line, where people can anonymously report violations. Other indicators may include customer complaints or, counterintuitively, extraordinary performance against measurable goals (think: Bernie Madoff’s investment performance, which was too good to be true).
* Encourage: Most leaders know how to use incentives when motivating employees toward a measurable goal. Here’s a different idea: Use incentives to prevent goal-induced blindness. Congratulate employees when they take time off. Self-care is needed for extraordinary long-term performance. On the ethical side, the traditional approach is to reward whistleblowers. I believe these programs are useful, but not as much as a positive approach. Give a special bonus to an employee who sacrificed a goal instead of acting against the firm’s values or doing something dodgy from a legal perspective. The difference with a whistleblower approach is that here, you encourage self-reporting. You want people to internalize the desire to do the right thing.
* Exit: Once you’ve systematized and encouraged, you must establish exit rules. Research on the psychology of decision-making under uncertainty reveals that leaders are terrible at quitting, hanging on to goals even in the face of clear evidence that they should abandon them. Managing director of expedition company Adventure Consultants, Guy Cotter, has been climbing Everest since the early 1990s. He’s been playing Russian roulette repeatedly. Click, click, click! Of course, there are ways to manage risk on Everest and bring the four percent probability of death down to a lower number. Cotter claims that the key is to be prepared to abandon the summit goal. To get better at quitting, you and your organization must pre-commit to abandoning your goal under specific scenarios before goal-induced blindness sets in.

There you go: To prevent goal-induced blindness, you must SEE. Cute, I know.

Reduced intrinsic motivation

Research in psychology differentiates between two ways people define engagement: based on ego factors, such as how one ranks relative to peers; and mastery factors, such as personal improvement. It turns out that those who focus on self-improvement and define their goal as mastery of a new skill or some such aspiration perform better than those who focus on outcome. Self-motivation is a powerful force. Again, the world of sports provides a proof point: Athletes perform better if they work towards self-improvement rather than focusing on the score and similar metrics.

It’s the difference between a golfer whose goal is to impress others with a low handicap and another who aims to improve their swing (and derives personal satisfaction in doing so). Both players want to improve. Both players are highly motivated to win. The difference is the why. Intrinsic whys are the most powerful. Sports performance coaches teach their mentees to focus on them. From Lee Crombleholme, a world-renowned pro golf coach:

It’s not how much you’re motivated. It’s the underlying reason for your motivation [that matters].

Crombleholme differentiates between the ego and mastery mindsets. With the mastery mindset, we focus on what we can control, and our self-confidence improves, no matter the final score (the outcomes). There’s a link to stoic philosophy here, but that’s a topic for another book.

Everest expert Guy Cotter talks of a shift in mindset. Climbers should make the journey, not the summit, the goal: “It is as if people are interested only in the goal of having climbed Everest and not climbing Everest as a major achievement in their climbing career.”

I must admit that I’m more in the ego mindset camp. I know this doesn’t sound very good, but let’s be transparent: I care about what my colleagues think of me and how much money I make. I think almost everybody does. But I’m trying to redefine my why towards the mastery side.

How can I become better at what I do for the sake of self-improvement?

That’s something I can control more easily than the opinion of others.

At the organizational level, I stress about the performance numbers for our portfolios and the growth numbers for our business. And again, there are good reasons why measurable goals are essential in the corporate world. We need to keep score, as far as I’m concerned.

But I also focus on what my team can control: our process. What makes us better investors? Should we change how we run meetings? Can we improve how we interpret financial and economic data? How do we maximize the impact of collaboration to enhance our investment decisions?

Traditional goals focus on outcomes, while mastery goals focus on process. Both are effective. It’s important to set goals for process improvements. Don’t overemphasize outcomes over process.

Failure to differentiate between luck and skill

In a world in which we can’t predict much of the future (in my business, we say there’s “randomness”), good decisions can lead to bad outcomes, and bad decisions can lead to good outcomes. This has implications for the psychology of leadership because leaders make decisions under uncertainty, and unfortunately, our egos are hardwired to fall into the trap of confounding luck and skill. You must be clinical about your wins and losses.

Suppose you decide to drive drunk, but you make it home safely. That was a bad decision with a good outcome. One week later, after a good night of drinking California wine, you ask a designated driver to drive you home. The driver gets into an accident. That was a good decision with a bad outcome.

Because of randomness, the outcomes we measure against goals are often silent on the quality of decisions. Worse, they can mislead. This problem is acute in the investment world. You can make money, at least for a while, by making bad decisions, like holding a concentrated portfolio or investing in fads. If you don’t examine your process and the quality of your decisions, in other words, if you only focus on outcomes, you may think you’re an absolute genius. But you’re unlikely to be a successful investor in the long run.

Annie Duke’s excellent book Thinking in Bets, has become required reading in the investment world. Duke is a professional poker player and business consultant. She explains that we instinctively associate good results with good decisions and bad results with bad decisions. She calls this instinct “resulting.” But she explains that in poker and many aspects of life, “winning and losing are only loose signals of decision quality.”

Reaching the summit of Everest is a good outcome. Was setting this goal a good decision?

As a leader, recognize that attaining your goal does not necessarily mean you’ve made good decisions along the way. This requires a remarkable level of self-awareness and a focus on your decision-making process rather than outcomes. Also, if you miss your target, remember that it’s possible you made the right decisions but got unlucky. That’s easier to tell yourself! (A mentor once told me that there are only two types of investors: those who are talented and those who are unlucky.)

Takeaways to improve your mental game

Measurable goals are essential in business and life. They enhance communication, collaboration, and performance evaluations. They motivate and align organizations. They take people to the moon. However, research in psychology reveals that, like performance-enhancing drugs, an excessive focus on measurable goals can have side effects.

Here’s my advice:

* Learn to prevent goal-induced blindness with the SEE model: systematize warning signals, encourage preventative behaviors, and exit based on stop-loss rules. Don’t lose sight of the big picture. Don’t cheat. Ever. It’s not worth it. Take care of yourself and prioritize work-life balance for increased productivity.
* Adopt a mastery mindset rather than an ego mindset. Define your why in terms of developing your skills and your organization’s capabilities rather than reaching specific outcomes you can’t control.
* Differentiate between luck and skill. Good decisions can lead to bad outcomes, and bad decisions can lead to good outcomes. Know the difference. Whatever the outcome, make sure you examine your decision-making process.

Lastly, if after reading this chapter you feel an urge to climb Everest “because it’s there” or for some other similarly bogus reason, here’s the most useful advice you’ll receive in this book: don’t.

Principle 2: Don’t Become a Miserable Rich Person—Redefine Success

“Happiness is the precursor to success, not merely the result.”

—Shawn Achor, author and positive psychology advocate

For most of my professional life, my goal was to advance my career. It’s all I thought about. Aside from family, which has always been my priority, deep questions about long-term goals and happiness were left in a mental fog as everything else was subsumed to the aim of professional advancement.

Over the last few years, I’ve achieved major goals. The nature and scope of my professional responsibilities are beyond my expectations. There are always stresses and frustrations, but I can say without hesitation that I’m happy with my current job. In fact, I love it. Some of the fog, or goal blindness, is starting to clear. Now that I have arrived at this place, I have begun to wonder for the first time if I am setting goals that will increase my happiness.

I’ve never tried to answer that question before. I’ve used my instinct, as you do when you walk in the fog. Looking back, I would not change any big decisions—moving from Canada to the US to work at State Street in Boston for ten years, to PIMCO in Newport Beach for five years, and lastly, to T. Rowe Price in Maryland.

But I’ve gone through rough patches as an individual contributor and a people leader. Occasionally, I would have benefited from a long-term perspective on the most basic question: Am I pursuing the right goals?

Principle 2, “Don’t Become a Miserable Rich Person—Redefine Success,” introduces the use of positive psychology, aka the science of happiness, to set better long-term goals for you and your organization. To do so, you must ask the right questions, some of which aren’t typically discussed in the leadership context. You must change how you think about goals and, ultimately, success. The next four principles, discussed in the following chapters, will dig deeper into these questions and give you additional practical leadership advice.

Reversing the happiness equation

In his book Finding Flow: The Psychology of Engagement with Everyday Life, the world-renowned academic psychologist Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi argues that “Everything we do is ultimately aimed at experiencing happiness. We don’t really want wealth, or health, or fame as such—we want these things because we hope that they will make us happy.”

When we set goals for ourselves or an organization, we define “success.” Most people think that success brings happiness.

Of course, it’s not that simple. Some successful people are unhappy. In his excellent memoir titled Open, Andre Agassi reveals that even when he was at the top of his game, ranked as the best tennis player in the world, he was miserable.

“A win doesn’t feel as good as a loss feels bad. Not even close,” he explains.

For most of his career, starting at a very young age, he kept thinking, “I hate tennis.”

In the corporate world, there are plenty of successful organizations with miserable employees. Anyone who’s worked on Wall Street knows what I’m talking about. One day, a friend commented that at his company (a world-leading money management firm), despite all the Ferraris in the parking lot, he had “never seen so many miserable rich people.”

Some people thrive in combative, in-your-face environments. These are the type of people who love the smell of napalm in the morning. But my friend explained that even when they were making lots of money for clients and winning new mandates, the knives were always out at his firm. Salespeople were at each other’s throats to take credit for new business wins, and portfolio managers were hoarding research and money-making trade ideas.

To be clear, these are merely counterexamples, perhaps exceptions, to the Success = Happiness equation. There’s nothing wrong with being successful! It must be fantastic to become an elite tennis player. I’m sure most of them are quite happy. I suspect many who focus on intrinsic goals are enjoying their pro career more than Agassi did. And I believe in capitalism, especially compared with other failed models. Companies should maximize profits for shareholders as long as they also care for employees, clients, and the environment.

My point is that we should tighten the links between goals and happiness for people and organizations.

To do so, we need to reverse the success = happiness equation. Shawn Achor, a leading expert on positive psychology and author of The Happiness Advantage, argues that happiness leads to success, not the other way around! Before you set goals, ask yourself, what’s your definition of happiness? From that definition, what would success look like?

Questions like “How do you define happiness?” and “What does success look like?” are popular at leadership seminars. One of my friends from the UK has an understated way of expressing frustration. Instead of blowing up in anger, he’ll say, amplifying his British accent: “This is incredibly irritating.”

Well, when I attend leadership training seminars, I find these questions incredibly irritating. They’re too personal. I don’t know you, seminar-moderator-person, and you’re asking me an intimate question right off the bat in front of my colleagues. Executive coaches do this as well. They get too personal, too fast. At least buy me a drink first! These are questions you want to ponder on your own. Find a quiet room or go on a walk, perhaps on a Sunday morning with a cup of coffee.

I said earlier that I don’t like acronyms. They’re overused in business. But when it comes to asking deep questions about happiness, there’s one acronym from positive psychology that I love. I believe I’m the first to use this model to develop leadership principles. It rolls off the tongue and is easy to remember.

The PERMA model

The PERMA model was developed by Martin Seligman, who’s considered the founder of positive psychology.

PERMA describes five dimensions of happiness: positive emotions (P), engagement (E), relationships (R), meaning (M), and accomplishments (A).

The key takeaway is that happiness is not just about positive emotions. Laughter, pride, getting “likes” on social media, enjoying a great bottle of wine, and other short-term joys are satisfying, but these emotions aren’t nourishing. They’re like sugar highs for the soul.

Moreover, some people are more predisposed to experience positive emotions than others. Think about that for a second. It seems incredibly unfair, doesn’t it? Seligman explains that:

Cheerfulness is normally distributed in the human population. That means that there are 3 billion people in the world right now who don’t feel happy, or merry, or cheerful. Cheerfulness is strongly genetically based. It’s about 50% heritable.

For example, there’s a link between genetics and major depression. It runs in families. When he says “normally distributed,” Seligman implies that the distribution is symmetrical. This means that if, say, 10% of the population is chronically depressed (the left tail of the distribution), then 10% of the population must be chronically joyful (the right tail). Some people annoyingly always seem cheerful, I suppose.

But depression and happiness aren’t opposite sides of a single dimension. Happiness has its own scale. Not feeling many positive emotions doesn’t equal depression and doesn’t preclude you from thriving—that’s where the ERMA part of PERMA comes in.

Another important characteristic of a normal distribution is that most people fall close to the middle. Most of the population is neither chronically depressed nor chronically joyful. Most people—the center of the curve—lean slightly positive. But we all lean in one direction or the other. Think about people around you—people you know well, your family, your colleagues. Aren’t some of them mostly cheerful, while others tend to walk around with a dark cloud over their head?

My daughter Olivia is on the cheerful side. My son Charlie leans to the melancholic side (and so do I). Anne and I have known this about them since their early childhood. They seem wired differently. Seligman explains that you can tell whether someone’s smile is genuine by looking at their eyes or, more precisely, the small muscles below them. This photo, taken a few years ago, is illustrative. It seems to me that while they’re both smiling, Charlie’s eyes are sad, while Olivia’s are cheerful.

![](image/IMG0562.jpg)

Here’s the tough question: Where do YOU fall in this distribution?

Seligman explains that if you focus on positive emotions, you can climb the distribution by about 10–15%.

“I know tricks to get you to live in the upper part of a set range,” he says.

The crucial part of this sentence is “of a set range.” Given this limited upside, and for many other reasons, we need to expand the definition of happiness beyond P, positive emotions, with the ERMA part of the model.

There isn’t much you can do about your biologically endowed happiness. But ERMA is entirely within your control.

Let’s look at the ERMA part of the PERMA model: engagement (E), relationships (R), meaning (M), and accomplishments (A).

This principle asks you to buy into the basics of positive psychology and begin to ponder deep questions. The next four principles will dig deeper into each of these components, one by one.

Engagement is related to the concept of flow. You’re engaged when involved in a task that isn’t too easy (so you don’t get bored) or too difficult (so you don’t feel discouraged).

There are many examples of flow states in everyone’s lives. Think of a musician playing an instrument, a surgeon performing surgery, a tennis player in the middle of a close game, etc. I’m engaged, or in flow, when I write.

When you’re in flow, you lose track of time. It’s a state of high concentration, mental productivity, and creativity. Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi has published numerous articles and books linking flow to happiness. He makes a convincing case. The best motivation comes when participation is its own reward.

In addition to engagement/flow (E), relationships (R), meaning (M), and accomplishments (A) also contribute to happiness, even though they aren’t necessarily related to short-term positive emotions. Although my son Charlie may struggle with positive emotions (P) at times, thanks to ERMA, he can lead a happy life. Contributing to something bigger than yourself is a meaningful, wholesome form of wellness. It’s more important than just feeling happy.

Imagine the old man sitting in a rocking chair and contemplating his life, thinking it was time well spent. I’m talking about meaning, but the old man would also feel good about having built positive relationships and having worked diligently towards major accomplishments. While P is sugar, ERMA are proteins for the soul.

Here are some questions to help frame your thinking as a leader:

![](image/Table01.jpg)

This grid is perhaps the best example of important-but-not-urgent work most organizations never get to because everyone, leaders included, is sprinting on the treadmill of urgent daily tasks.

These aren’t questions you ponder between two emails. They require a block of time, a calm state of mind, and in my case, strong coffee. At the team level, think of them as offsite questions. Convene your team for a day somewhere outside the office. Print a copy of this grid for each team member. Maybe hire an experienced moderator.

And if you’re brave enough to risk a mutiny, ask everyone to shut off their cell phone for the day.

Of course, positive psychology goes beyond asking questions. It provides answers. It reveals that people thrive when they:

* engage in tasks that are challenging yet match their capabilities,
* maintain positive relationships with people around them, and
* find meaning and accomplishment in their work.

Nothing is surprising or groundbreaking here. But these conditions for thriving are timeless and often underappreciated in the traditional approach to setting goals, especially in a business context. The next four principles will give you practical actions to move your organization toward these conditions. For now, I’m merely asking you to lay the foundation by contemplating deep questions and discussing them with your team.

Takeaways to improve your mental game

Step one in deploying positive psychology into leadership practice is to reframe how you and your organization think about success.

Groundbreaking research in positive psychology proves that success rarely brings happiness. Counterintuitively, it’s the other way around: Happiness leads to success.

What should you, as a leader, learn from this research?

This principle introduces a new way of setting goals and inspiring organizations to achieve peak performance.

I recommend starting with this expanded definition of happiness and working your way back to goals. The fog should clear on your long-term ambitions and the obstacles that stand in your way. And don’t worry, ERMA has nothing to do with being less ambitious. In fact, I expect that you’ll set a higher bar for yourself.

Although, you might lose interest in buying a Ferrari.

Principle 3: Learn to Enjoy Mowing the Lawn—Set Goals That Maximize Engagement

“In the moment, man. Get in the moment and stay here.”

—Michael Jordan

My colleague david giroux is the best money manager you’ve never heard of. He’s up there with Warren Buffett, Peter Lynch, and Bill Gross. David is a five-time nominee and two-time winner of the Fund Manager of the Year award in the allocation category from Morningstar (a firm that rates funds and portfolio managers). He has beaten 99% of his peers over the last ten years. Leo Acheson, an analyst at Morningstar, wrote that David “belongs on the Mount Rushmore of the greatest investors of the decade.”

I’ve been fortunate to work with hyper-successful people throughout my career. They share common traits that we’ll discuss in this book. We’ll even spend a chapter on the psychological makeup of famous billionaires. But one aspect of David’s work style is off the charts: I’ve never met anybody with such an ability to focus. To do so, he uses the most straightforward, most powerful word in time management: “No.” That includes clients who want to give him more money to manage. If managing larger portfolios reduces his ability to beat the market, he stops accepting new money. His flagship fund has been closed for years.

By saying “No” as often as he needs to, David has organized his life to spend as much time as possible in flow (which we discussed in the previous chapter). Beyond his remarkable intellect, I believe engagement is the secret to David’s success. As a leader, he fosters engagement in those around him as well.

Most people think of flow as an individual contributor concept. We picture the tennis player who gets in the zone. David can spend hours of uninterrupted work analyzing a company’s financials to uncover insights other money managers have missed.

Yet there are applications for leadership, too. Teams can get into a state of flow. Think of the orchestra when everything clicks between individuals and the performance seems effortless. Conversations can also happen in flow, for example, when smart people get in a room to debate a tough problem, engaging their full intellect. In these conversations, time flies.

You must set the conditions for people in your organization to spend as much time in flow as possible, whether working individually or in teams.

Principle 3, “Learn to Enjoy Mowing the Lawn—Set Goals That Maximize Engagement,” introduces the groundbreaking idea of applying the concept of flow to corporate goal setting.

Let’s start with David as our case study. How does he maximize engagement or flow in his life? When I sat down with him to discuss his habits, he gave me a simple time management model. I started applying it right away, and you can too.

How to maximize engagement or flow

The principle David Giroux follows for maximizing engagement is to maximize return on time spent (ROTS).

“Ask yourself, what activities yield the greatest return on your time?” he advises.

At least once per year, David audits how he spends his time. He estimates how many hours in a typical week he spends meeting with CEOs of companies he follows, reading research, crunching numbers in spreadsheets, collaborating with his team, and so on. He takes a clinical view of each activity by estimating how much it contributes to his goal of making money for his clients. An activity may bring some value to his process, but if it’s not the highest and best use of his or his team’s time, he’d rather redeploy the time on higher-returning activities.

His ROTS audit is probably why you may not have heard of David Giroux. He rarely appears in the media, despite incessant requests. In his audits, he has found that media time is not an activity with a great return on his time. He’d rather spend his time on investment research.

Have you ever done such a ROTS audit? Most people don’t, even though it’s easy. You don’t need a spreadsheet or detailed account of your activities. Just look at the big picture. Step back and contemplate how much time you spend daily on useless emails and meetings. I suspect these low ROTS activities add up to 30%, maybe 40%, of most people’s time. Why? After all, low ROTS activities aren’t particularly enjoyable and are less likely to lead to a flow state than high ROTS activities.

Yet low ROTS activities are irresistible for two reasons. First, they have low activation cost—they require little energy to get started. It’s so easy to pull your phone from your pocket and scroll. Second, they tend to provide a short-term reward. They entice you with a quick shot of dopamine (the brain’s reward molecule). Checking your email is like pulling the lever on the slot machine—you’re looking for the possibility that something exciting may have hit your inbox. Likewise, going to a meeting instantly relieves the boredom of solitary work. In the long run, however, these activities lead to disengagement.

High ROTS activities, in contrast, often have high activation cost. But once started, they lead to higher engagement and more time spent in a state of flow. High ROTS activities are more likely to align with your long-term goals and values. And they’re more likely to recruit your full and differentiated capabilities. In the long run, they contribute to your energy reservoir rather than drain it.

We all want to make an impact. To do so, we must tackle more challenging, meaningful work than the average corporate zombie who shuffles from one status update meeting to another.

Applying ROTS as a leader

You can apply this principle to your organization. The low-hanging fruit to increase ROTS in most organizations is to cut back on meetings.

Here’s a thought experiment: Imagine that you’re setting up a half-hour status update or catch-up meeting. You pull up the calendar invite and add the names of the people you want to invite. On the right corner of the invite, you see a cost of $1,153. If you add a few people, the price goes up to $1,590. Would that make you reconsider whether the meeting is necessary or whether you need to invite so many high-powered, otherwise busy colleagues?

The company Shopify has built an app that displays the cost of each meeting on calendar invites—right there for everyone to see. The app estimates meeting costs by multiplying the time spent across invitees by their compensation. “The average size of a meeting at Shopify includes three people and the average length of a meeting is about 30 minutes. A typical meeting of this size and length would cost between $700–$1,600,” the company said in a statement.

Rolling out this app has immediately reduced the number of meetings. The COO says, “People ask questions: What is this meeting for? Why are this many people in it? And those questions will put an immense amount of pressure on organizers to organize fewer meetings—and leave the rest of us alone.”

I’m going to guess that this COO is an introvert. (We’ll learn more about personality psychology in the third section of this book.) In any case, research suggests that unnecessary meetings cost a typical company of 100 employees about $2.5 million per year. Shopify has 11,000 employees, so if we assume that meetings scale linearly with the number of employees—which is a conservative assumption because I suspect meetings increase more like an exponential function with the number of employees—Shopify could save $275 million per year.

The principle is sound: Meetings are costly, and many have low ROTS. But I don’t recommend the approach unless you’re prepared to handle the side effects.

Some meetings are necessary. The obvious downside of cutting too many meetings could be that collaboration plummets.

Whatever medicine you use to reduce meetings, make sure it’s not too strong—you could cure cancerous meetings but destroy healthy ones in the process. And there could be other unintended consequences. For example, I discussed this approach with my Steering Committee. I wasn’t suggesting implementing the approach, but I thought it would foster a good discussion. It took a few seconds for a couple of the math whizzes on the committee to describe how one could compile data across meetings and devise an algorithm to solve for everyone’s compensation. “We’ll know exactly how much you make, Seb,” one said.

Instead, encourage everyone in your organization to focus on high ROTS meetings. Eliminate the rest. High ROTS, high engagement meetings include those that:

* Unleash the creativity of the team (for example, brainstorming).
* Require nuance in addressing disagreements.
* Involve important decisions.
* Involve selling to a prospect or getting feedback from a client.

Disagreements and negative feedback should never be handled via email. Doing so leads to escalation. Email lacks nuance, and adding spectators to the cc field is too tempting. On the other hand, status updates and information-sharing should be done by email.

Unfortunately, too many people do the opposite: They lack the courage to handle disagreements in person, so they send an email. But they enjoy getting an audience for sharing information, so they set up low ROTS meetings and bore their colleagues to tears by walking them through a PowerPoint. No one likes having a PowerPoint read to them at a slower pace than they can read it for themselves. I’d rather stick a fork in my eye.

And yes, research shows that in-person meetings lead to higher trust and better communication than Zoom meetings.

You can apply the ROTS approach to your leisure time as well. For example, it’s hard to think of passive media consumption as something that brings anything meaningful to your life. Any other activity is likely to have a higher ROTS. Spending quality time with family has a higher ROTS than watching TikTok videos. Exercising has a higher ROTS than eating chips in front of the television. Reading (this book!) has a higher ROTS than binge-watching a reality TV show (I hope you know that almost all of them are scripted?). I’m not arguing for an austere lifestyle, and I love going to the movies once a week with my wife. But I hope you get the idea: You can find flow in all aspects of your life.

Engagement in boring jobs

Now, what if the work is boring? Some jobs are repetitive, monotonous, and tedious. Most organizations have them. These are critical, but thankless, high-turnover roles. To reduce turnover and increase performance, you must learn to manage the people in these roles not by force but with empathy. Put yourself in their shoes. How would you find self-motivation?

Besides, everyone has parts of their roles that are boring. Even writing this book, a passion project for me, requires me to find motivation to format, edit, and compile references.

Whatever the task or job definition, it’s possible to increase engagement. In his book Finding Flow, Csikszentmihalyi explains that when you pay attention to what you’re doing, you become more interested in your work. It’s a virtuous circle, he explains:

If you focus attention on anything, it is likely that you will become interested in it. Many of the things we find interesting are not so by nature, but because we took the trouble of paying attention to them.

There’s a direct link to mindfulness, a concept familiar to those involved in meditation. It’s a bit counterintuitive, but it helps reframe the idea that “I’m focusing on something because it’s interesting” to “something is interesting because I’m focusing on it.”

My first summer job was in general building maintenance. My friend and I worked for a real estate mogul who owned various commercial and residential buildings across the city. We swept parking lots, cleaned factories, mowed lawns, painted apartments, and so on. None of these tasks were particularly engaging. And, because this was my first job, I hadn’t developed any work ethic, which is another way of saying I was lazy. I would count the minutes between breaks.

But sometimes, I would get in the groove, and time would fly.

Looking back, the number one contributor to my engagement in a task was how easy it was to notice progress. Some tasks seemed senseless—for example, sweeping a parking lot or cleaning a factory where we barely made a dent and couldn’t see progress. These tasks were impossible to complete. My friend and I could have spent years on them. And even if you broke down the task to a smaller area, no matter how hard you tried, you couldn’t entirely remove the accumulated dirt. It was hard to measure progress, and the goal was unattainable anyway.

However, I enjoyed mowing lawns and painting apartments because the goal was clear (to finish the task), and I could track progress in real time. When you mow the lawn or paint an apartment, you can eyeball the percentage you’ve completed. That’s immediate feedback. There’s something satisfying about seeing the immediate impact of your work toward a well-defined, attainable goal.

Linear vs. non-linear goals

Mowing the lawn, or well-defined projects in general, involve what I would call linear goals. There’s little, if any, randomness involved. You rarely face setbacks. Progress is cumulative and irreversible. You work hard, you get closer to your goal.

The business world, in contrast, is full of randomness, which means non-linear goals. If you’re growing a business, you’ll face setbacks no matter how hard you work. Your sales numbers will go up and down. The impact of randomness is particularly acute in the money management business. You win some, you lose some. Markets are incredibly difficult to predict.

How can you calibrate non-linear goals to maximize engagement/flow when randomness is a factor?

High-performing investment organizations set performance goals for portfolio managers that are difficult yet attainable. The bar can’t be so high that it becomes impossible to clear. And it can’t be so low that there is no incentive for the portfolio manager to take calculated risks. A goal could be to rank in the top 25% of your peer group.

Portfolio managers are lucky (or cursed, depending on how you look at it) in that they get daily updates on their returns. Frequent performance updates are a key component of engagement or flow. But short-termism is a risk in our business, so performance evaluations cover different time horizons. Investors usually look at one-year, three-year, five-year, and ten-year returns.

Whether the goals are linear or non-linear, to stimulate engagement, they should be difficult yet attainable. Feedback should be transparent and frequent. It’s simple stuff. Consider the SMART acronym, a favorite of old-school corporate leadership trainers. SMART goals should be specific, measurable, achievable, realistic, and time-based. Each of these elements maximizes flow!

An organization may set a goal to increase by ten percent over the next year, increase factory output by five percent in four weeks, win “Best Place to Work” within the next three years, or increase consumer satisfaction scores by ten percent over the next six months…

It’s easier for some goals than others, but ultimately, you can dress most goals in a SMART attire.

“I need to go to the grocery store” becomes “I need to go to the grocery store to buy milk, eggs, cereal, and bread before 5 p.m. today.”

Or, “I should spend more time with my kids,” becomes “I will go to the mall with Olivia for a couple of hours every Saturday this month (even though I can’t stand to spend another hour at Claire’s), and I will play tennis with Charlie every Sunday morning (unless he starts to win, at which point I will throw my racket and take a few weeks to process my loss).”

Give it a try. SMART goals increase engagement. It’s cheesy corporate leadership training stuff, but… it works.

Takeaways to improve your mental game

In this book, we mine research in positive, sports, and personality psychology to improve leadership. Principle 3 proposes a new idea: Relying on the well-researched flow model to increase your and your organization’s engagement. Here are my key recommendations:

* Like legendary investor David Giroux, use the most powerful word in time management—“No”—and focus on high return on time spent (ROTS) activities.
* Your goals should be clear, and the rewards or feedback should be well-defined.
* Use metrics that will help focus attention on progress towards goals.
* Learn to differentiate between linear and non-linear goals.
* Remember that any task can be enjoyable if you pay enough attention.
* Seek (and provide if you’re a manager) consistent, frequent, and immediate feedback.
* Set individual or organizational goals that balance challenge and capability.

Also, immediately and forever ban status update meetings during which someone slowly reads a PowerPoint.

Principle 4: Make Year-End Reviews Useless—Prioritize Relationships

“Some cause happiness wherever they go; others whenever they go.”

—Oscar Wilde

In a famous Harvard study that spans 80 years, researchers tracked the health and happiness of a large sample of people throughout their lives. The study has involved four generations of researchers and is still running. The current director, Robert Waldinger, gave a 12-minute TED talk that has been viewed more than 39 million times.

When asked about their life goals, 80% of millennials say they want to become rich, and over 50% say they want to become famous.

Yet in the Harvard study, there was no evidence that those who climbed the status ladder lived happier lives than those who went down the ladder.

I’m not saying there’s a negative correlation. I don’t think that being rich and famous makes you less happy. And I believe ambition is a good thing if managed properly.

The Harvard study’s punchline is that the number one factor of long-term happiness in people’s lives is positive relationships. “People who are more socially connected to family, to friends, to community, are happier, physically healthier, and live longer than people who are less well connected,” Waldinger explains.

It’s the “R” in Seligman’s PERMA model. Relationships are so important to the psychology of leadership that we’ll spend more time on Principle 4, “Make Year-End Reviews Useless—Prioritize Relationships,” than on most other principles in this book.

In the corporate world, people love to hate meetings. As mentioned earlier, meetings often have a low return on time spent (ROTS). Everyone complains about them, but we keep having them.

Viewed through the “R” lens, we understand why meetings are so popular. We’re wired to crave social connections. Also, there’s a link between the importance of relationships and our earlier discussion on flow. Social interactions, Csikszentmihalyi explains, can bring a sense of flow—for example, when you work with a colleague to solve a challenging problem, when you debate a smart person on a controversial topic, or when you engage in witty banter with friends. These situations put your brain in the sweet spot for engagement. “Socializing is more positive than being alone, that’s why meetings are so popular,” concludes Csikszentmihalyi.

I can’t believe I just defended corporate meetings. Let’s just say I explained corporate meetings.

On the other side, the lack of positive relationships can be debilitating. “The experience of loneliness turns out to be toxic. People who are more isolated than they want to be from others find that they are less happy, their health declines earlier in midlife, their brain functioning declines sooner, and they live shorter lives than people who are not lonely,” reports Waldinger.

You must prioritize relationships at work and at home. Some of the most important goals you can set for yourself and your organization should focus on relationships. These goals aren’t perfectly measurable, and that’s okay. Relationships are the most important and biggest challenge to mastering the psychology of leadership. They’re too important for you to get stuck on traditional models for setting goals.

As a business leader, I care about relationships with employees, colleagues, and bosses. Relationships at work significantly influence how we think about job satisfaction. How do you feel in the morning before going to work? Are you dreading starting the day? If so, chances are that poor working relationships have something to do with it. In turn, chances are that these poor relationships are a side effect of poor culture.

At one point in my life, I made a career choice almost entirely based on corporate culture.

I had to decide between two compelling job offers. It came down to the wire. I sat down at the dinner table with Anne, and we made a list of pros and cons from a professional point of view and for the family. The pros and cons were different, but the two balances looked equal. It was so close that in the end, we decided based on a single factor: At one company, people emphasized positive working relationships.

Of course, every company will say that. Collaboration and mutual respect are the motherhood and apple pie of the corporate world.
