I dare you to find one senior executive who says collaboration is unimportant at their company. But through the interview process, I could tell these people were the real deal. Interviewers went out of their way to discuss mutual respect and collaboration as a competitive advantage. Everyone talked about the firm’s culture with passion. They asked about my leadership style. Between the lines, the message was: You may be an expert at what you do, but if your leadership style is lacking, you won’t be a good fit.

It turned out to be one of the best decisions of my life. The job is full of challenges, which keeps me in flow, miles away from the boredom zone. And, every day, I work with nice people.

One day, I was walking by the office of a new employee who had joined about a month earlier. I knocked gently on his open door and asked him how he was doing. “People are so nice here! You hear it in the interview process, but when you get here, you realize it’s real,” he said.

He gave me an example. Our Head of High Yield Fixed Income had taken time over the weekend to help him look for a house. “At my previous job, nobody at that level said a word to me in two years,” the new joiner said.

Anne had a similar realization after we were invited to a small company event at our CEO’s house. Several colleagues had heard that while I was excited about the new job, Anne’s transition was not smooth. She wasn’t thrilled about leaving Newport Beach, California, with its perfect climate, our great group of friends we call the “Nucleus,” and near-zero crime rate.

At the cocktail party, everyone we talked to asked her how she was doing and offered to help with the transition. You could tell people were genuinely interested in her well-being.

As we drove home that evening, she was quiet for a few minutes. Then she said: “I get it. I understand why we’re here.”

She was referring to the relationships (R) aspect. And the good news is that a decade later, despite the distance, the Newport Beach Nucleus survives. The group continues to spend vacation time together.

Positive relationships are important at work, but they’re as important, if not more, outside of work. Try to avoid having to make Sophie’s choice between one or the other. When we left Newport Beach, I started a group chat on WhatsApp with the Nucleus, and I bought a small condo there so we could go back whenever we had the opportunity.

Of course, there are downsides to corporate cultures that over-emphasize collaboration and being nice to each other. Dr. Zimet, whom we met in the introduction, explains, wearing his sports psychologist hat, that “elite athletes value two things in a coach: feeling that they care about you as a person, not just an athlete, AND applicable feedback that leads to improvement.”

People may hold back valid criticism or contrarian ideas at nice companies that value cohesion above all else. Meetings may get too big because no one wants anyone to feel left out. And the need for consensus may put sand in the gears of the decision-making process—something we’ve had to overcome as a company over the last few years.

To manage these issues as a leader, focus on mutual respect. An important skill to develop as an effective collaborator is the ability to have tough conversations (give constructive feedback, present contrarian ideas, etc.) and sometimes make unpopular decisions while never, ever compromising on mutual respect and empathy.

That’s hard sometimes. When you’re under pressure, you become more impulsive. Stress hijacks your amygdala, the part of the brain responsible for the fight-or-flight response.

Mental preferences

I once took the Herrmann Brain Dominance test and asked everyone on my leadership team to do the same. It’s a simple test: You answer questions on how you think and make decisions to understand your “mental preferences” across four categories: analytical, process-oriented, interpersonal, or imaginative. It turns out I’m quadruple dominant, which means I don’t have a bias towards any of these four categories. This result suggests that I can work with different types of personalities and make connections between groups. When drawn on a circle with four quadrants, my results look like a near-perfect square. Only 3% of the population is quadruple dominant.

But there was another set of results that raised alarm bells. As he walked me through his analysis, the specialist running the test explained that some questions were designed to uncover how you think under stress. He showed me these results on a dotted line below my perfect square. They most definitely did not look like a square. Under stress, my preferences looked like a triangle, with roughly equal preferences to three of the four categories, but one category—one corner of the square—compressed towards the center of the square, i.e., close to zero (see illustration). That category was interpersonal, which includes concepts such as anticipating how others will feel and understanding emotional elements.

![](image/SebPageHermanntest.png)

I agree with this result. Under pressure, I underestimate whether I might ruffle feathers. I focus on the problem, and my communication style deteriorates. I may commit a political infraction, fail to persuade someone, not listen enough, or give feedback in an unskilled way (by hitting the email nuclear button, also known as Reply All). It’s something I’m working on. In my defense, I make these relationship mistakes because I focus on solving the problem from the analytical perspective, through process improvement, or with a creative solution (or all of the above). And referring to my earlier comment on the amygdala, in a sense, humans are wired to get tunnel vision in stressful situations.

However, I know some executives who remain smooth as silk on the interpersonal side through any crisis. It’s an impressive skill. To sprint up the corporate ladder, you must foster positive relationships in good times and bad. Learn to keep cool by recognizing when you’re having a stress response. Self-awareness is the key to developing this skill. And here’s another trick: Use humor to diffuse tensions. Crack a joke, preferably at yourself, and watch the mood lighten instantly.

EQ vs. IQ

I’ve also noticed, by working with brilliant analytical people for over two decades, that when the intellect (IQ) is fully engaged (say, for example, debating a mathematical model), emotional skills (EQ) take a backseat. It’s like the brain has a finite amount of energy to allocate between IQ and EQ. I’ve heard the research seminars at the famed finance program at the University of Chicago described as an “intellectual blood sport.” Remember the last time someone disagreed with you at the intellectual level on a complex matter? Did you feel, in the moment, like they were insulting your intelligence? My guess is that you did. And with the benefit of hindsight, do you think they were insulting your intelligence? Probably not.

The ability to debate ideas at an intellectual level without bruising relationships is crucial for money managers. In his book Principles, Ray Dalio, a famous hedge fund manager, argues that you don’t need to sacrifice relationships to pursue “truth.” He makes the case that, “Embracing radical truth and radical transparency will bring more meaningful work and more meaningful relationships.”

To be clear, Dalio admits that working at his firm is not for everybody. I don’t endorse the more extreme components of his philosophy, especially after reading Rob Copeland’s unauthorized book on Dalio and his firm, titled The Fund. But the idea of setting your ego aside to look for the truth is a powerful one.

Think about it: As investors, our job is essentially to predict the future. What a humbling task! One of my colleagues, Jim Tzitzouris, is a quantitative expert with a PhD in mathematics. He also happens to have remarkable people leadership and mentoring skills. It’s somewhat rare for someone to have both extremely high IQ and EQ.

So, Jim is awesome. To encourage truth-seeking debates within his research team, he often says, “Your idea is not you.” He believes you should approach intellectual debates on technical or complex matters with open-minded curiosity and an unquenchable thirst for the truth… and you shouldn’t take anything personally. Of course, when they offer challenging feedback, good managers are mindful of the emotions of their team members.

My advice is that you pay special attention to debates when IQs are fully engaged. Start by putting all the facts on the table. Too often, heated debates occur because people aren’t starting from the same facts. And before you disagree, take a second to point out something you agree on. At the very least, people should agree on the facts. Say, “We all agree that…” followed by “but where I see things differently is…” Or even better, pay a genuine compliment to the person you’re about to disagree with.

I also recommend that you establish rules of the game within your team for such discussions. Explain that you’re about to debate ideas, nothing personal. Encourage people to be clear when they’re playing devil’s advocate, i.e. disagreeing for the sake of exploring different angles and strengthening the analysis.

With clear rules of the game and genuine respect amongst team members, IQ-led debates can turn into positive experiences. Breakthroughs happen when intellects wrestle and ideas collide. There’s no reason to sacrifice relationships to get there.

The feedback process

It may seem counterintuitive to set measurable goals for positive relationships, for an individual or an organization. How do you make a relationship-focused goal measurable and timely? It seems silly to set goals such as, “Improve your positive relationships by 10% before the end of the year.” At the organizational level, what do we mean by positive relationships anyway? Measuring sales growth is easy, but how do we measure culture?

Survey-based feedback can help. An individual’s peer review should cover their employees, peers, and bosses—the so-called 360-degree process. But please, keep it simple. Ask people to rate each other’s job performance and collaboration skills on a 1–5 scale. Then, ask two open-ended questions: What does this person do well, and what would help their development?

Also, remember that there are downsides to 360-degree feedback. Some people on my team dislike the process. They make good points. They argue that others’ perception of your performance is not your performance.

Peer feedback reports are full of biases, such as:

* Recency: people giving feedback based on recent interactions.
* Outliers: one response driving your average scores down.

Personally, I’m too sensitive to peer feedback. I’ve been working on adopting more of a take-some-and-leave-some approach. Like many people, I obsess way too much over the negative comments. When you receive feedback, you must decide what you want to internalize and what you should ignore.

In a controversial Harvard Business Review article titled “The Feedback Fallacy,” Marcus Buckingham and Ashley Goodall argue that, “More than 50% of your rating of someone reflects your characteristics, not hers.”

They explain that your peers can’t judge you on a loosely defined criterion such as collaboration, because their definition of this term changes over time and is colored by their own experience. They recommend focusing on positive reinforcement and giving feedback on the spot when you observe a great outcome.

Still, when used skillfully by the manager, peer feedback helps the employee with self-awareness. Positive relationships require self-awareness.

As a people manager, I collect feedback for my direct reports every six months. I’m lucky to manage an experienced, high-performing team. There are rarely any problematic individual performance issues. As a team, we’ve met with remarkable success over the last several years. You can’t stand still in the competitive world of money management. Your organization is either moving forward, or it’s quickly losing market share. Since I joined my current organization about nine years ago, we have continued to deliver strong investment performance, bring new products to markets, and grow our assets under management.

I attribute our success, in great part, to productive collaboration. Our working relationships aren’t perfect, but we keep track of issues and seek to address them as they arise. I’ve built a scatterplot in a spreadsheet that shows collaboration scores on the y-axis and job performance on the x-axis for all my direct reports. This chart shows a strong positive correlation between job performance and collaboration: Those who perform well in their role (according to survey responses) also tend to collaborate well. That’s the sign of a healthy culture.

A colleague once told me they had to sacrifice working relationships to get things done on a big project. They assumed job performance and collaboration are negatively correlated, or mutually exclusive. I disagree with such a cynical view. Anecdotal results from my study of over one hundred evaluations performed over nine years show a strong positive correlation.

Yet some people receive high collaboration scores, with below-average job performance scores. I never take someone’s peer feedback results at face value without my own assessment (which I like to do before I look at peer results), but if I think the feedback is valuable, I may ask the person to speak up more, even ruffle some feathers, to be more effective at their job. Effective collaboration is not about holding back on contrarian views. It’s about being able to disagree with mutual respect.

This approach can be hard to follow. Some people view any disagreement as the start of an argument or fight, while others love to discuss different points of view and see it as an opportunity to learn. The difference is how the individual interprets—or assesses—the meaning of someone presenting an opposing point of view. Collaborative (two heads are better than one)? Or combative (either I’m right or you’re right, but we can’t both be right)?

Some people get a high job performance score, but a low collaboration result. These players are scoring goals but not passing the ball (or the puck, for my fellow Canadians). If they did, the team as a whole would perform better. In these cases, I’ll tell the person they’re doing great work, but they could add more value to the organization if they built more positive working relationships.

In addition to quantitative data, qualitative responses in peer feedback surveys help with performance reviews. For my direct reports, I read every response to every question. Each person gets feedback from ten or more people. I read a couple hundred pages of feedback for every performance review cycle.

While it’s difficult for me to take some and leave some when I receive feedback, it’s easier when I compile peer responses to give feedback to someone else. Not all feedback is created equal. As a manager, you must add your judgment to the process. You may want to hold back on feedback that you think is unfair. Or you may emphasize some that you think will be helpful, uplifting, or well-deserved.

Also, you must provide qualitative feedback along the way. Day to day, you observe performance. You hear feedback for the people you manage from various sources, such as clients, peers, neutral observers from other divisions, senior management, etc. Don’t wait for the official year-end review. Jack Welch, former CEO of GE, explains in his book Winning that there should be no surprises in performance reviews. Give ongoing, honest feedback. If you do this well, the reviews should become almost redundant. I’m serious: Your goal as a manager is to make the year-end reviews useless. Not useless because there is no feedback, but because people have received the feedback they needed along the way.

“You have no right to be a leader if someone who works for you doesn’t know where they stand,” said Welch, whose sometimes harsh leadership philosophy has been questioned in recent years, but whose writings remain a rare source of candid, pragmatic insights on business.

(Later in this book, we’ll discuss separating signal from noise. The feedback must be constructive and relevant. Don’t micromanage.)

And for crying out loud, as I mentioned earlier, don’t give feedback via email unless it’s praise. I’m still working on this advice myself! Email has no tone. Even neutral comments are often heard as criticism.

How to improve feedback

Here’s an unappreciated leadership tip based on neuroscience: ask for feedback. In a seminar he gave at my company, Dr. David Rock, author and expert on the neuroscience of leadership, explained that “thousands of experiments to improve feedback have failed.” People hate feedback. They don’t like year-end reviews, and they don’t like ad-hoc feedback either. Giving feedback also increases stress. Yet Dr. Rock argues that we don’t get nearly enough feedback at work.

I’m a fan of Dr. Rock. He’s no self-help hack. He approaches leadership questions from a scientific point of view. He backs his recommendations with research, including his own. He has pioneered the field of neuroleadership. He studies the brain to improve leadership effectiveness. It’s hard science applied to the very human activity of inspiring others.

Dr. Rock says that the problem is not with feedback per se. The problem is with unsolicited feedback. Our brains are wired to get defensive when we hear, “Can I give you some feedback?” What a scary question! When I hear this, I raise my mental boxing gloves. It’s a reflex.

A simple solution is to foster a culture where people ask for feedback. As a leader, you can lead by example on this. “When someone asks for feedback, both sides feel less threatened,” explained Dr. Rock.

In a controlled study, receiving feedback after asking for it generated a 50% lower stress response in participants, as measured by heart rate and other factors, compared to receiving unsolicited feedback.

Also, in my process, I emphasize self-evaluations. Giving feedback is easier if it’s already in someone’s self-evaluation. Most professionals are remarkably self-aware. You just need to encourage them to be clinical about their performance.

Relationship goals

Qualitative feedback can inform relationship goals, but we must accept that these goals won’t be measurable. So, please, go ahead and violate the SMART goal edict. Positive relationships are too important to ignore. Everyone in your organization must strive to make how we work together as positive and productive as possible. Think of how sports teams succeed. Picture soccer players passing the ball to each other. It’s the sum-of-the-parts argument.

Write it down: “Improve your passing game.” Or “Communicate better.” Then, make it part of the feedback process. It’s so simple. And remember, while you’ve thrown out the M (Measurable), you can still make these relationship-focused goals specific, applicable, realistic, and timely. For example, instead of “Communicate better,” you could write, “To improve transparency, give weekly project status updates to your key internal stakeholders, including Legal and Human Resources.” Or, “Resolve tensions between your team and Technology. Present a strategic roadmap for how the teams will work together to finalize the minimally viable product by the end of the year.”

These examples are tactical, but relationship goals can be broader and longer-term. A CEO might set a goal to improve her firm’s culture of inclusivity. Or she may want to reinvent how cross-enterprise projects are managed across her organization. These changes don’t happen overnight, but they can be impactful.

Culture is everything

In business school, I had to sit through boring theoretical discussions on how CEOs drive culture from the top. Professors would show diagrams with abstract concepts such as culture, vision, mission, strategy… So tedious. I was more interested in finance and investments than in these management classes. I would memorize the mumbo-jumbo for the test and then forget all about it.

Until about 15 years later when I switched companies. Then, I realized that culture drives everything. Culture is the shared set of norms, beliefs, and expected behaviors in a group of people. It defines how people work together.

Moreover, it drives performance. In his bestseller The Culture Code: The Secrets of Highly Successful Groups, Daniel Coyne quotes a Harvard study that showed a strong culture improved net income by 756 percent over eleven years. To uncover what defines a strong culture, Coyne studies successful organizations such as Pixar, the San Antonio Spurs, and the US Navy’s SEAL Team Six. He wants to answer a basic question: What do successful organizations have in common? In other words, is there a secret sauce to culture? Early in the book, he reveals one of his most important findings:

When you ask people inside highly successful groups to describe their relationship with one another, they tend to choose the same word. This word is not ‘friends’ or ‘team’ or ‘tribe’ or any other equally plausible term. The word they use is ‘family’.

Coyne explains that safety, belonging, trust, and shared vulnerability are essential building blocks of this type of family culture. He concludes that, “…being smart is overrated, showing fallibility is crucial, and being nice is not nearly as important as you might think.”

To be clear, being nice is a positive thing to do! But when the team needs to have challenging discussions and give each other feedback, it’s not as important as belonging, trust, and shared vulnerability. These are the essential cultural norms that allow tough conversations to be productive. When people feel safe and respected, they can hear feedback because they feel less threatened. If you’re a senior executive, to foster mutual trust should be a long-term goal for your organization.

Communication is only the beginning. Many executives give excellent speeches and write wonderful emails about culture. Some large companies employ employee communications specialists to ghost-write a steady stream of platitudes. Yet company culture doesn’t improve. Why? Communication is essential, but culture is more about what you do than what you say. To foster a high-performing culture like the ones Daniel Coyne describes, you might need to make significant changes to your organization. If you say “we collaborate” in town halls and official communications, but you set up incentives that encourage people to compete against each other, your words on collaboration will sound hollow. You must get to the root of the problem.

Role clarity

As a business leader, I’ve struggled with an underappreciated detractor to positive collaboration. When I look back at negative working relationships that I’ve had or that people on my teams have had, I can trace almost all of them back to this single detractor. It leads to internal competition, bickering, and bad blood. It can turn otherwise nice people into enemies. And it seems to permeate almost every organization.

This detractor is a lack of role clarity. By role clarity, I mean the ability to answer one basic question: Who does what? At our firm, we debate this concept. When teams aren’t clear on who owns what, conflicts arise. Think about a negative relationship you’ve had with someone at work. Was there a role clarity issue? In sports, it’s simple: You don’t put two players in the same position on the soccer field. In corporate life, it’s complicated. It’s challenging to create role clarity around complex projects with lots of interdependencies.

As a leader, you must do many things beyond giving speeches to foster productive collaboration. For example, you may need to make organizational changes to improve role clarity. These changes may involve rethinking some people’s job definition or reporting lines, as well as hiring and firing decisions.

However, role clarity isn’t a magic bullet. Don’t go overboard. Some people ask for role clarity because they’re territorial. They want people out of their sandbox. You should know the difference. Don’t let role clarity become an excuse to avoid collaboration. Instead of increasing role clarity, sometimes you need to remind people that they have incentives to work together. Or, if they don’t have such incentives, you may need to create them, for example, with team-level goals and bonuses, or by making collaboration part of performance assessments. Sometimes, it’s simpler than that: You need to tell people to grow up. Many businesses, such as startups or companies that operate in rapidly changing industries, require flexible and interchangeable roles.

During my more than twenty years in the money management business, I’ve led or co-led the development of three Solutions businesses. “Solutions” has become a buzzword in our industry. I define it as a consultative approach to institutional sales. It’s a business model focused on customization to client needs. We meet with clients who control billions of dollars, and instead of pushing a specific investment product, we ask them: What do you need? We analyze their asset mix and liabilities (i.e., how the money is distributed or spent). Based on this analysis, we may recommend an off-the-shelf product or a customized portfolio.

When a client asks for an analysis or a proposal for a customized portfolio, the Solutions Strategist must mobilize the entire organization. Forget role clarity. Everyone must work towards one objective: Deliver the best the firm can offer this client. Whoever happens to have bandwidth (the ability to drop other projects to focus on this one temporarily) must step in. Organizations that are siloed and rigid about role clarity don’t do well as Solutions providers. But if you focus on the client, you can break silos without bruising relationships.

From personal to corporate goals

The research on PERMA and the Harvard study on happiness tell us that your most important life goal should be to nurture positive relationships. This goal should apply across the board: with family, friends, co-workers, and community. Again, I’m not telling you to give up the pursuit of wealth or fame.

Be ambitious! It will move you forward.

But when the rubber hits the road, and you face a time management challenge, remember that relationships supersede wealth and fame as drivers of your long-term happiness. Never neglect your family. When considering a job offer, ask about culture and listen carefully to how people respond. Everyone will tell you they have a collaborative company culture. But look at their body language. Ask for examples of corporate decisions that illustrate a positive culture. Do they give you a long, enthusiastic answer or switch topics quickly? Do they seem sincere? If not, run away. Fast.

For an organization, happiness is challenging to define. Perhaps it’s the sum of the happiness of stakeholders.

Every CEO must address the tensions between maximizing profitability for shareholders, treating employees right, and keeping clients happy. These are all important relationships. As an analogy, think of how teachers are often caught between students, parents, and administration.

To look at corporate goals through the lens of relationships, you can use survey data to measure employee and client satisfaction. You can set measurable goals, such as “improve employee engagement survey results by 5% over the next year” or “increase client satisfaction by 10% over the next year.” Relationships—between peers, employees and their bosses, salespeople and clients—will drive these results. “If I had to run a company on three measures, those measures would be customer satisfaction, employee satisfaction, and cash flow,” said Jack Welch.

Takeaways to improve your mental game

Remember the Harvard Study: Relationships, the R in the PERMA model, drives long-term well-being. And this is not just feel-good advice. Well-being boosts performance.

Money and fame don’t make people happier in the long run. Pay attention to the quality of relationships in your life and your organization. Company culture drives how people feel about their work day to day, how they work with each other, and how the organization performs.

As a leader, you must create an environment where people can disagree because there’s enough trust and mutual respect to maintain positive relationships. Be mindful of role clarity, but also its potentially undesirable side effects, such as increased bureaucracy. Pay attention to how people engage with each other on an intellectual level, especially on technical matters. That’s often when emotional intelligence (EQ) takes a back seat.

Feedback, including from surveys, helps measure progress on relationship goals. Try to score yourself and your team members on two dimensions: individual job performance and collaboration.

Also, set goals for consumer satisfaction. Inside your organization, de-emphasize unsolicited feedback by encouraging people to ask for feedback. Don’t be afraid to ask for it yourself. And try to correct biases before you present peer reviews to someone. Give on-the-spot positive reinforcement, especially when someone achieves a great outcome. Always be ready to say, “Yes! Do more of that!”

Now, can I give you some feedback? Stop using the phrase, “Can I give you some feedback?” You’re welcome.

Principle 5: Don’t Fake it—When You Talk About Meaning, Mean It!

“The meaning of life is to give life meaning.”

—Viktor E. Frankl, psychiatrist, and Holocaust survivor

Meaning, the M in PERMA, is about the things that feed your soul for the long run. People want to contribute to something larger than themselves. Just like positive relationships, it seems difficult to express meaning as a measurable goal.

Corporate leaders believe it’s important to provide broader meaning to goals to motivate employees. There is merit to this idea, but let’s reframe it as follows: You and your organization must pursue goals that have meaning. I’m not playing with words. There’s a difference between adding meaning to goals and pursuing goals that have meaning. With Principle 5, “Don’t Fake It—When You Talk About Meaning, Mean It!” we explore the powerful role that meaning plays in the psychology of leadership.

Making the world a better place

Below are examples from final presentations at a tech startup competition in which a few CEOs provided meaning to their goals rather than pursuing goals that had meaning:

“We’re making the world a better place through Paxos algorithms consensus protocols.”

—CEO of Systobase

“We’re making the world a better place through software-defined data centers for cloud computing.”

—CEO of BitFlenser

“We’re making the world a better place through scalable fault tolerant distributed databases with asset transactions.”

—CEO of Flingual

In every instance, the CEO seeks “to make the world a better place.” Really? Highly unlikely. The quotes are fictional, from an episode of the satirical HBO television series Silicon Valley. My point: Don’t pay lip service to meaning. Aside from being cliché, these quotes violate all the components of the SMART model.

As we discussed, you should deviate from the SMART model for goals that you can’t measure, for example, to foster positive relationships. Still, in these examples, “To make the world a better place” is a zero out of five on the SMART-meter. They may as well have said “end world hunger,” “create peace among all nations,” “solve climate change,” and “make all stakeholders richer than in their wildest dreams.” You can’t prove to others that you care by making broad claims lacking in credibility because you think they are necessary to win people over. The opposite will happen, as no one will take you seriously for one simple reason: They know you don’t mean it.

Simon says…

There is a good chance you are familiar with the work of motivational speaker and bestselling author Simon Sinek. His books, such as Start with Why and Leaders Eat Last, are regarded as staples by many business professionals. Sinek has also delivered the third most-watched TED Talk of all time—“How Great Leaders Inspire Action”—with over sixty million views.

Sinek is a high-energy, intelligent, and charismatic speaker. And he has a knack for hyperbole, as exemplified in his opening to his TED Talk:

All the great inspiring leaders and organizations in the world, whether it’s Apple or Martin Luther King or the Wright brothers, they all think, act, and communicate the exact same way. And it’s the complete opposite to everyone else.

Apple, Martin Luther King, and the Wright brothers think, act, and communicate the exact same way? And it’s the complete opposite to everyone else? Tell me more!

Sinek makes an essential point about meaning. He explains that, to achieve greatness, leaders and organizations must emphasize their why. “What’s your purpose? What’s your cause? What’s your belief? Why does your organization exist?” he rhetorically asks.

From there, he directs you to communicate the how and the what. Mediocre organizations focus on what they want to achieve (profits) and how they will get there, often losing sight of the all-important but fuzzier why.

I read Start With Why years ago. I thought I was buying a book on leadership—wrong! It’s about marketing. There is nothing inherently wrong with that; it’s a terrific marketing book. One of Sinek’s mantras is: “People don’t buy what you do; they buy why you do it.”

Sinek’s advertising background is revealed in this book and his TED Talk. He cites Apple as an example of a company that conveys values and meaning because their products reach consumers on a deep level. According to Sinek, people buy into the company’s ethos to challenge the status quo. Of course, it helps when the products are awesome. Yet Sinek also argues that Apple crushed several competitors who offered products with comparable—if not better—features. Sinek seems to attribute Apple’s explosive success entirely to how they marketed the company’s why. But it’s clear that technology and design matter, too.

Sinek acknowledges that you must believe in your why. Don’t be like the fictional Silicon Valley CEOs who want to make the world a better place. He also mentions the Wright brothers, builders of the first motor-operated airplane. The famed duo—who were under-resourced compared to their high-profile competitors—succeeded because of their sense of purpose and mission.

From Orville Wright: “The desire to fly is an idea handed down to us by our ancestors who, in their grueling travels across trackless lands in prehistoric times, looked enviously on the birds soaring freely through.”

From Wilbur Wright: “We could hardly wait to get up in the morning.”

We circle back to the idea of the mastery vs. the ego mindset. For the Wright brothers, I’m guessing that (and, of course, Sinek is stating that) money and fame were nowhere near as important as the mastery of flight.

The bottom line is that meaning fuels intrinsic goals. And, viewed through Sinek’s marketing lens, it sells products. But you can’t fake it.

Does finance have meaning?

In money management, we don’t talk often enough about our why. Finance makes the capitalistic world go round. We allocate resources in the form of the investments we choose to make or, just as importantly, choose not to make. We create liquidity in markets, manage risks, and price risks. But what does it all mean? Companies that sell consumer products deliver something tangible, such as an iPhone. In finance, it’s all just… spreadsheets, fancy graphs, and boring talking heads on TV (like me).

Recently, I had dinner with two friends, Christian, a CEO and engineer, and Charles, a successful mortgage bond trader on Wall Street (now retired), during which we became involved in a heated philosophical discussion about meaning and purpose in finance.

Christian runs a company that makes specialized nuts and bolts that hold reinforced concrete together. I don’t understand these products, but it sounds impossible to build any infrastructure—an office building, a road, or a bridge—without them. Looking at his company’s website, his marketing strategy isn’t focused on the why, but rather the technical details of the products. The following jargon sits at the top of his company’s website:

Our products are designed to exceed the tensile properties of the reinforcing steel used. That means the stress and strain capacity of the parent material is available and not limited by the bar end preparation. A706 is standard and recommended for ductility and capacity design control, but A615 grade 60 and 75 is fabricated upon request along with other bar grades.

Do you understand any of that? I don’t, but it doesn’t matter because you and I aren’t Christian’s target audience. His customers are professional buyers who presumably get excited by products that “exceed the tensile properties of the reinforcing steel being used.” Wow! And the strain capacity isn’t “limited by the bar end preparation?” Awesome!

I bring this up because Christian is a no-nonsense, highly intelligent, and successful businessman and engineer. He’s wealthy but wears old t-shirts, swears a lot, and doesn’t try to impress anybody. He’s never even heard of Simon Sinek. And not only does he think that what I do for a living lacks meaning, he thinks it’s “complete BS.” He has been known to call me a “bankster.”

I told Christian, “Look, every day we go to work, we try to make money for our clients. That’s all we think about—how can we give people a better financial future.

Financial markets exist to allocate resources between people who want to save/invest money and those who want to start or grow companies.

Christian still wasn’t buying it.

So, I decided to throw Charles under the bus.

Trading is different from asset management, and when people negatively think about Wall Street, they usually refer to trading and structuring (what we call the sell side) as opposed to money management (the buy side).

“You think I’m a bankster?” I snapped. “What about him? He traded mortgage bonds on Wall Street!”

Charles is a listener who talks less but says more. He combines a remarkable sense of humor with the wisdom of someone who has lived several lives. He paused, followed by his characteristic deep nasal breath, before explaining that, yes, speculation in securitized products had contributed to the financial crisis of 2008–2009. Then he added that, before mortgage-backed bonds were invented, it was almost impossible for anyone outside of big cities to finance their homes through a reasonably priced mortgage. According to Charles, trading in these bonds has created the liquidity necessary for anyone in Wyoming or Nebraska to get a mortgage at the same rate as someone in New York City.

For some jobs, meaning isn’t obvious. It may be misconstrued as the superficial pursuit of profit. There’s nothing wrong with this, but if it’s what serves as your driving force, I encourage you to dig deeper. Ask the big questions. You might realize your work has more purpose than you thought. If you are a leader, you must rally your team around meaning.

Despite his incredible success, Charles eventually grew disillusioned with Wall Street. We’ve never had an in-depth conversation about it, but I’m guessing he lost meaning in his work and went to find happiness elsewhere. Now, he has control over how he spends his time. He’s raising two daughters (his top priority), trades his personal portfolio, and is involved in private equity and venture capital. When I think about the concept of freedom, I imagine Charles’ incredible life in Newport Beach, California.

That night, my discussion with Christian and Charles eventually moved on to other topics. I didn’t give it much thought until about two weeks later when Christian sent me a brief email: “Tell me, where should I open an account to get my money professionally managed?”

What kind of bricklayer are you?

Jim Baker—a church organizational leadership and management coach— recounts the famous parable of the three bricklayers and the cathedral on the Sacred Structures website. The story, whose original author is unknown, is often used to illustrate the power of meaning.

One day in 1671, architect Christopher Wren observed three bricklayers on a scaffold—one crouched, one half-standing, and one standing tall—working hard and fast. To the first bricklayer, Wren asked, “What are you doing?” to which the bricklayer replied, “I’m a bricklayer. I’m working hard laying bricks to feed my family.” The second bricklayer responded, “I’m a builder. I’m building a wall.” The third bricklayer, the most productive of the three, when asked the question, “What are you doing?” replied with a gleam in his eye, “I’m a cathedral builder. I’m building a great cathedral to the Almighty.”

This story is about how you choose to define your work. The three bricklayers may have performed the same function, yet they assigned different meanings to it.

Religion fills the lives of many people with meaning and purpose. Like politics, it isn’t part of most work conversations. However, the cathedral story applies to meaning in a broader sense beyond religion.

In my organization, I might ask three analysts working on an Excel spreadsheet the following question: “What are you doing?” and receive three different answers. The first might say, “I’m debugging this spreadsheet.” The second might reply, “I’m building a model to predict the stock market.” Thinking about a metaphorical cathedral, the third might respond, “I’m finding ways to help our clients enjoy better retirements.”

In this alternate version of the cathedral story, the third analyst is undeniably happier, more motivated, and more productive than the other two—and not because she’s doing something different. It’s because she thinks about her work differently. She has given meaning to her actions.

Meaning is the great motivator in life. Your sense of purpose gets you up in the morning and keeps you going when you face obstacles. Meaning is what enabled Viktor Frankl—a famous psychiatrist and philosopher who survived the Holocaust—to endure the worst possible human suffering. In his influential book, Man’s Search for Meaning, Frankl wrote: “There is nothing in the world, I venture to say, that would so effectively help one to survive even the worst conditions as the knowledge that there is a meaning in one’s life.”

If you have the stomach to look into the abyss of ultimate human cruelty, read Frankl’s book, and you will gain a new perspective on the indisputable power of meaning.

Given its importance, meaning isn’t discussed nearly enough when organizations set goals. I’m not talking about spirituality or religion, but rather the more pedestrian topics of your career ambitions and the organization’s direction. Companies like Apple, Microsoft, Tesla, Patagonia, and Google may sometimes be perceived negatively (just like no one is perfect, no company is perfect), but their leaders set big goals. They create a sense of direction. They focus on a worthy mission. Perhaps companies like Enron, Lehman Brothers, Sears, Worldcom, BlackBerry, and Blockbuster failed because, at some point, they lost their way, their sense of meaning. Companies need a North Star.

What about you? How often do you take a step back and ask yourself these two questions: “What’s the meaning of what I do?” and “What is my purpose, my cathedral (or synagogue or mosque…)?”

I admit that I didn’t spend enough time on this topic in the past. I kept my head down and focused on laying the bricks. I would have benefited from a more profound sense of purpose and direction when I faced setbacks. As I’ve climbed the ranks into leadership positions of increasing responsibilities, I’ve realized that most people and organizations are starved for meaning, especially considering how much time we devote to work every day. Research in positive psychology—starting with the PERMA model—shows that it’s a key component of well-being.

Takeaways to improve your mental game

When you talk about meaning, be sincere! You may have to dig below the surface to find purpose. That’s okay. You must think and talk about meaning and define metrics for success that reflect the organization’s purpose. Of course, sales and profits are essential performance indicators, but so are customer satisfaction and employee retention. You can use simple surveys to take the pulse on your organization’s sense of meaning.

And please, make it real. Don’t say you want to “make the world a better place” if it’s not an attainable goal, and it rarely is. Don’t rely on marketing gimmicks, corporate shadow writers, or cliches. Take meaning seriously. Roll up your sleeves, convene your leadership team, and start the discussion. Don’t merely attach meaning to goals; pursue goals that have meaning.

In other words, don’t be a bankster.

Principle 6: Ignore the Gifted Kids—Don’t Focus on Winning

“Happiness is the absence of the striving for happiness.”

—Zhuangzi, Chinese philosopher

When my son Charlie was nine years old, he started making fast progress in Tae Kwon Do. He had been learning since he was four and was now practicing several hours a week at a good school in Maryland. The Master invited him to be part of a team representing the state in his age group at a competition in New York City.

That was a big mistake. When we arrived in New York, I realized this competition was for gifted kids. The best in the world. The Mozarts of Tae Kwon Do, if you will.

Charlie ranked last in every event: form, sparring, and board-breaking. Not just last, but he got “destroyed,” as he later described it. I had to take him out of the competition before the end of the day. The coaches didn’t know what to do with him: he was on the floor, face in his hands, crying.

I mentioned that mastery goals are essential for intrinsic motivation, more than ego-driven goals. Charlie’s goals at Tae Kwon Do were ego-driven: get the next belt and do well relative to others. His self-worth was shattered that day.

If only he had had a mastery orientation, which seems critical in martial arts, maybe he would still be involved in Tae Kwon Do. But that day was the beginning of the end of his involvement in it. He pushed hard to get his black belt (an ego-driven goal) and quit immediately after.

Now, thankfully, he seems to be enjoying weightlifting, with more of a mastery orientation. Videogames too, although I’m on the fence as to the pros and cons of computer gaming.

In his book David and Goliath, Malcolm Gladwell explains that high-achieving, but not genius-level, students who attend prestigious Ivy League universities give up on their ambitions more than other students. They face their version of Charlie’s Tae Kwon Do competition when they get on campus. They may have been at the top of their local high school academically, but when they compete with the Mozarts of their fields, their egos shatter. Gladwell suggests that if they attend less competitive universities, these students are more likely to achieve their goals, such as becoming doctors or engineers.

The last component of PERMA is accomplishment. That one seems simple. It’s where Seligman’s scientific theory of happiness meets our discussion on goals. When we set goals, we define accomplishment. It feels good to achieve your goals, to succeed, to win. And it should be the same for your organization: strong sales growth, above-expectations earnings, a new contract, a successful acquisition, and a high-flying stock price… all represent corporate accomplishments worthy of stakeholder happiness. All these concepts are connected: goals, accomplishment, and happiness.

But I’m hoping that by now, I’ve made a strong case that you must dig deeper than these surface connections. Principle 6, “Ignore the Gifted Kids—Don’t Focus on Winning,” focuses on well-being, the sustainable form of happiness that goes beyond the sugar-high of positive emotions. If you want to lead your organization in an inspiring, sustainable, and positive way, you can’t just focus on winning for the sake of winning. You must get off the goals-accomplishment treadmill and rethink your entire goal-setting process. You must redefine success. Seligman, for example, defines the A in PERMA not in terms of winning for its own sake, but as a function of mastery goals.

Mastery in business leadership is all about process. It means making how you do things better. Mastery means improving your technology, operations, culture, supply chain management, recruiting capabilities, strategic planning, ability to engage in mergers and acquisitions, and so much more. It’s about sharpening the organization’s saw so it can chop down more trees. Often, it feels counterintuitive to prioritize process improvement over short-term financial results. You will rarely see the impact of process improvement in your next quarterly earnings. You may even have to take a step back in profitability to invest in the organization’s future. Yet if you lead your organization with a mastery mindset, and everyone gets excited about doing things better, faster, and cheaper, then over time, you’ll deliver extraordinary results.

In the introduction to his book Flourish, Seligman compares two types of bridge players. Here’s how he describes the players that focus on mastery goals: “Some expert bridge players play to improve, to learn, to solve problems, and to be in flow. When they win, it’s great. … But when they lose—as long as they played well—it’s almost as great.”

Seligman then describes a second type of player. You probably know people who fit this category:

Other experts play only to win. For them, if they lose, it’s devastating no matter how well they played; if they win, however, it’s great, even if they ‘win ugly.’ Some will even cheat to win.

In the golf example I mentioned earlier, we compared two players. One makes it their goal to improve their swing and derives personal satisfaction from it. This player plays for the sake of playing. The other player wants to impress others with a low handicap. She plays for the sake of winning. These two players may be equally successful. They may have the same handicap. But I bet the mastery-focused player enjoys the game a lot more. They’re better equipped to cope with mistakes.

Think of Sinek’s example of how the Wright Brothers’ desire to master flight superseded everything else, such as money and fame. Or think of my example of how we approach money management in my organization: We strive to improve our process.

To redefine success as mastery is a powerful concept.

I’m a runner. Most runners enjoy exerting mastery over our mind and body. It’s an intrinsic motivation. No one forces us to get up early and go out in the dark and cold to do something hard, lonely, and repetitive. We’re not trying to impress anybody at the beach with our skinny arms and low heart rate. Most of us like to benchmark our performance against our prior runs. Runners like to test how far we can push ourselves. That’s mastery.

The other side

Or is it? I can’t emphasize this caveat enough: There is nothing wrong with wanting to win! Let’s be realistic: Competition is a fantastic motivator. We’ll learn this in Principle 8, which covers the power of social comparison. Runners have some ego-driven motivation, like everyone else. My fastest runs have always been during races. It’s exciting to push against other runners and get our time published on the website… for everyone to see.

How you define accomplishment can’t be a black-and-white matter. To define the “achieving life,” Seligman differentiates between winning at all costs and a broader notion of accomplishment. He asks us to focus on accomplishment, yet he admits that “such a life is almost never seen in its pure state. People who lead the achieving life often pursue pleasure avidly, and they feel positive emotion (however evanescent) when they win.”[1](ThePsychologyofLeadership-ebook-050325-12.xhtml#footnote-000)

Your psyche combines a drive towards mastery and a desire to win. No one is asking you to give up your desire to win. Being uninterested in winning doesn’t make sense to me, no matter what academic psychologists claim. However, you must dig deeper than the surface notion that reaching your ego-driven goals means happiness. To get out of this mental fog, consider your life goals and how you lead your organization. Ask yourself whether your ego is too involved. If so, redirect your goals toward mastery. Redefine accomplishment. Here’s paradoxical advice: Don’t focus so much on winning, such that in the long run, you’ll win.

Correlations across the components of PERMA

Seligman presents each component in the PERMA model as independent. He must demonstrate that he’s not double-counting to give the model academic credibility. Each dimension must add something to happiness, independent of the others. He claims that “Accomplishment is often pursued for its own sake, even when it brings no positive emotion (P), no meaning (M), and nothing in the way of positive relationship (R).”

I don’t know why he leaves engagement (E) out in this quote. In any case, I disagree. When he insists on this notion of independence, I suspect he caters to peers in the ivory tower and perhaps confuses his non-expert readers. Basic intuition says we experience the components of PERMA in some combination and that one must contribute to the other. There are lots of connections. Positive relationships bring meaning to your life. Meaning gives you an intrinsic drive towards accomplishment and the resilience to ignore short-term wins and losses. And when you pursue goals with meaning and focus on accomplishment, you’re more likely to experience engagement. And ultimately, I’m convinced that a focus on ERMA, over time, brings a fair amount of positive emotions!

Let us agree that a) I’m a fan of Seligman’s research (clearly); and b) the components of PERMA reinforce each other.

An article published in 2018 in the Journal of Positive Psychology reports the following correlations across the components of PERMA based on a survey of 517 adults:

PERMA component correlations

![](image/Permacorrelations.jpg)

Fallon R. Goodman, David J. Disabato, Todd B. Kashdan & Scott Barry Kauffman (2018) “Measuring well-being: A comparison of subjective well-being and PERMA,” The Journal of Positive Psychology, 13:4, 321-332, DOI: 10.1080/17439760.2017.1388434

Let’s look at the extremes. The highest correlation (79%), highlighted on the bottom right, is between meaning (4) and accomplishment (5). Most people who score high on one will also score high on the other. At 37%, the lowest correlation is between engagement (2) and relationships (3). This result makes sense: Flow tends to be a solitary experience. However, it’s still a positive correlation. Csikszentmihalyi explains that social interactions can bring a state of flow—when your intellect is engaged in stimulating conversation, for example in a battle of the wits.

Seligman has published a rebuttal of this article. He clarifies that correlation does not mean redundancy. It’s “a complicated issue, one that transcends psychometrics [statistics],” he writes.

He uses an analogy: A good baseball pitcher will likely have both a good fastball and curveball. So, fastball and curveball skills are correlated, but they’re still two distinct elements of pitching. The pitcher must practice both.

Takeaways to improve your mental game

Incorporating positive psychology into goal setting could redefine the science and practice of leadership.

The elements of positive psychology—engagement, relationships, meaning, and accomplishment—are tightly connected. They de-emphasize scoreboard goals in favor of what motivates people in the long run. It may seem counterintuitive—especially for a book on mental toughness—to tell leaders not to focus so much on winning. Still, in this Principle, I make a case for why you must redefine success as mastery for mastery’s sake, rather than winning for winning’s sake.

I’m not asking you to give up the fire in your belly. However, I invite you to expand your definition of accomplishment. Mastery and meaning are long-term, intrinsic, well-being concepts that differ from the relentless pursuit of short-term positive emotions.

My point, I suppose, is that winning at all costs is for losers.

---

1. [1](ThePsychologyofLeadership-ebook-050325-12.xhtml#footnote-000-backlink) This quote has been shortened for clarity. The original, from the book Flourish, is as follows: “I fully recognize that such a life is almost never seen in its pure state (nor are any of the other lives). People who lead the achieving life are often absorbed in what they do, they often pursue pleasure avidly and they feel positive emotions (however evanescent) when they win, and they may win in the service of something larger.”

Principle 7: Don’t Admire Billionaires—Choose Your Role Models Carefully

“What did that guy do? He just told other people what to invent.”

—Bill Burr, comedian, on his skepticism of Steve Jobs as a business hero

Research in psychology reveals that if you identify with a celebrity, you will likely change your attitudes and behaviors to be more like them. This does not only apply to teenagers who want to dress like Taylor Swift. It applies to everybody, including you and me. Related research on role models shows that your sense of ethics and aspirations are influenced by who you admire—perhaps more than you realize.

Having good role models will give you a North Star. It will make you a better person, which will make you a better leader… and, ultimately, a better role model to others. It’s the circle of leadership life.

But remember that no one is perfect.

In the next few chapters, I explain my ambivalent views on billionaires. You can learn important leadership lessons from those who have accomplished extraordinary things and become celebrities in the business world. However, these lessons come with warning labels. I advise that you take a balanced view of the role of money as a source of motivation.

For example, Steve Jobs, Elon Musk, and Jeff Bezos seem to have mastered the psychology of leadership, don’t they? The truth is this: What worked for them may be innate, idiosyncratic, and come with unattractive traits that the rest of us can’t overcome. Most incredibly successful people are bad at relationships. Jobs treated some of his colleagues poorly. At Twitter, aka X, Musk is taking a brutal and impulsive approach to employee layoffs and ignoring advertisers’ sensitivities. Jeff Bezos has been known to harshly insult the people who work for him.

Did you know that there have been 13 divorces among the ten richest men in the world and that seven of the top ten have been divorced at least once?

My message? It’s not necessary to be a jerk or sacrifice family life in the name of accomplishment. And money is great, but it’s not everything. The psychology of extreme wealth is fascinating. Most billionaires are, surprisingly, not motivated by money as much as big goals and social comparison. With Principle 7, “Don’t Admire Billionaires—Choose Your Role Models Carefully,” I pose the following questions: Who do you know that’s successful but isn’t focused on external rewards? Who hunkers down and does the hard work every day?

Some might be billionaires or powerful CEOs, but it’s equally as likely you’ll find teachers, doctors, nurses, and budding entrepreneurs as strong role models. It’s not about the job definition. It’s about how they approach their work that makes them role models. While you can learn from billionaires—especially when it comes to meaning and setting big goals—you can broaden your list of role models. Billionaires have blind spots.

Steve Jobs

Steve Jobs had a strong sense of meaning. While recruiting John Sculley, President of Pepsi, he famously asked: “Do you want to sell sugar water for the rest of your life or come with me and change the world?”

And when you think about it, unlike the caricature CEOs from the show Silicon Valley I mentioned earlier, Jobs kind of did change the world. Of course, it depends on your definition of changing the world, but I hope you’ll agree that he had a meaningful impact.

We must also give credit to those who did the work. Comedian Bill Burr refers to Jobs as “nerd Jesus.” He makes fun of nerds who say that Jobs changed the world: “What did that guy do? He just told other people what to invent … walking around, eating some pretentious fruit, like a pear! These poor, nameless, faceless scientists had to go in the backroom and figure it out!”

Burr has a point. Sometimes, we build cults around leaders and fail to give enough credit to the doers. Apple has done well in its post-Jobs era. Until he became the company’s new CEO, Tim Cook was one of Apple’s doers, working in Jobs’ shadow. I don’t know if he walks around eating pretentious fruits, but as a leader, Cook has contributed to increasing Apple’s market value from $348 billion to $3 trillion. (Regarding the pear comment: Jobs was a vegetarian.)

Despite Burr’s critique, a leader’s job can be incredibly hard. As a leader, you know that creating a vision of the future, inspiring people, and marshaling resources to turn your vision into reality aren’t easy things to do.

Jobs’ accomplishment orientation kept him going through the ups and downs of his career. For example, after he was fired from Apple in 1985 (by John Sculley, of the sugar water quote!), his new company, NeXT Computer, failed to gain traction in the workstation market. But he kept going with meaningful, long-term goals, turning Pixar into a powerhouse and eventually returning to Apple and transforming the company. Think of the accomplishment orientation as the ability to ignore short-term losses and to get back up every time you fall.

Jobs did not want to exert editorial control over Walter Isaacson’s biography of him. That was a brave decision. We learn that while Jobs scored high on meaning and accomplishment, he scored low on relationships. Isaacson says he interviewed “scores of people he had fired, abused, abandoned, or otherwise infuriated.” One of the people Jobs abandoned was his firstborn daughter.

Jobs may have suffered from a PERMA imbalance. Perhaps his relentless focus on accomplishment crowded out his ability to develop positive relationships. He accomplished great things, and I’m sure he had some positive relationships in his life, but was Jobs happy? I don’t know. The Harvard study on happiness we discussed earlier suggests that he probably wasn’t. You’ll recall that this study revealed that positive relationships are, by far, the most critical predictor of well-being. I also believe Jobs had an ego. He chose the smug cover photo for his biography. Have a look. That picture says a thousand words about the man’s ego.

Models like PERMA help us understand the psychology of leadership at a deeper level, but as human beings, we’re much more complex than any model can describe. Again, I don’t think the factors are independent. Meaning, accomplishment, and engagement reinforce each other. Your drive towards accomplishment comes from a profound sense of meaning. You then become engaged in your work and frequently experience flow. These gears of happiness all work together like inside a finely tuned machine. But it’s possible, if you go to extremes with your big, meaningful goals—like Jobs did—that your relationships will suffer. Earlier, I showed my Hermann Brain Dominance test results. I tend to sacrifice relationships when under pressure. When I’m in flow, if I’m writing on a Saturday morning, for example, I can lose sight of the importance of spending weekend time with the family, and I get irritable if someone interrupts me.

Elon Musk

There’s a remarkable parallel between Jobs’ and Elon Musk’s deep accomplishment orientation and their blind spot on the relationship side.

Musk wants to rid the world of its dependence on fossil fuels and make humans an interplanetary species by establishing a colony on Mars. Also, he’s toying with concepts such as turning humans into cyborgs by implanting computers in their brains (I’m not kidding) and building so-called hyperloop trains that operate in a vacuum at 670 miles/hour… which would be faster than commercial aircraft. And let’s not forget self-driving cars. As a Tesla owner, I can attest that we’re almost there. My car now does at least eighty percent of the driving without my involvement. Talk about big goals!

Like Jobs, positive relationships seem to be less important to Musk than accomplishment. In fact, for Musk, it appears there’s nothing more important than big goals for the sake of big goals. He famously sometimes sleeps under his desk or on his factory floor. Also, like Jobs, he has zero tolerance for mediocrity. He apparently berates and fires employees for small mistakes or lack of problem-solving abilities. One of my friends who worked directly under Musk told me: “What you read in the media about his insane work ethic, and frequent disregard for people’s feelings? It’s all true.”

Below is a quote from an email Musk apparently sent an anonymous Tesla employee who missed a company event. It exemplifies the tension in Musk’s psyche between accomplishment and positive relationships. Also, notice the heavy dose of meaning involved. Accomplishment and meaning seem to be the most correlated components of PERMA. These two factors of happiness are tied at the hip. But for Jobs, Musk, and many others, unfortunately, they’re negatively correlated with relationships.

Musk emailed this employee: “That is no excuse. I am extremely disappointed. You need to figure out where your priorities are. We’re changing the world and changing history, and you either commit or you don’t.”

Can you guess what the excuse was? The employee had missed a company meeting to attend the birth of his child. Musk has since disputed this anecdote. It may be an exaggeration. He wrote in a tweet that he would never do that. One of his followers responded: “I hear talk that you may potentially be… a supervillain.”

Billionaire car crash stories

It’s not okay to be a jerk in the name of accomplishment. Billionaires succeed despite relationship blindness, not because of it.

Let me mention two car crash stories of billionaires who have destroyed their careers in spectacular ways due to personality and relationship issues, despite their accomplishment focus.

The first is from Reeves Wiedeman’s book Billion Dollar Loser, about Adam Neumann, founder of office-sharing company WeWork. I don’t know whether Neumann ever had a genuine accomplishment focus. Wiedeman quotes him as, of course, wanting to “change the world.” He certainly sold a grand vision of changing how people live and work in shared spaces. WeWork’s valuation, at one point, topped $47 billion. Neumann said he wanted to be the world’s first trillionaire and “president of the world.” That’s not “accomplishment for the sake of accomplishment,” as Seligman would define it. That’s just being an egomaniac. Neumann certainly suffered from a relationship infirmity. He treated employees like dirt. From the New York Post:

WeWork senior executives requesting an in-person meeting might be asked to fly with Adam from New York to San Francisco …. But then he was just as likely to make them wait for hours, leave before they arrived at the airport or simply not have time to talk to them on the six-and-a-half-hour flight.

Just think about that for a second: you’re a senior, hard-working executive. You need to talk to your boss. He tells you to fly with him from New York to San Francisco, then find your way back home. He doesn’t care that flying across the country and back is a complete waste of your time. What’s worse, when you get to the airport in New York, after battling traffic, he’s already left. Or, if you do get on the plane, he ignores you for the entire 6.5-hour flight. What a jerk! My blood boils just thinking about leaders who treat employees like that. Neumann was ousted from the company in 2019. At some point, investors realized he was over-selling and mismanaging growth. As of 2023, WeWork was valued at $270 million, a 99% drop from its peak.

My second billionaire car crash story is nuanced. You can’t say the car was totaled. It involves a successful company: Uber. I recommend Mike Isaac’s book Super Pumped: The Battle for Uber, which details co-founder Travis Kalanick’s tenure and eventual ouster as CEO of the company. As of 2023, Uber had 30,000 employees, 5 million drivers, and a market capitalization of $135 billion. I use Uber, and I was an early adopter. It’s one hundred times better than taxis.

Kalanick had a vision and plenty of intrinsic drive toward accomplishment, meaning, and purpose. As Isaac describes it, his strategy was straightforward: “Conquer.” Uber’s view was that taxis are “corrupt and protectionist. You can’t take on regulators and disrupt an entrenched business model like taxi services if you only care about money or external validation. You need intrinsic drive. According to Isaac, Kalanick would describe the battle with taxi services as a political campaign: “The candidate is Uber and the opponent is an a\*\*hole named Taxi. Nobody likes him, he’s not a nice character, but he’s so woven into the political machinery and fabric that a lot of people owe him favors.”

That may be a mild metaphor for how Kalanick felt about his mission. Kalanick “saw himself locked in an existential battle with entrenched taxi operators….” The better metaphor wasn’t a political campaign. It was war.

So, why was Kalanick fired? Kalanick had built a sexist, bro culture inside Uber. From the Chicago Tribune: “After multiple investigations, Uber fired 20 employees accused of sexual harassment, bullying and retaliation against those who complained.”

Issues with the company’s culture also extended to a disregard for regulations, (allegations of) stealing technology, and an overall pirate mentality that went as far as building a fake version of the app to mislead authorities. I’ve said that culture is everything and starts at the top of the organization. Uber’s investors concluded that Kalanick was the problem and he wouldn’t change. Firing a company’s founder, who’s also a large shareholder and has appointed the board, is not easy. Isaac gives a captivating, play-by-play account of Kalanick’s ousting. (And don’t cry for Kalanick. As of 2024, he’s worth over $3.6 billion.)

I’ve read countless books about billionaires.[2](ThePsychologyofLeadership-ebook-050325-13.xhtml#footnote-001) I suppose I’m somewhat of a billionaire expert. Not all of them seem to have a relationship blind spot, at least not those who had creative control over what was written about them… if you catch my drift. But they all seem extraordinarily intense. There is no such thing as a mellow self-made billionaire. In a counterintuitive way, billionaires don’t seem primarily motivated by money. They have an accomplishment focus. Whether you call it a sense of purpose, mission, meaning, intrinsic motivation, or simply having big goals, they’re willing to take short-term pain in exchange for long-term gain. And they’re willing to take significant risks, including the risk of losing it all.

What does this teach us about leadership in the world of mere mortals?

Your effectiveness as a leader depends in part on your ability to understand people’s motivations and mindsets.

In his bestseller The Psychology of Money, Morgan Housel explains that “we think about and are taught about money in ways that are too much like physics (with rules and laws) and not enough like psychology (with emotions and nuance).” Studying billionaires puts these nuances under a magnifying glass.

Some people buy expensive things because they think it will earn them respect and admiration. Others value autonomy—being able to control their time—more than additional compensation. Yet others will never be happy because they always want more money, no matter how much they have. These different money personalities will respond to incentives differently.

Remember that your motivational tools as a leader include so much more than compensation. Once again, positive psychology enters the picture: It helps to have candid conversations about people’s deeper, longer-term motivations and to discuss the meaning of their work.

My favorite story in The Psychology of Money is one that John Bogle, the Vanguard founder, told Housel:

At a party given by a billionaire on Shelter Island, Kurt Vonnegut informs his pal, Joseph Heller, that their host, a hedge fund manager, had made more money in a single day than Heller had earned from his wildly popular novel Catch-22 over its whole history. Heller responds, “Yes, but I have something he will never have… enough.”

If you don’t practice gratitude, you’ll become dissatisfied with external rewards almost as soon as you receive them. A big bonus, a promotion, a new car, more social media followers—the satisfaction from these types of rewards typically doesn’t last long. In psychology, this is called “hedonic adaptation.”

To nudge your team members toward gratitude, ask them what they like most about their jobs. Make it a standard question in self-evaluation forms. Pay attention to what they write, and don’t hesitate to remind them of these positives when they feel down or frustrated. As Cheryl Crow sang, “It’s not having what you want, it’s wanting what you’ve got.” (Marcus Aurelius would have been a fan.)

Intrinsic rewards and self-motivation

In his book Drive, bestselling author Daniel H. Pink studies some of the same driving forces of intrinsic motivation that we’ve discussed already, such as mastery and purpose (meaning). One of his best examples of the power of intrinsic motivation is Wikipedia. Volunteers have contributed 57 million articles to the site. There’s zero compensation involved—everyone contributes for free. The goal of contributing to something greater than oneself seems to be at play.

Like Morgan Housel, Pink emphasizes the concept of autonomy. Never underestimate people’s need for autonomy. In a study of 320 small businesses, companies that offered autonomy grew four times faster than so-called control-oriented firms.

Pink goes one step further than we have so far. He argues that extrinsic rewards, like cash bonuses, can be damaging to motivation: “Adding certain kinds of extrinsic rewards on top of inherently interesting tasks can often dampen motivation and diminish performance,” he explains.

He makes a compelling case, and if he’s right, the entire corporate world should rethink how we set goals, measure performance, and compensate people. He explains that with the carrot-and-stick method (if-then rewards), we’re asking people to give up their autonomy.

He emphasizes issues with hedonic adaptation when he writes, “cash rewards and shiny trophies can provide a delicious jolt of pleasure at first, but the feeling soon dissipates—and to keep it alive, the recipient requires ever larger and more frequent doses.”

Like a drug addiction. One of his most potent arguments against extrinsic rewards is the following (parentheses are mine): “By offering a reward, a principal (boss) signals to the agent (employee) that the task is undesirable.”

Pink presents influential theories with extensive empirical support in psychology research. You can apply this to parenting, too.

But as any parent or corporate leader knows, plenty of undesirable tasks must be done when the rubber hits the road. I can’t get my son to be intrinsically motivated to do the dishes. If there’s a way, I haven’t found it.

In my career, I’ve seen freshly minted MBAs show up at work with lots of intrinsic motivation to run the company. That’s what they’re trained for: to be leaders, decision-makers, dealmakers. They’re ready to revamp our strategic plans on day one. They start telling people what to do, maybe even walking around eating a pretentious fruit like a pear. Then, they go through a tough period. They realize their job is also going to involve execution and lots of less glamorous tasks.

Their motivation to apply textbook leadership fresh out of business school also takes a hit when they realize that many companies sometimes value technical domain expertise more than platitudes about strategy. Several years ago, I had dinner with a pretentious quantitative analyst (PhD), and a pretentious MBA. It didn’t take long before they started comparing their educational background:

“What’s your background?” asked the PhD to the MBA.

“I have an MBA from Harvard.”

To which the PhD responded:

“Ha, so you went to PowerPoint school!”

I almost spat out my wine.

The unfortunate reality of the corporate world—at least for large companies—is that people can’t always apply their intrinsic motivation for meaningful work in entry-level jobs. You must be pragmatic when setting accomplishment goals for yourself and your organization. Intrinsic motivation is great, and self-made billionaires have lots of it, but there are caveats. Despite our idealized example of the bricklayer building a cathedral, some jobs are more difficult to tie to accomplishment and meaning goals than others. For these jobs, the famous book Fish! A Proven Way to Boost Morale and Improve Results argues that focusing on attitude, making the customer’s day, and play may be more appropriate than looking for an elusive notion of intrinsic motivation.

People also like being part of a team. Take football, for example. The guys on the line never score touchdowns, but nobody would be able to score without them.

Whether intrinsic motivation is easy or difficult to find, the question remains: What do you truly care about? So much of the psychology of leadership hinges on that question.

Takeaways to improve your mental game

It’s absolutely okay to pursue massive financial success. But there has to be more to it.

Ask yourself: If I had all the money in the world, how would I want to spend my time? This question is simple and profound.

You might find that you don’t need to be a billionaire to achieve your goals in life.

To become a better leader, choose your role models carefully. You may be more influenced by whom you admire than you think. Remember that no one is perfect. Intrinsic motivation and big goals are great, but only if they don’t come at the expense of your mental and physical health, your sense of balance, strong family bonds, and positive and productive working relationships.

Think about people in your life. Who’s a good role model for the accomplishment orientation? Who’s not focused on external rewards like fame and money? Who keeps their ego in check and does good work every day? It’s not about the job definition. It’s about how they approach their work. Accomplishment-oriented people have a long-term, often lifelong, mission and a sense of purpose.

The same ideas apply to motivating others. The psychology of money and the psychology of leadership are intertwined. You should pay employees at competitive levels and differentiate based on performance.

Beyond these basics, I invite you to expand your definition of compensation. Many people are motivated by additional factors such as autonomy, power, prestige, the social aspects of work, or altruism.
