To the extent that you can and that doing so will further the goals of your organization, help employees achieve these goals.

Also, why not make work as fun as possible and foster esprit de corps, especially for entry-level jobs?

Remember that hedonic adaption is the human tendency to return to the same level of happiness after reaching goals. Make gratitude part of your team’s culture. Model it yourself and encourage others to reflect on it.

For example, you should be grateful that applying the principles you learned in PowerPoint school made you rich.

---

1. [2](ThePsychologyofLeadership-ebook-050325-13.xhtml#footnote-001-backlink) Other biographies and related business books worth reading include Alice Schroeder’s The Snowball: Warren Buffet and the business of life; Nike founder Phil Knight’s outstanding autobiography Shoe Dog; Ron Chernow’s biography of Rockefeller, Titan; Netflix co-founder Marc Randolph’s That Will Never Work; Ex-Disney CEO Bob Iger’s The Ride of a Lifetime; Walmart Founder Sam Walton’s Made in America; Greg Zuckerman’s biography of hedge fund wizard Jim Simons The Man Who Solved the Market; Ray Dalio’s Principles; Sheelah Kolhatkar’s account of hedge fund billionaire Steven Cohen’s shenanigans Black Edge; Eric Schmidt’s biography of Bill Campbell Trillion Dollar Coach; Tom Wright and Bradley Hope’s book about Jho Low, a man who stole 5 billion dollars (!) Billion Dollar Whale; John Doerr’s Measure What Matters; Rob Copeland’s The Fund, about Ray Dalio; Brad Stone’s The Everything Store: Jeff Bezos and the Age of Amazon; and Walter Isaacson’s biography of Elon Musk.

Principle 8: Build a Bigger Rocket—Unleash the Power of Social Comparison

“Jeff who?”

—Elon Musk, interrupting his interviewer when asked about his competition with Jeff Bezos on space exploration

Our analysis of billionaires’ motivations is missing an obvious part. We saw that they score high on meaning and accomplishment, but low on relationships. Surprisingly, money is rarely their primary motivation. Like everyone else, they experience hedonic adaptation. They put their pants on one leg at a time, and they enjoy the first cookie a lot more than the 11th.

So, what did we miss? It’s perhaps the most important caveat to the naïve interpretation of their accomplishment orientation.

Billionaires are incredibly competitive. Social comparison is often a motivator for the uber-wealthy. There’s always somebody to compete against. Somebody who makes even more money than you.

Principle 8, “Build a Bigger Rocket—Unleash the Power of Social Comparison,” invites you to manage the good and the bad of social comparison. As we discussed with measurable goals, you can use competition as a tool to increase productivity among people and teams you are leading if you also manage its potential downsides.

Hold on, you might be thinking: Didn’t Principle 6 tell us to ignore the gifted kids and not focus on winning? Haven’t we learned from sports psychology that intrinsic motivation and mastery are the keys to long-term success?

Once again, we must accept the nuance and trade-offs in the psychology of leadership.

You won’t find much about social comparison in the biographies of billionaires. Most of them keep this motivation private, I suppose.

Yet I’m convinced billionaires like to keep score. I’m sure many of them care about their rankings on the Forbes list of wealthiest people in the world, for example. You may not need to make more money if you’re already rich, but you can still feel the urge to make more money than your neighbor. In other words, the absolute utility of wealth—how much happiness you derive from making more money—may decrease the wealthier you get, but its relative utility—how you feel about making more money than others—may not. Or at least we can theorize that these two utility curves are different.

An example of the value of social comparison in finance

Consider the ultracompetitive world of money management: We keep track of how much money we make for our clients and whether we’re delivering better returns than our competitors. Beating the competition is a matter of survival. Clients can quickly move their money to our competitors if we don’t win consistently enough.

Institutional owners of capital also care about their peers. My early career mentor, Mark Kritzman, CEO of Windham Capital Management, once advised the Harvard University endowment on asset allocation. He suggested a model that would allocate assets to get the best expected risk-reward ratio. Then he realized that the investment team at Harvard didn’t just want to earn good returns to fund the university’s various spending projects. They also wanted to beat Yale’s endowment.

That’s not irrational. Harvard competes with Yale to attract students. A bigger endowment helps fund construction projects for fancy new facilities, support student aid, and attract the best professors. Mark incorporated a new measure of risk into the model, defined as the volatility of returns relative to peers.

“Investors want to make money for clients and outperform their peers. Being ‘right and alone’ feels good. And they’re somewhat comfortable being wrong… if everybody else is wrong. But what investors hate more than anything is being ‘wrong and alone,’” he explained.

Based on a model built by his then-colleague George Chow, he redefined the utility function to incorporate this peer group comparison goal. I used this multi-goal model at State Street for years to advise institutional investors on their asset mix decisions.

Big rockets, Stoicism, and the upside of social comparison

Social comparison is a powerful motivator for individuals and organizations. Consider how Jeff Bezos, Elon Musk, and Richard Branson compete to develop private space exploration. There’s social comparison involved. If you look at the shapes of some of these rockets, there’s probably a phallic interpretation as well. Mine is bigger than yours. I mean, just do a Google search for “Blue Origin rocket.”

Does social comparison provide the wrong kind of motivation? If you’re a proponent of Pink’s model, then yes, social comparison is bad because it’s extrinsic motivation. Therefore, it’s less effective than an intrinsic accomplishment focus, á la Seligman. Stoic philosophy, which I find powerful, soothing, and practical, also frowns upon social comparison. Stoics argue that you can’t control what other people do. You can only control how you react, and it’s silly to worry about the success of others. From a highly rated yet little-known Stoic podcast by Erick Cloward:

You can’t control how good someone else is going to be at something, and when you compare yourselves with them, you are tying your success to something you can’t control. You can only control yourself and your own skill, so the only real measure should be, are you improving?

On this one, Dr. Zimet, the sports psychologist, added a bit of realism: “True, but I have rarely met an elite athlete who, as a child, never had the thought, ‘I want to be the best!’”

Most of the themes in academic psychology that we’ve covered tie back to Stoicism. The mastery focus, intrinsic motivation, each component of ERMA… you’ll find these concepts in the writings of Seneca, Marcus Aurelius, and other Stoics. These philosophers tell you to work on yourself before leading others. Seneca, for example, wrote: “No person has the power to have everything they want, but it is in their power to not want what they don’t have, and to cheerfully put to good use what they have.”

You may think that “to not want what you don’t have” is trite advice. But pay attention to the second part of the quote: “cheerfully put to good use what [you] have.” Gratitude is the cure for hedonic adaptation. Seligman emphasizes gratitude as a component of positive psychology. It’s a well-trodden self-improvement topic, yet it’s rarely applied as a leadership skill. There’s a need for more gratitude in the workplace and in teams in general. Leaders may celebrate wins, but they rarely spend enough time reminding everyone of what makes the team great, why the organization is lucky to benefit from competitive advantages, or why their culture is special. Even in tough times, or especially in tough times, gratitude lifts morale.

For the Stoics, it’s about managing one’s perceptions. You can choose to be grateful. And for the record, the idea that Stoicism is about ignoring pain is wrong. It’s about how you handle yourself in the face of adversity. Take action on what can be controlled and accept the uncontrollable as a given. Getting angry or anxious about something you can’t control is a waste of energy. With practice, you can control your reactions to setbacks. This applies to organizations, too. Our Chief Financial Officer told her team that the organization should “accept what we can’t control, such as changing business trends, and focus on what we control—how we adapt to these trends.” Well said.

I encourage you to read about Stoicism. And if you don’t like reading abstract texts with old language and grammar, I encourage you to read any book by Ryan Holiday, a bestselling author and modern student of Stoicism. He’s a fantastic writer. In fact, he’s a better writer than me. (See what I did there? Social comparison.)

Humans seem wired for social comparison. There’s an entire field of study in psychology devoted to it. Research suggests that as much as ten percent of your thoughts involve comparisons to others. You compare how successful you are, how you look, how much money you make, how smart you are… How often do you think about these things? Be honest!

When I lived in California, I regularly went trail running with friends. Sometimes, we couldn’t help but compete to see who could run up a steep hill the fastest. We had the urge, the uncontrollable itch, to measure ourselves against each other. It was all polite and everything, but occasionally, we just had to find out who was the fittest runner. Something in our DNA forced us to compete.

One of my friends invited his wife, a strong runner, to join us one day. After the run, when they got home, she said to my friend: “Sébastien is so competitive. If someone sneezes, he’s going to want to sneeze harder.”

Social comparison has benefits and drawbacks. It’s a double-edged sword. It fuels capitalism, an economic system that’s not perfect but for which there is no better alternative. It motivates people to improve in sports and many other areas.

My advice? If you use social comparison within a broader accomplishment mindset to calibrate your intrinsic goals, compare away! Sneeze harder.

Psychology Today has a straightforward, plain-language summary of the scientific literature on Social Comparison Theory, which goes back to 1954. The authors highlight the positive side:

Social comparison can be highly beneficial …. In a study, friendly competition was highly effective in pushing people to exercise more, as peers pushed each other to keep up and do more. In such a ‘social ratchet effect,’ each person’s activity generates more activity among others.

But to me, the most interesting part is the next sentence in this summary: “Social networks in which people simply offered each other positive encouragement were far less helpful.”

Most people get more motivation through competition than positive encouragement.

If Musk’s mom calls him to tell him that he’s doing a good job building reusable rockets, I bet it won’t be nearly as motivating as Jeff Bezos bragging about doing the same on X (Twitter) and not giving Musk credit for pioneering the concept. Here’s the X exchange:

“The rarest of beasts – a used rocket. Controlled landing not easy, but done right, can look easy.” –Jeff Bezos, November 24, 2015, Twitter

“@JeffBezos Not quite “rarest.” SpaceX Grasshopper rocket did 6 suborbital flights 3 years ago & is still around.” –Elon Musk, November 24, 2015, Twitter

They both posted a picture of their rocket. (Insert facile mine-is-bigger-than-yours joke here.)

As a leader, you can use healthy internal competition to boost productivity. Popular tools for this include sales or productivity leaderboards and the classic employee of the month award.

External competition works even better. I’ve found that almost nothing motivates the troops as much as reminding them that a competitor is gaining market share on us. It helps to post industry rankings for everyone to see and set targets to rise in the ranks, providing updates along the way to keep the organization in flow.

Like its benefits, the drawbacks of social comparison are intuitive. From Psychology Today:

Research has shown that people who regularly compare themselves to others may find motivation to improve, but may also experience feelings of deep dissatisfaction, guilt, or remorse, and engage in destructive behaviors like lying or disordered eating.

That’s why the Stoics, Seligman, Pink, and other thought leaders frown on it.

Social media is a good example of what can go wrong with social comparison. It’s been linked to depression and, counterintuitively, loneliness. In 2021, the Wall Street Journal exposed a series of internal communications from Facebook that revealed the company is aware of the adverse mental health side effects of its platforms. “Facebook Knows Instagram Is Toxic for Many Teen Girls, Company Documents Show,” screamed one of the headlines. Social media gives you intravenous social comparison—in pure, concentrated doses. The problem is that people are putting on a show on Instagram and Facebook. It’s not real life. Comparing yourself to ever-happy, curated versions of others is not healthy.

Also, if you’re posting content, every time you get a “Like” or a positive comment, you’re getting a small rush of dopamine, which, over time, will turn you into a digital addict (see Anna Lembke’s excellent book Dopamine Nation). I only use Strava, an app for runners and bikers to share their activities, and LinkedIn, a professional network. (And yes, I admit, I look at how many “Likes” I get for each post.)

In organizations, social comparison can hurt collaboration. Use it carefully. Internal competition must not come at the expense of trust, mutual respect, and a shared understanding of the team’s mission. It must be friendly and enjoyable. Counterintuitively, you can even make internal competition a tool for collaboration. How? Make collaboration the metric on which people compete. For example, you could give out a “top collaborator of the month” award.

Also, focusing on external competition can turn everyone’s competitive attention to the outside. It’s good for team cohesion to have a common enemy. The villain is not your peer; it’s your external competitor. Here, the same warning as for measurable goals applies: Your organization’s values come first. Remind everyone to play by the rules.

Takeaways to improve your mental game

When it comes to social comparison, you must consider the negatives and the positives. Use social comparison to your benefit. Enjoy the competition. Go ahead and keep score—just don’t get wrapped up in it. If you’re a runner, racing will help you push yourself. Running also requires a ton of intrinsic motivation. That’s a good balance. But if you spend too much time on Facebook and Instagram, you may be dipping into the negative side of social comparison.

Because it largely ignores research in psychology, the literature on leadership is silent on the negatives and the positives of social comparison in business. A competitive mindset is a potent motivator. Leaders should keep score to push themselves and their organization. You can create competitive, mentally resilient environments but remain self-aware enough to avoid introducing the negatives of social comparison into the individual mindset and company culture.

Learn to use internal and external competition to benefit your organization. Rally your team against a worthy competitor. Make internal competition friendly and enjoyable. Internal competition should be secondary to collaboration—or even a tool for it.

And please, please, please, I beg you to hit “Like” on my LinkedIn posts.

Principle 9: Upgrade Your Operating System—Identify and Manage Your Core Beliefs

“Beliefs are the driving force behind our actions.”

—Martin Seligman, psychologist, father of positive psychology

One of the best things you can do to improve as a leader is to know yourself better. To do so, it helps to understand your roots and define your (and your organization’s) belief systems.

If you met me, you’d never guess I grew up on a farm. If you give me the choice between manual labor and a root canal, I might ask you to start the drilling. I wear suits, and I can’t fix anything around the house. If a lightbulb goes out, I ask, “Can we get someone to fix this?” However, my upbringing has shaped how I make decisions today as a leader. From my mother, I’ve learned to combine empathy with personal boundaries. From my father, I’ve learned how to value hard work as its own reward, as well as the importance of self-determination and the concept of the meritocracy.

In psychology, belief systems are called core beliefs. These are the mental reflexes that drive a lot of your decisions. Core beliefs may be a set of “We believe” statements for your organization, such as “We believe the customer is always right.” You can trace most of your personal core beliefs to your upbringing, just like corporations’ core beliefs are often established by their founder.

Principle 9, “Upgrade Your Operating System—Identify and Manage Your Core Beliefs,” proposes a new way to apply the power of psychology to improve the practice of leadership. This principle requires a hefty dose of personal and organizational self-awareness. To put it into practice at a personal level, you may have to dig into your past.

My roots

My parents are examples of role models with a lifelong accomplishment orientation. Reflecting on my upbringing, I find many of the core beliefs that drive how I make decisions today as a leader.

My mother

My mother carries herself confidently, is physically fit, drinks a little too much coffee (like me), and is incredibly intelligent. She’s pleasant to be around and quite social, but if she wants to, she can be a little scary. She commands respect.

She was a social worker in a psychiatric hospital for her entire working life. She helped people with mental illness reintegrate into society and provided most of the counseling and mental therapy they needed.

That was her lifelong mission: to help people with mental illness. It was a dangerous job, being a woman, driving people with severe mental illness around in her car without security backup. But I heard her say once that after decades in the job, she had never felt really in danger, minus one or two minor incidents.

Now that she’s retired, she goes on humanitarian missions around the world, helping people who live in poverty. When I think about the accomplishment orientation, I don’t just think about business leaders. I also think about my mother, who has helped thousands of people improve their lives. She’s never derived satisfaction from making more money, getting a promotion, or getting more “Likes” on social media. Her purpose has been about altruistic relationships.

My father

As for my father, he was a finance professor for over 40 years. He was a tough grader—the kind of professor who pushes strong students to grow and allows for grade dispersion. Strong students loved him, but he made the mediocre students sweat. In his classes, you either learned something for life or you received a bad grade.

Sometimes, I run into his former students. Many of them say that my father was the best professor they ever had. On the other hand, some remember his class as “torture.” I secretly put these complainers in the mediocre student category.

During his working life, my father had a herculean work ethic. In addition to his day job as a professor, he maintained a working cattle farm by himself. Being a farmer requires hours of physical labor at all times of the day. Now, imagine it’s your second job. He also published more educational material than any other professor across Quebec. If you stacked his published textbooks, you’d have a tower higher than your kitchen table. I may have inherited some of his workaholism, but I’ve learned to pace myself in recent years to remain productive and healthy in the long run.

I surmise that my father was working hard to get in a flow state. It didn’t matter whether he was cleaning the barn, putting up a fence, chopping wood, teaching finance, writing, or deriving mathematical equations. All these activities put him in a state of mental or psychomotor concentration, during which he lost track of time.

When I was a teenager, his idea of quality father–son time was to force me to join manual labor projects on the farm. It would drive me crazy. I wasn’t intrinsically motivated to do the work.

It took me years to understand that he was teaching me by example.

He’s retired now. If you visit him, you’ll find a white-haired man in his seventies running a chainsaw, veins popping out of his forearms, standing next to a giant pile of freshly cut logs. He’ll be in his third straight hour with the chainsaw running, with no intention of stopping any time soon.

My father’s engagement/flow orientation came with the big life mission version of accomplishment. The best way to describe his lifetime goal is to replay a conversation I had with him about 30 years ago.

It was a normal conversation, albeit profound, which is probably why I remember it to this day. I asked my father: “Why haven’t you made tons of money in the private sector, maybe as a consultant, instead of teaching? You would have been incredibly successful. You’d be wealthy.”

I didn’t mean it that way, but “you would have been incredibly successful,” came across as a backhanded compliment. He was successful as a professor. He answered: “How do you define ‘wealth’? I’ve trained generations of financial experts. Most of them, thousands of them, have become incredibly successful. It makes me happy just thinking about that. This is my definition of ‘wealth.’”

In a sense, my father is a billionaire—just add up the money his students have made. That’s how he thinks about it. He talks about their success with a sense of pride. He’s maintained lifelong friendships with a few of them. They buy him good wine. They can afford it.

What about your parents? There’s an important strand of research in psychiatry and psychology about how childhood shapes your psyche—with Freudian theory (psychoanalysis) as the pillar. I’ve never been into that. Although, for most of us, whether it’s nature (inherited) or nurture (acquired), our parents have played an essential role in shaping our belief systems.

Core beliefs: definition

In psychology, core beliefs represent what’s important to us, what we’re striving for, what motivates us, and what we’re afraid of. They drive the decisions we make.

But most people don’t really know what their core beliefs are. Do you know yours?

Core beliefs can come from different sources, usually from childhood and development. They can be things you observe in your parents. Often, they can be from critical events.

Consider the example of someone who was betrayed in a significant way. This experience, which could have happened decades earlier, drives how the person makes most of their decisions—it’s one of their “core instructional messages”: Don’t trust anybody.

When you feel deeply about something, describe what’s going on in your head. You can often distill those emotions and thoughts down to a specific sentence that you can write down.

Core beliefs are often extreme and hyperbolic. They might have worked for the situation in your childhood that they’re founded upon, but are rarely effective once you’re an adult. They leave no room for flexibility. And since they’re formed in childhood from profound circumstances, they lack an adult’s ability to consider alternatives, modulate the intensity, or recognize the situation’s complexity.

I encourage you to manage and improve your core beliefs.

Core beliefs and personal leadership

On the personal level, identifying—and managing—core beliefs will help you improve your leadership style. Here’s a personal example: For most of my career, I’ve almost always assumed the worst-case scenario when it came to office politics. My boss at my prior employer used to say that I heard “high frequencies.” You know how dogs can hear sounds that humans cannot? He thought I was too sensitive to the political noise that most people don’t even perceive.

“Well, it’s not paranoia if they’re really after you,” I would joke.

Over the last few years, however, I’ve learned to assume good intentions, which is a much better core belief. He wasn’t a trained psychologist, but my old boss helped me identify an unhelpful core belief. I’m grateful for that. He gave me an opportunity to improve. I’ve built a thicker skin, and my peer relationships are now built on a default position of trust. Did any of this process involve going back to my childhood? Apologies to Freud, but my childhood never crossed my mind. I’ve probably built this political wariness over several years and across various events. Does it matter?

My point is that you can work on your core beliefs without going to therapy (not that there’s anything wrong with going to therapy). So, give it a try! If you’re brave, ask people close to you to help. Or, next time you get a performance review, look at the process through a new lens—look for what might help you pinpoint your core beliefs.

Core beliefs and organizational leadership

As we do throughout this book, we can borrow from psychology and apply the model at the organizational level. Organizations are merely a collection of individuals. How can you apply core beliefs to your leadership role and your responsibility to set goals and cultural norms for your team?

Companies, like individuals, have their own core beliefs—I call those organizational core beliefs. Some of them are useful, others perhaps counterproductive. And in a lot of cases, you can trace them to the company’s childhood, i.e., to its founder(s). Think of how organizations define their mission statement. Or how most companies internally publish “we believe” lists to outline cultural norms.

Here’s a list of my favorite “we believe” business principles from my own organization:

* Investment Excellence: The heart of everything we do.
* Clients First: We succeed if our clients succeed.
* Collegiality and Collaboration: Leveraging the best ideas creates competitive advantages.
* Trust and Mutual Respect: Essential for a strong community.
* Long-Term Horizon: A true competitive advantage.

Do these sound like the corporate equivalent of motherhood and apple pie? Perhaps. Often, published cultural norms are merely words on a PowerPoint page. However, for my team, these are core beliefs, whose origins predate my tenure. These bullet points also define our accomplishment orientation of focusing on long-term investment results for our clients.

Our company was founded in 1937. Our founder, Thomas Rowe Price, exemplified these values. “We succeed if our clients succeed,” is a direct quote from him.

We aren’t perfect at each of these cultural elements every day, but when times get tough or our performance is subpar, we fall back on them. They make us stronger and bind us together. They are deeply engrained into the company’s ethos.

It’s when you face stressful situations that your core beliefs kick in. It’s the same at the organizational level. I’ve been in many difficult meetings when people disagreed on the way forward, on product development, pricing, investment, risk management, and various other issues. In all cases, whoever can prove—or convince others—that their proposal is best for our clients wins the argument. I’m not exaggerating. This is how we resolve arguments: Make your case on behalf of our clients.

This approach is not as simple as it seems. There are tradeoffs involved. Money managers sometimes need to turn down prospective clients. This occurs when accepting new money would make it harder for the portfolio manager to beat the markets on behalf of existing clients because the trades would get too big. In these cases, you really, really need to believe in the “investment excellence is the heart of everything we do” mantra. It needs to be a core belief.

Portfolio managers have their core beliefs as well. I’ve mentioned before that managing money is about as stressful a job as it gets. You will lose money at some point. Will you double down because you believe the investment is still solid? After all, if fundamentals have not changed, you now have an opportunity to buy it at a cheaper price, i.e., buy the dip. Or will you cut your losses to avoid catching the proverbial falling knife?

There’s no perfect answer. Your core beliefs help you navigate these treacherous waters. If you focus on valuation and your core belief is “buy low, sell high,” you must be comfortable being a contrarian.

Another core belief portfolio managers can rely on is “the trend is your friend.” In industry lingo, we refer to it as “momentum.” The theory is that when the price of an investment goes up, more people want to buy it, which pushes the price even higher. And when the price goes down, more people want to sell it, either because they panic or merely to limit their losses. Often, valuation investors trade against momentum investors. These two types of investors think differently, especially when markets get volatile.

These are just examples of core beliefs in money management. Remember that every organization has core beliefs. Too often, they’re left unseen and unsaid, and some of them might be counterproductive. How do you identify and manage your organization’s core beliefs? Here’s a three-step process:

1. Observe: Core beliefs will often be expressed when stress is running high and important decisions need to be made. Sit quietly in meetings and pay attention to the rules of thumb or common sayings being used at critical moments. (Like “safety first” or “the customer is always right.”) Write them down.
2. Discuss: Bring the debate into the open with your lead team. Explain what core beliefs are. Are the company’s written values or mission statement consistent with what you’ve observed? Reflect on the company’s DNA, its founders, and critical moments in its evolution. How do these drive organizational core beliefs?
3. Formalize: Decide which core beliefs should be abandoned or modified, and which ones should be emphasized. Write down the final set of your organizationa’s core beliefs, and formulate a plan to communicate and reinfore them. Keep it short and simple.

Takeaways to improve your mental game

This principle introduces a new model to master the psychology of leadership: organizational core beliefs. How do people at your company react in stressful situations? What are the common assumptions about the world everyone seems to agree with? What are the go-to sayings or phrases used to interpret challenges and make decisions?

Whether at the individual or the organizational level, self-awareness is powerful.

Organizational core beliefs are rooted in the firm’s history and can often be traced back to its founders. You must identify, manage, and memorialize them in writing. Make them part of the company’s language, culture, and mindset. Revisit trite mission statements. Manage the operating system that drives behaviors inside your company.

Do the same with your personal core beliefs. Go ahead and explore your childhood and traumatic or memorable life events if needed. Managing and upgrading your core beliefs will help you avoid frequent errors in judgment, better allocate your time and energy, and ultimately, become a better leader, which is what this book is all about.

As for me, I’m pleased to report that I’ve recently changed a light bulb.

Takeaways on Goals

That brings us to the end of Part 1, where we’ve looked at setting long-term goals.

Goals impact your mental game, and setting goals is the most important part of your role as a leader. You must get your personal goals in order. Returning to core beliefs, here’s a good one: “Manage yourself before you manage others.”

Yet most of us spend little time defining our long-term goals. Core beliefs can help explain WHY a goal is essential to you personally. And while the corporate world is indeed obsessed with goals, organizations too often set the wrong targets.

So, we’re all walking in the fog without a clear view of where we’re heading. I ask you to step back from the daily grind and search your soul. I’m giving you the best tools from scientific psychology. Ask yourself, how thick is the fog?

If you focus on money and career, there’s nothing wrong with that. And if you manage your organization through specific and measurable goals, that’s a good thing. But beware of the side effects of goals. You must avoid goal-induced blindness. Some people obsess over the Everest summit and lose fingers to frostbite, or even worse, die on the mountain.

Also, remember that mismanaged goals can reduce intrinsic motivation and may push you or your team toward an ego-driven orientation rather than a mastery focus. Golfers should derive satisfaction from improving their swing, not their scorecard.

And don’t forget that luck plays a significant role. You must examine your process at least as much as the actual results. Differentiate between luck and skill. Don’t think that making it home after you drove drunk is a good thing. Even if the goal was achieved, you made a bad decision.

In 2021, my division had one of the best years in our franchise’s history. We hit home runs in our investment results (many of our strategies were in the top one percent of their peer groups), product development milestones, and revenue growth. But the stock market gods were favorable. And I made plenty of blunders as a leader despite a near-perfect outcome.

Remember these key questions

Once you’ve realized the importance of goals and their side effects, next comes the tougher question: Which goals should you set for yourself and your organization? Most people think that to reach your goals means you’ll be happy. That’s not necessarily true! Andre Agassi’s goal was to become a pro tennis player. He became one of the best players of all time. Read his biography and tell me if you think it made him happy.

You must reverse the equation: Start with your definition of happiness, then define your goals. As a leader, the definition of organizational happiness is fuzzier, but the same idea applies.

The PERMA model tells us that long-term happiness—well-being or thriving—differs from day-to-day positive emotions. Most of us spend too much mental energy seeking positive emotions. And companies focus too much on quarterly earnings and short-term wins. Reframe your goals around ERMA: engagement, relationships, meaning, and accomplishment.

Lastly, perhaps the most important thought exercise that brings this all together is one Seligman recommends: Write your obituary. I mean, you don’t have to actually write it. Just think about what you want it to say. To put day-to-day frustrations in perspective, Stoic philosophers meditated on death and the shortness of life relative to the infinite vastness of time. On a cosmic level (bear with me!), we’re here for a microsecond. Zooming out on your life does two things: First, it’s soothing if you’re going through a tough time; second, it gives perspective on the relative importance of P vs. ERMA.

So, if you want to be happy, think about death.

Part II: Executing Your Goals

Now that we’ve covered how to set goals for yourself and your organization, we need to discuss Japanese dancing mice. Why, you ask? We’ll get back to this vital topic. For now, let me introduce the notion that even if you clear your mental fog by setting long-term goals, your life and career can and will go astray. Execution is hard. Really hard. And guess what? Stuff happens. (By “stuff,” I mean a four-letter word that also starts with “s” but that my compliance department won’t let me use.)

In the first part of this book, I asked you to brew a big cup of coffee, think long term, and set big goals. It’s time to stop walking in the fog. Positive psychology encourages us to think about how we define happiness. I proposed a new approach to leadership that expands this field of research from personal goals to leading an organization.

This second part covers execution. It begins with a discussion on managing stress for optimal performance. You can’t go after big goals if you can’t manage your stress. Then, I explain how control theory—a powerful set of mathematical models and principles typically used in engineering—addresses many of the execution challenges you will face on the journey towards your big goals.

Challenges with execution include how to:

* calibrate your goals (how high should you aim?),
* deal with personal and organizational stress,
* build resilience when you face setbacks, and
* determine when to stick to your plan and when to change strategic direction.

When goals collide with the real world, s(tuff) gets messy.

Earlier in my finance career, I submitted a paper to a finance journal where my co-authors and I suggested a new trading strategy that we thought was quite clever. But the anonymous journal referee—the gatekeeper who decides whether your paper gets published—hated it. Our model optimized the buy and sell transactions involved in moving massive amounts of money between asset managers. It was, in theory, a beautiful model. It laid out the complete roadmap for the buy and sell transactions based on the volatility of all the stocks involved and their correlations (how the stocks typically move together).

The model set the right goals: minimize trading costs and maximize trading speed. And it produced a clear roadmap for execution. No fog whatsoever. It would be an industry-changing, award-winning paper. Or so I thought.

There was only one problem. When you trade stocks, especially in large amounts, other traders notice. They try to guess your next buy or sell trades. Nowadays, robots (computer algorithms) execute trading strategies in milliseconds, ingesting, analyzing, and acting on massive amounts of data faster than any human could.

Our paper was naïve to the dynamic (action-reaction) nature of execution. No matter how clear and thoughtful your goals are, life is chaotic. The referee used a kinder analogy than sharks, but I’ll never forget what he wrote because it applies to so many areas of life: “You’re not playing tennis against the wall. You’re playing against a flesh-and-blood opponent.”

Don’t get too enamored with theory, like we did with that article. Too many corporate executives, when they pontificate about strategy, sitting in comfortable meeting rooms inside the walls of their organization, forget that whatever strategy they come up with, their competitors will react.

I call this the ivory tower problem. It happens when the decision-makers (generals) have no awareness of what’s happening in the trenches (soldiers), but they’re making critical decisions for those soldiers. Lives are at stake. How do you get generals aware of what’s going on?

Remember that PERMA has much empirical support in scientific psychology, but it’s a model. You need the tools and practice to help you play the game better. That’s what we’ll discuss in the next few chapters, starting with how to calibrate your goals based on research performed over 110 years ago on Japanese dancing mice.

(Oh, and what did we do with our paper on optimal trading? We did what every sensible researcher does when facing an antagonistic referee: We added a few caveats to address the questions raised and sent it to another journal.)

Principle 10: Tune the Engine—Optimize Stress for Peak Performance

“Embracing stress is like a superpower.”

—Tim Ferris, author and lifestyle guru

My job is stressful. There’s no way around it. I manage a team of ultra-high performers who are smarter than me and don’t suffer poor leadership. Our division oversees hundreds of millions in assets under management, on which millions of people depend for their retirement and to improve their financial lives.

Principle 10, “Tune the Engine -Optimize Stress for Peak Performance,” reveals that stress can be both good and bad, and that it’s impossible to minimize it to zero. Instead, it must be optimized. This may seem counterintuitive, but you need stress to perform at your best. It’s the same for your team in terms of organizational stress.

We begin our discussion on stress by looking at the Yerkes-Dodson (YD) law. As a leader, you must manage stress across three dimensions: your own, your employees’, and your organization’s. This model is useful across all three. It will help you decide:

* How ambitious to make goals and targets to maximize performance.
* When to crank up the pressure and when to slow down.
* How to adjust stress or pressure levels based on whether a job requires concentration or is simple to execute.

The beauty of this model is its simplicity. It deserves broader use as a self-improvement tool and in leadership practice. Of all the models we discuss in this book, this is the most potent medicine to increase resilience for you and your organization.

The surprising link between stress and performance

The Yerkes-Dodson (YD) curve, or law, maps performance to stress levels. It looks like an inverted U. Initially, as stress increases, performance increases because you become more motivated and alert. At some point, you maximize your performance. After that threshold, performance deteriorates. You choke under the pressure. Hence, the YD law seeks a Goldilocks level of stress. There’s a similarity to the flow theory we discussed earlier. Flow predicts that you maximize engagement between a task that’s not too cold (easy and boring) and not too hot (impossible to attain).

The Yerkes-Dodson law

![](image/YerkesDodson.png)

Francesca Gino, behavioral scientist and Professor at Harvard Business School, published an article on the YD law in the Harvard Business Review titled “Are You Too Stressed to Be Productive? Or Not Stressed Enough?” These two questions are an excellent way to capture the inverted U curve in a nutshell.

“I always assumed that if I could just reduce any stress I was facing, my productivity would rise. But my intuition was, in fact, wrong. It’s true that stress can be a health risk …. But research suggests that some stress can actually be beneficial to performance,” Gino explains.

Like an elite athlete, realize that you can’t operate at your level without a bit of stress. Isn’t that liberating? Now you can stop stressing about stressing.

Embrace stress and look for the top of the inverted U. To embrace stress is an exciting concept. Most people think stress is bad. Not necessarily! In sports, it would be silly for a top-level athlete to expect not to feel nervous when the game is close and there are a few seconds left on the clock.

As I dug into the psychology literature on Yerkes-Dodson, I found a few things worth clarifying. First, when they describe the YD law, most psychologists refer to “arousal” instead of stress. They use the term arousal as a broader concept, including alertness and vigilance. In what follows, we’re using a broad definition of stress that includes feeling elevated, ready and alert, focused and prepared—fully present and energized.

Second, the origins of the YD law are quirky. Psychologists Robert M. Yerkes and John Dillingham Dodson wrote the original paper in 1908. If you want to research any field outside your area of expertise, you should read some of the landmark papers. These are the papers that survive the test of time.

But the YD paper is strange. And apparently, it was largely ignored for about 50 years.

Yerkes and Dodson trained Japanese dancing mice to solve a simple problem: go through a white door, or a black door? If the mouse went through the black door, it got electrocuted. Ouch.

Imagine two bespectacled intellectuals in their white lab coats. They’ve built a small maze with electrical apparatus, and they’re taking notes on what the mice are doing. That’s how we learn about human psychology? Really? I’ll get back to this question.

As mice circled, the black and white doors randomly changed position.

YD varied the doors’ brightness and the shock’s intensity behind the black door.

When the authors cranked up the shock, the mice learned to avoid the black door faster than with a weaker shock. But up to a point. The strongest shock level messed with the mice’s heads. They became too stressed to choose the right door.

This is the origin of the inverted U curve.

YD were surprised. They expected that the mice’s performance would continue to increase as they increased the strength of the shock.

Their conclusion from this part of their experiment was that: “An intermediate range of intensity of stimulation proved to be most favorable to the acquisition of a habit.”

Stress isn’t a bad thing. You just needed to operate in the intermediate range—the top of the U curve—that’s optimal for cognitive (or athletic) performance.

Task-specific optimal stress

There’s more to the YD experiment. The researchers also found that the optimal stress level depends on task difficulty.

This is less intuitive. But it’s essential when it comes to calibrating goals for optimal performance. Here’s a quiz: Do complicated tasks require a higher or lower stress level for optimal performance compared to simple tasks? Give it a second. Think about it…

I’m guessing your answer is that more complicated tasks require a higher stress level. If so, your answer is wrong. If you want to optimize performance, it’s not that simple.

We must distinguish between easy vs. hard and simple vs. complex. Psychologists have interpreted YD’s mice maze experiments as simple vs. complex, not necessarily easy vs. hard. Hence, the YD law refers more to cognitive difficulty (think: puzzle-solving) than, say, physical effort.

The key is concentration. If you need more concentration, your performance will peak at a lower stress level. Beyond managing your own stress, this has profound implications for leadership. Try to avoid giving hard deadlines to teams that need to solve complex problems. But if you manage employees involved in simple, repetitive, operational tasks, it’s quite possible that if you (gently) increase the pressure and set more aggressive targets, performance will increase.

Here are examples from a psychology textbook: “It is almost impossible for sprinters to get too stressed for a race. The task is direct and uncomplicated …. [But] a basketball player making a game-deciding free throw faces a more sensitive and complex task. Excessive stress is almost certain to hurt his or her performance.” [3](ThePsychologyofLeadership-ebook-050325-19.xhtml#footnote-002)

Here are more examples of simple vs. complex tasks:

![](image/Table02.jpg)

You can perform the tasks on the left under tremendous stress because they’re simpler. These tasks require low levels of concentration. If I blast Metallica over loudspeakers and poke you in the ribs with a stick, you won’t like me, but you’ll probably still be able to calculate the answer to 2 + 3 at about the same speed as if you were sitting quietly at your desk. But if I ask you to calculate 349 + 1,134? That may be harder, especially if I play “Master of Puppets.”

“Master of puppets, I’m pulling your strings; Twisting your mind and smashing your dreams; Blinded by me, you can’t see a thing…” If you’re a Metallica fan, you’ll have this song in your head for the rest of the day—you’re welcome.

Another takeaway is that if optimal performance is reached at a lower stress level, it doesn’t mean a task is easier. It just means the task requires more concentration. Research and development involve hard and complex tasks. Don’t push your research team too much with productivity metrics and hard deadlines. Instead, focus on accountability and autonomy, which, as we’ve discussed, are strong long-term motivators.

But your assembly line? Go ahead and crank up the pressure a little. Of course, I don’t mean that you should apply undue pressure. There’s a skillful way to do this, using metrics and short-term rewards to get the team in flow, and fostering a fun and friendly competitive environment. Basically, you should gamify the work!

There’s a fine line between stress and feeling energized and excited. As mentioned, research in psychology—the purist version of the YD law included—lumps these concepts together using the broader terms of arousal and activation.

For very easy or simple tasks, the inverted U curve may never invert.  You can brush your teeth faster and faster under increasing stress, but there’s a point at which you can’t brush them any faster. Therefore, the YD curve for simple, easy tasks flattens rather than bends down. I mean, how much stress would you have to be under not to be able to run in a straight line for 100 meters or calculate 2+3?

With bright doors making the choice easy for the mice, YD kept increasing the shock, and the mice kept learning faster until the authors gave up. They went all the way to “410” shock strength, at which point the mice learned to pick the white door in 40 trials. Perhaps the mice could have learned the task in as little as 30 or 20 trials… as long as the shock didn’t kill them.

We can interpret how YD curves change as a function of task complexity through the lens of Daniel Kahneman’s work on fast vs. slow thinking. Kahneman is a Nobel-prize-winning psychologist and economist. His book Thinking, Fast and Slow is a bestseller, with over 2.6 million copies sold. A warning: It’s a repetitive book. This one-star review on Amazon of the 500-page book made me chuckle: “Most of the times we think fast. Then we make mistakes. Rarely, but sometimes, we do think slow. Then we get it right. There, you’ve read the book. Read this fast, think it over slowly, and skip this book.”

This link between YD curves and Kahneman’s work is yet another example of how different strands of research in psychology boil down to a handful of core concepts. Maybe the human psyche isn’t that complicated after all.

The part of our brain that takes over in crisis allows us to act on first impressions. Think of the fight-or-flight response. It’s primitive thinking. It’s not a very thoughtful mode. You can make terrible choices that way, but you act immediately. In contrast, the thinking slow part of the brain is deliberate and conscientious and solves problems through deep processing. This mode is thoughtful. That’s the part of the brain you need for complex tasks. Unfortunately, it’s hard to access it when you’re under a lot of stress.

You will be stressed when you try to make the three-point shot in basketball at the buzzer in a tied game or when you take a math exam. It’s unrealistic to expect not to be.

I don’t think I’ve ever been more stressed than when I took the Chartered Financial Analyst exams (if you’re in the finance industry, I’m sure you can relate). But you need to be calm enough to perform optimally. You can’t go full fight-or-flight. When people get too stressed during exams, they tend to get stuck on a specific question they can’t answer. This echoes my father giving his students exams with more questions than they could answer under the time limit. He was training us to handle the pressure of the race for points. To win that race, we had to bring ourselves back up from the declining leg of the inverted U. Similarly, think about what’s needed to perform at your best in public presentations: You’re stressed, no doubt, but you take a deep breath, calm yourself down a bit, and enter the stage at the top of your inverted U.

A few caveats on Yerkes-Dodson

While writing this book, I attended a quantitative finance conference where I ran into an old colleague and friend, Dave Turkington. I greatly respect Dave’s intellect—he works hard, understands abstract math, and has intuition and common sense. What’s more, he’s a good guy. I don’t know many people who combine these positive attributes like Dave.

Cocktail receptions at conferences are meant for networking. I don’t like these receptions. I find small talk excruciating, and I think Dave feels the same, so we usually find each other to have a real conversation.

We discussed Dave’s latest book, Prediction Revisited, which he co-authored with Megan Czasonis and Mark Kritzman. The book shows how to improve data analysis to predict the future. The statistical methods are most valuable when there’s not only randomness but also uncertainty in the data, like in finance and economics. Dave was excited because the book had been accepted for publication. He thought the content might appeal to a broader audience beyond quantitative finance experts.

Not to be outdone, I said: “You know, I’m also working on a book for a broader audience. And I’m writing about a topic outside of finance.”

Dave isn’t motivated by social comparison. Going back to our PERMA model, he finds meaning in his work. I suspect Dave would pass on a promotion if it meant he’d have to give up his intellectual endeavors to spend more time in boring meetings dealing with company politics.

But what he said next bruised my ego a little: “Ah, yes. I’ve read some of your recent articles on LinkedIn. You’ve gotten into pop psychology.”

Ouch. Dave didn’t mean it that way, but I could swear I saw a slight smile when he said, “pop psychology.”

In this book, I try to present the research and models objectively. I hope you’ll agree that, unlike in most self-help books, I don’t follow the oversimplify-and-repeat formula. It’s unhelpful and uninteresting to present a one-sided view of the world.

Which is why, before we dig further into how to use the YD law to calibrate goals, I need to clear the air. Unlike the academic psychology models we’ve covered so far—and we’ve covered many, including Nicholls on mastery vs. ego orientation, Seligman on PERMA, Csikszentmihalyi on flow, Waldinger on relationships and happiness, Hermann on Brain Dominance, Rock on the neuroscience of feedback, Pink on intrinsic motivation, and more—the YD law is… how should I put it… a bit more pop psychology than academic psychology.

Think about It: it’s based on a strange experiment on Japanese dancing mice that was conducted in 1908. Since then, the YD law has been co-opted by psychologists in many ways. I can’t find much serious research on it, but it’s ubiquitous in pop psychology. You know the type of article: 800 words, without references, published online. Karl Halvor Teigen of the University of Tromso, Norway, published a scathing article on the YD Law in 1994, titled “Yerkes-Dodson: A Law for All Seasons.” One of the problems is that it’s been applied in ways that are not directly related to the original idea. Teigen explains:

Authors have rendered it variously as the effects of punishment, reward, motivation, drive, arousal, anxiety, tension or stress upon learning, performance, problem-solving, coping or memory. … These changes are seldom discussed and are often misattributed to Yerkes and Dodson themselves.

The concept of the inverted U is appealing. Once you grasp it, you’ll find all sorts of areas where it applies. YD laws are everywhere. “It is disturbing to discover that successive generations of psychologists cannot agree as to what this celebrated law actually states,” adds Teigen.

Then comes the ultimate insult, a reference to “pop” psychology: “…versions of this ‘law’ seem to mirror the currently most popular concepts in [psychology].”

Another article by Martin Corbett, published in 2015 in the Journal of Managerial Psychology, goes further. It states that the YD law “has no basis in empirical fact.” Corbett argues that all stress is bad and should be systematically minimized.

I disagree with this simplistic and impractical view of the world based on common sense. And so does Dr. Zimet, who tells me that it’s been empirically verified in sports psychology studies that athletes perform better at optimal, not minimal, stress levels. I can tell you, without a shadow of a doubt, that I perform better in various tasks when my stress level rises. Don’t you?

It can’t just be that the lower the stress, the better. It’s not that simple. We all perform better when we get closer to a deadline, right?

“Being underwhelmed [under-stressed] can lead to laziness, complacency, and procrastination. Motivation is low. There’s a loss of engagement,” said Zimet. Think about the last time you spent an afternoon surfing YouTube videos. Did you feel good about yourself?

I prefer to think of YD’s 1908 study as a useful metaphor for psychological processes that are difficult to measure yet useful to understand. Teigen doesn’t just throw Yerkes and Dodson under the bus—he throws the entire field of academic psychology with them. And yet he does recognize that psychology isn’t hard science: “The vicissitudes of the YD law can be regarded as a demonstration of the mutability and fuzziness of … psychological concepts.”

The bottom line is that the YD law works. It’s a valuable tool. You’ll find it in most psychology textbooks, and practitioners like Zimet use it to coach world-class athletes.

People-specific YD curves

There’s another extension of the YD model that’s more important than the academic tergiversations on the 1908 study: Different people have different YD curves. (There you go, you won’t find this word in pop psychology articles: “tergiversation.”)

It’s not just that you need to keep your stress at a lower level for optimal performance at archery—picture the athlete taking deep breaths to lower their heart rate—and crank it up for weightlifting—picture a powerlifter sniffing ammonia before a lift to increase their heart rate—it’s also that different people have different optimal stress levels. Obvious!

This insight is important for leaders. You must adjust how you manage employees based on their stress tolerance. Organizations, too, have an optimal level of stress.

And, of course, you need to understand where your optimal functioning lies. Find the optimal RPM level on your engine.

Some people perform their best at the higher end of the stress curve, while others get overwhelmed quickly. Unless they seek and respond to treatment, many people with clinical anxiety struggle to handle even minimal stress. In clinical situations, extreme anxiety and panic attacks can be triggered by the body itself with minimal—if any—external stimulus.

At the other end of the spectrum, some people function optimally when they’re living on the edge: base jumpers, tightrope walkers, and the like. My brother-in-law is an expert rock climber, mountain biker, and kite surfer. When I went mountain biking with him, he was bored out of his mind. We were going way too slow on way-too-easy trails. In business, think about Elon Musk. He thrives by setting almost impossible goals for himself and his companies. He seems proud that Tesla almost went bankrupt a couple of times. And blowing up rockets at SpaceX seems to be how the company learns and improves.

Where does your optimal functioning lie on the YD curve? And as a leader, do you understand your organization’s curve? Does the team function better under a bit of pressure?

YD curves are also personalized in another way. It’s not just that people’s optimal stress RPM levels differ. The steepness of the curve, or peakedness, also differs across individuals.

I think I run my engine hot. On a scale of 1–10, I need to be at least at 7.5 to perform optimally. But I have a narrow band. I perform best within the 7.5–8.5 range. Below 7.5, I get underwhelmed. And when I’m at a 9, I need to take corrective action to bring my stress down.

My example illustrates how subjective, task-specific, and people-specific YD curves are. It doesn’t mean this model isn’t useful. It’s a framework to help you learn about yourself and calibrate your goals.

Organizational implications

The YD law is an excellent example of one of the themes of this book: Models in psychology that apply to you as a leader also apply to your organization.

In my division, I calibrate goals to be hard to reach, yet attainable.

We have simple, top-level goals for the division to rally around. These cover investment performance over various time horizons and business growth targets. For product development, we have established a three-year roadmap with a specific launch date for each new product. These goals dovetail with firm-wide goals. In turn, they cascade down to team-level and individual-level goals so that people can measure their contribution to the broader division-wide and firm-wide outcomes. Meaning is derived when you feel like you’re a part of something bigger than yourself. These are optimal goals because they encourage us to perform at the top of the organizational stress curve. It’s our optimal RPM. More aggressive goals would make our organization red line. Less ambitious goals would weaken our focus—and jeopardize our ability to realize the team’s full potential.

If I merely wanted to minimize organizational stress, per Corbett, maybe I’d say things like “Let’s do our best” and “Everyone gets a trophy.” I mentioned Pink’s and others’ work on intrinsic motivation. Intrinsic motivation is more powerful than extrinsic motivation. So, perhaps Pink would argue that if I got rid of these goals, our performance would increase because people would discover their intrinsic motivation.

I don’t think so. We pay for performance, and we keep score. It works. I reject the notion that extrinsic goals and rewards crowd out intrinsic motivation. Both can coexist and reinforce each other. As a leader, remember that both dimensions matter when you strive for your organization to perform at the top of its YD curve.

Takeaways to improve your mental game

Don’t assume all stress is bad. For optimal performance, you shouldn’t eliminate stress. That’s impossible. Instead, find your optimal stress level. As you calibrate your goals, don’t make them too easy or too hard. Remember that the more complex the work, the more you need to concentrate, and the lower the optimal stress level.

You want to operate at the top of the inverted U. That’s when you’re in flow, when the task’s difficulty matches your capabilities. Here’s Dr. Zimet, sports pscyhologist, describing how match-for-task goals lead to flow in his pro handball career:

It’s the optimal utilization of myself. It’s a great feeling. You want to take on a task that uses all of who you are. Handball requires every ounce of my ability. But you need the possibility of winning. Don’t ask the impossible; it leads to anxiety.

You can apply this idea to organizational stress. You should calibrate your team’s goals as a function of task difficulty. Set the bar high and give feedback such that people remain engaged and, dare I say, on their toes. But don’t overdo it—you don’t want people to burn out. It’s a balancing act. The challenge with operating at the top of the inverted U is that you’re never too far from the too much stress part of the curve.

For simple, repetitive tasks—think of an automobile assembly line—it’s okay to crank up the pressure with aggressive goals. For complex projects that require concentration, such as research and development, goals should be ambitious but perhaps more flexible. Don’t scream at your team to “innovate now!” or put undue pressure on them to solve complex problems. The extra stress is unlikely to help them perform better.

Before we leave the YD law topic, you may be wondering why the mice were called Japanese dancing mice. Did they dance? Apparently, they did, due to a hereditary ear defect. Yerkes wrote a book about them titled The Dancing Mouse.

---

1. [3](ThePsychologyofLeadership-ebook-050325-19.xhtml#footnote-002-backlink)

   I’ve substituted “stressed” and “stress,” respectively, for “aroused” and “arousal.”

Principle 11: Think About Death—Work Backwards from Your Goals

“Life can only be understood backwards.”

—Søren Kierkegaard, philosopher

To lift your existential fog, you must embrace long-term, meaningful goals. To do so, you must define happiness in personal and organizational terms. We saw that the PERMA model motivates such reflection. The YD curves help calibrate your goals for flow and optimal performance. Tools of scientific psychology apply to organizations as much as individuals—perhaps a lot more than people think.

Now, suppose you’ve set meaningful goals for yourself and your organization. You’ve calibrated them to be hard enough, but not too hard. You’re running the engine with the right fuel and at optimal RPM. What next?

Nothing will go as planned. Almost immediately after you turn to execution, you’ll deviate from the theoretical path toward goal achievement.

Suppose you want to lose weight at a rate of one pound per week. You may set a goal to lose 15 pounds in 15 weeks. You won’t lose precisely one pound per week for the next 15 weeks. Sometimes, things will go better than planned. More often, you’ll fall behind. We tend to underestimate the randomness and friction between the planning process and goal achievement. Someone at work will bring pizza for lunch. Later that week, you’ll be so bored at the cocktail reception that you won’t resist the fancy appetizers. Or, in my case, my kids will bring home some Chick-fil-A. Olivia will take a few bites and leave the rest on the table as if to test my willpower. Ninety-nine percent of the time, I’ll fail that test.

No matter your goal, you’ll need to course-correct along the way. Reality won’t match the plan you wrote down on paper. What’s the best way to handle the inevitable deviations? Principle 11, “Think About Death—Work Backwards from Your Goals,” introduces control theory as a framework to dynamically manage execution against a plan. In an unusual case of intellectual cross-fertilization, psychology borrowed control theory from science and engineering. My approach to it doesn’t require complex scenarios or computer-based optimization models. Leaders can use the model as a way of thinking—a rule of thumb.

Control theory to the rescue

Despite decades of research in the ivory tower, the applications of control theory in psychology—let alone the psychology of leadership—haven’t been popularized.

The literature on control theory is abstract and jargon-heavy. Here’s a sample from a paper by one of the fathers of the approach, Charles S. Carver, Professor of Psychology at the University of Miami:

We now suspect that an over-responsiveness to emotion may be an important transdiagnostic liability for psychopathology when combined with extremity on another of the core traits that likely yield frequent emotions to respond to (e.g., approach or avoidance temperament).

Huh? I suppose this quote says that if you’re the type of person who overreacts to emotions, and you have other extreme personality traits that make you emotional, you’re more likely to suffer from mental illnesses (you have “transdiagnostic liability for psychopathology”). Makes sense: If you’re quick to act on your emotions and are very emotional, you’re probably not in the best mental shape.

Let me take this abstract academic research and make it simple and practical for you.

First, let’s start with the basics. As mentioned, psychology borrowed control theory from engineering. You read that right: psychology and engineering! Here’s the simplest definition I can think of: Control theory is a framework to handle dynamic feedback loops. Think of it as a system or an algorithm. In addition to engineering and psychology, a wide range of scientific fields use control theory, including mathematics, life sciences, operations research, and computer science. It’s a powerful framework.

Using our 15 lbs. weight loss example, here are the building blocks of the model:

* A target state (your goal): You want to lose 15 lbs. at a rate of 1 lb. per week.
* A measurement device: Your scale.
* Discrepancies: Deviations from losing 1 lb. per week.

Control theory tells you how to handle the discrepancies. It anticipates the effect of your corrective measures (“self-regulation”) on progress toward your goal. It’s a dynamic system because the decisions you make now will have an impact on future observations, and future decisions.

If your weight falls below the -1 lbs. per week line, you may start eating more. If your weight trends above the line, you’ll want to reduce calories. Simple enough.

But let’s say you need to cut back. The question is, by how much? Should you cut 1,000 calories today? Or try to cut 200 calories per day for the next five days? How will fluctuations in your weight over the next few days influence this course of action? Or, if you need to eat more because the scale tells you you’re at risk of crash-dieting, should you immediately eat an extra 2,000 calories that same day? Probably not. If you do this, you’ll overshoot. (Been there, done that. Repeatedly!)

The scale gives you a feedback loop. And your actions influence the outcome. There’s also noise in the system—variables you can’t control. It’s impossible to keep your hydration level the same every day when you step on the scale. It depends on the weather, for example. And you’d be surprised how much hydration impacts your body weight.

Control theory also deals with complex hierarchies of goals. To understand hierarchies of goals, it helps to act like a five-year-old, i.e., just keep asking “Why?” in an obnoxious way. Why do you want to lose weight? Maybe you want to feel more attractive. Why? You want to get back on the dating scene. Why? You want to find someone to spend your life with. Why? You want to be happy. After all, the Harvard Study on happiness predicts that the R (relationship) in the PERMA model is the most important predictor of lifelong well-being.

The cruise control on your car illustrates a feedback loop in control theory. Your goal is to maintain a target speed. The car has a sensor that measures deviations from this target speed. If you’re going up a hill and the car slows down, the sensor tells the engine to work harder. If you’re going down a hill and the car accelerates above the target, it tells the engine to throttle back. The phrase “negative feedback loop” is often used in control theory because the algorithm is focused on minimizing this negative feedback that you’re deviating from the target.

Let’s think of the hierarchy of goals in the car example. The person driving the car wants to get somewhere. That’s a higher-order goal. They may want to get somewhere to spend time with family—an even higher-order goal. You can keep asking them “Why?” like a five-year-old to reveal all the layers of the goals-hierarchy onion.

Control theory and your personal goals

How can you use control theory to execute your goals? How can this model improve your leadership? You won’t need complicated mathematics. As for the YD law, you can use control theory as a mental model. The three most important rules are the following:

1. Start from the endpoint. Work backward from your big goal to develop plans, milestones, and performance indicators.
2. Optimize the path. Set targets to be difficult yet achievable. Manage expectations and build a plan that will allow you or your organization to make progress at a rate that maximizes engagement.
3. Don’t anchor decisions on past information. When results deviate from your plan, adjust execution based on forward-looking analysis (again, working backward from your endpoint) rather than what has happened so far.

Let’s start with your personal goals and the power of expectations. A theme of this book is that you should set long-term goals. Big goals. Otherwise, you’ll stay in a mental fog, unsure of where you’re headed.

Yet, reaching goals doesn’t necessarily make you happy. Soon enough, you’ll want something more. Suppose that five years ago, you dreamt of owning a fancy car. You’ve finally bought it. After a few months, you realize… it’s just a car, and maybe you’d be happier with the fancier model. Hedonic adaption is like carbon monoxide for the psyche. It’s the silent killer of gratitude.

But there’s more to it than gratitude. Even if you have the right goals, focused on meaning and intrinsic motivation, you will want to progress towards them at a rate that matches or surpasses your expectations. Expectations are significant in psychology. Take the New England Patriots. If they don’t win the Super Bowl, it’s a bad season!

At some point in the early 2000s, I wasn’t very happy at work. My career wasn’t advancing fast enough. I was complaining to my mentor, Mark Kritzman, when he said something I’ll never forget. He started by asking: “Seb, do you know the secret to happiness in life?”

Before I tell you about what he said next, I should say that Mark has a flair for dry humor, of the cynical kind (in a good way).

Here’s what he said: “Lower your expectations.”

You can interpret this advice in at least two ways. Perhaps it’s a statement about how life always disappoints. Or it’s a Stoic statement about people setting their expectations too high. Maybe it’s both. Happiness is not about reaching goals. It’s the journey. And here’s the good news: Centuries of exploration in psychology and philosophy suggest that you have control over your expectations.

Years later, I reminisced on this conversation with Mark over a glass of wine. He remembers it differently. He said he wasn’t advising me on my career but on marriage. When he said, “Lower your expectations,” I had just gotten engaged. For the record, Anne and I have been together for over 20 years, and we have an incredibly happy marriage. And Mark has been happily married for several decades as well!

The models in scientific psychology are all connected. Engagement (from PERMA), flow, and the YD law tell us to find the sweet spot between setting our goals either too low or too high. One way to interpret the use of control theory in psychology is that when it comes to execution, the rate of progress also matters.

You can and should still set stretched goals. Elon Musk manages to mobilize organizations towards almost-impossible goals. How? Isn’t he setting the bar too high?

The path matters. Musk and his organizations are making enough progress toward their goals to keep everyone engaged. Big, crazy goals are okay, even motivating, if you hit milestones along the way. Besides, the higher you set the bar, the more you may achieve, even if you miss the big goal.

“Shoot for the moon. Even if you miss, you’ll land among the stars,” positive-thinking guru Norman Vincent Peale said.

But remember that when you set a stretched goal, you create high expectations. If you shoot for the moon and proceed to make zero progress, you’ll lose engagement. The why bother mentality will infect your organization and, ultimately, your own mind. As the great philosopher Jimmy Fallon said, “Don’t keep reaching for the stars because you’ll just look like an idiot stretching that way for no reason.”

A personal case study

When I joined T. Rowe Price, I took a career risk. I was hired as a new leader from the outside to manage a team of experienced high performers. Setting a big goal and managing execution towards it based on the principles of control theory helped me establish leadership credibility and move our business forward.

Our core business, offering Target Date Funds in the U.S. retirement space, was thriving. Senior team members had all been at the firm for at least a decade, with some having accumulated over 20 years in their roles. You can imagine the challenge. How was I to take this organization to the next level? How would I establish credibility with the team? Imagine you sign up to coach a sports team after they’ve won several championships in a row. The bar was high. Thankfully, my predecessor, Rich Whitney, stayed on board to manage the division with me (as Co-Head) for a transition period before his retirement. I don’t think I would have succeeded without him.

The threats to our market leadership in Target Date Funds were mounting. We urgently needed to innovate and diversify while protecting our core business. It’s a typical strategic challenge for corporations: How do you simultaneously defend the core and innovate? With support from Rich, the firm’s Management Committee, sales leaders, and an external consultant, I formulated a three-pillar strategic plan. We asked the firm to make a significant investment to:

1. Maintain our leadership in U.S. Retirement (Target Date Funds).
2. Expand globally, with a focus on Europe and Asia-Pacific.
3. Scale our Solutions capabilities through advice and customization.

The team rallied behind this plan, which included a roadmap of product development milestones and metrics (key performance indicators) to measure progress. It was enthusiastically approved as part of a broader, firm-wide strategic plan that our CEO was sponsoring. Over the next few years, my team leaders and I embarked on a hiring spree, growing the division in Baltimore, London, Tokyo, Singapore, and Hong Kong.

There was a critical moment in the early days of planning when I set a big goal. I was in a meeting with my leadership team when someone asked: “What do you expect our assets under management to be five years from now?”

I hadn’t done the math—we were still working on the projections. It was a tricky question because we were looking at a successful but mature business, as well as new products and markets. It was hard to estimate the threats to our core business, but they were real—we were (and remain) under constant attack from index-matching providers. As for new products and markets, the projections were even fuzzier, given the risk involved in launching new capabilities. On top of all these complexities, answering the question required an estimate of market returns. That’s the beauty—and the curse—of money management: Your revenues largely depend on the returns of the stock and bond markets.

I’m not necessarily proud of how I answered the question. In hindsight, I should have explained the complexities of such a forecast and asked the team for their input. But I was still new at the firm, and through an odd combination of insecurity and bravado, I gave a very ambitious target.

There was silence in the room. Big goal. I wanted us to stop thinking of our business as mature.

Together with the planning team, we backed out year-by-year growth projections from this big goal. We didn’t use control theory explicitly, but we did rely on its central tenet: We started from the endpoint and worked backward to set an optimal path. The target annual rate of progress was calibrated accordingly.

Years later, after ups and downs, we reached our target. We started with high expectations. We made progress along the way at a rate that kept the whole team engaged. And let’s not forget that the market gods (or Central Banks, depending on your persuasion) smiled upon capital markets.

The importance of working backward

Control theory solves multistage problems, such as managing execution towards a big goal. The term “multistage” means that you make multiple decisions over time.
