These decisions may have a type of chain-reaction effect. Chess is an illustration of a multistage decision process. When you play chess, you must think several moves ahead, working forward and backward, through a series of if-then scenarios.

I first encountered control theory in finance in the early 2000s when we investigated the optimal rebalancing problem. It’s a simple issue that affects every investor’s portfolio, but for which money managers haven’t found a good answer. Let me describe this geeky finance problem to you. Then, we’ll extend it to a similar mental leadership model.

Suppose you want to allocate 60% of your savings to stocks and 40% to bonds. Now, suppose stock markets rally. If you don’t do anything, your stock allocation will drift higher as the value of your stocks starts to outpace the value of your bonds. Over time, you may end up with a more aggressive portfolio than you intended, allocated to, say, 70% in stocks. Your asset mix can also drift the other way: Your stock allocation will decrease if markets crash. Market returns cause undesirable deviations from your target risk level.

You can buy and sell stocks and bonds to bring your asset mix back to target. However, there are costs (and often taxes) involved in these transactions. The multistage problem is to minimize the sum of the deviations from your target mix and transaction costs. You can rebalance often, which will help you keep your asset mix within a tight band, but it will be expensive. Or you can let the portfolio drift, potentially accumulating large deviations from your target. In all cases, your rebalancing decisions will impact future deviations and, therefore, future rebalancing decisions.

There’s an analogy to managing long-term relationships. If you give constant negative feedback on every little thing that irritates you about your spouse or work partner, you’re correcting minor issues, but it’s expensive in terms of the quality of the relationship. Soon enough, you’ll hear that you’re being overbearing. However, making small and frequent corrections (talking through a difference of opinion) can be less painful than infrequent but large corrections (a fight). You can’t let issues fester too long. Either way, your decision to address or not to address a problem now will impact future issues and decisions. Relationships are a multistage process.

For our finance problem, to optimize the rebalancing rules, we relied on massive computer simulations that pushed the limits of our firm’s grid computing capabilities and a mathematical technique called Dynamic Programming, which is a powerful model used in control theory to solve recursive problems.

What does this mean for leadership of large projects?

When you set a long-term goal, you’re dealing with a multistage decision problem. Along the way, you’ll face deviations from your idealized execution roadmap. Your decisions to rebalance or not to rebalance, to stay the course, or to re-baseline the project will impact future decisions and actions. Control theory, as applied in various fields, says you shouldn’t make these course-correction decisions myopically. Instead, you must start from the end goal, work backward through a wide range of scenarios and decisions, and solve for the optimal course of action.

When you face a major setback in a multi-year product development project, instead of trying to fix the immediate problem, take a step back and ask the following three questions:

1. When do we want to launch the product? By asking this question, you frame the discussion with the end goal in mind instead of the current issue that needs to be fixed.
2. What needs to happen between now and the launch date? Work backward from the launch date through each milestone, step by step. Recognize that there’s uncertainty at each step.
3. What should be our response to today’s setback? Most discussions start here, but control theory says you must start with questions 1 and 2 to optimize today’s decision.

These three steps may lead you to erase the previous plan and start clean.

Suppose you’ve been put in charge of a large cross-enterprise project. You’ve built a detailed road map, including charts showing team dependencies. You’ve asked every team leader to give conservative timelines to create positive momentum with a few early wins.

You’re on top of things. You convene the steering committee weekly. Some of the teams meet daily. You take a hands-on role and tell team leaders you are available anytime to help resolve bottlenecks.

Yet, the teams are slipping on execution. One day, as you review a status report, you realize that you’re not just missing a few milestones; the project is falling behind on all your milestones!

You tell the teams to push harder and communicate better. You roll up your sleeves and try to fix the bottlenecks.

It doesn’t help. Everyone’s already doing their best. Frustrations are rising.

That’s when you take the control theory approach of working backward. The discussion moves from resolving issues one by one to asking questions about why the projections were almost all wrong and whether you need to re-baseline the project with a new launch date.

The first two of the three questions I outlined above—when to launch and what it will take to get there—forces you to look at the project from the top down. Hedge fund manager Ray Dalio would call this process “practicing higher-level thinking and looking down at the machine.”

What’s wrong with the machine? Why is it producing disappointing outcomes?

Perhaps the problem with the machine has to do with team dynamics.

The complexity of a large project can make it difficult to resolve issues quickly. Often, too many layers of well-intentioned project managers and administrators are involved. Don’t get me wrong: You need these roles in large organizations. If you build a team comprised strictly of individual contributors and subject matter experts (SMEs), things start to break above eight to ten people—especially when more than one team is involved in the broader project. Jeff Bezos recommends that no meeting be so large that you must order more than two pizzas for lunch.

SMEs like to keep their heads down and get stuff done, especially those who write computer code, which requires uninterrupted, deep work. To protect their time, project managers and administrators attend meetings and coordinate the work. The issue with this model is that project managers and administrators can’t answer technical questions. So, the telephone game begins. A project manager may ask the technical question to their counterpart in another department, who in turn may escalate the question to their boss… who finally will gently knock on the door of the SME to ask: “Excuse me for interrupting, but which type of salary user data do you want to use?”

After a sigh of exasperation at having to stop coding while solving a complicated equation, the SME will likely ask for clarification, starting with: “Huh? There’s more than one?”

Then, the telephone game starts in reverse, going through the same communication chain in the other direction.

And this is an optimistic scenario. Playing the telephone game not only slows decision-making, but by the time the question gets to the SME, it’s likely to be distorted and misrepresented. What’s worse, the more intermediaries are involved in communicating contentious issues, the more tensions rise. I think that’s the whole premise for reality TV shows like The Real Housewives of… She said what?! He did what?! Getting all riled up in your conversation with the messenger is easier than having a less comfortable conversation with the source. Yet the less comfortable conversation is the one you need to have with the source (after you calm down), especially if the messenger tends to indulge, or even egg on, your frustrations.

Sometimes, I forget this basic principle. My General Manager often brings bad news to me. It’s part of his job—he watches my back. In high-stress situations, I have occasionally expressed my frustrations during our weekly catch-up meetings, or worse, over email. I’m thankful he’s an unflappable corporate veteran with a thick skin.

The takeaway is that the goal of protecting the SMEs’ time comes with a giant side effect of inefficient communication. But you can’t ask your SMEs to spend all their time in meetings because… who will do the work? Every organization must deal with some version of this conundrum. There’s no easy solution.

Back to your cross-enterprise project. After diagnosing what was wrong with the machine, you devise a remediation plan. For example, you could hire an Agile Coach. The Agile model is an iterative project management approach. It provides a framework for SMEs and stakeholders to collaborate in real-time. The whole point of Agile is to avoid the telephone game. Agile often goes all the way to keeping the end client in the loop with intermediary prototypes. Build fast, fail fast, improve fast. One of the key features of the approach is to hold short, daily scrum or stand-up meetings. When the right people are in the room, these meetings improve communication significantly.

And you could designate leads amongst the key SMEs and stakeholders to be part of the Agile team. In doing so, you prune the number of project managers and administrators involved. Your goal is to collapse departmental walls and blow-torch all the red tape you can find. It helps to create a virtual team that cuts across different parts of the enterprise, such as research, technology, and sales. Your Agile team should look and act like a start-up, even if it operates inside a large company.

Don’t let the process become the thing

In the early 2000s, when I worked at State Street, our Global Markets division acquired a small fintech. The CEO of the acquired start-up was taken aback by the complexity of our big company of over 20,000 employees. So many different administrative jobs are involved in making a big machine work. To illustrate the agility of a smaller, less bureaucratic company, he explained that everyone at his company reported directly to him. He had over 100 direct reports. He added: “At my company, you’re either coding or you’re selling.”

It’s not a bad model when you think about it. However, when companies scale, their machine requires more components. It’s unavoidable. A way to mitigate this issue is to create small virtual start-ups inside the big company. In doing so, you must allow for risk-taking and its corollary, failure. Jeff Bezos calls this the “Day 1” model.

With all the pontificating about planning and execution inside every corporation (and in this book), it’s helpful to remember that one of the largest and most successful companies in the world learns by trial and error. From Bezos: “Staying in Day 1 requires you to experiment patiently, accept failures, plant seeds, protect saplings, and double down when you see customer delight.”

Agile fosters a Day 1 culture. I love this quote, also from Bezos: “If you’re not watchful, the process can become the thing… You stop looking at outcomes and just make sure you’re doing the process right. The process is not the thing. It’s always worth asking, do we own the process or does the process own us? In a Day 2 company, you might find it’s the second.”

You become myopic when the process—or the HOW—becomes the thing. You push through your timeline, fixing bugs and addressing delays as they surface. You don’t question the process because the process owns you. In contrast, the control theory model forces you to examine the outcomes. Are you losing weight? Is the rocket heading toward the moon?

Following the control theory principle of starting from the endpoint, you may realize that the machine produces unsatisfying results despite everyone’s best efforts. As a leader, you’re responsible for the machine.

The last decision in your review is the most important. Should you re-baseline the project? To re-baseline means pushing the go-to-market date forward, say by six months or more. Again, working backward from the endpoint, you may realize that you won’t meet your target launch date even if you solve most of your issues through better project management.

What will you tell your higher-ups? Over time, I’ve learned that senior managers want bad news to travel fast. They also want to hear solutions, not just problems. If you follow both dictums, you’ll earn the respect and support of your colleagues, no matter how bad the news is.

This case study illustrates how to use the three steps I outlined above to address execution setbacks via our control theory model. You must plan backward from the endpoint. When your project looks like a train wreck, don’t focus on what has happened and is no longer relevant. Look forward. Be prepared to cut your losses. Hit the reset button and re-baseline.

Two additional takeaways apply to project management in general: Keep teams small and avoid the telephone game. In other words, blow-torch the red tape. This is not as easy as it sounds in large companies. Here’s my advice: 1) create a virtual team that removes traditional reporting lines and departmental silos; 2) give this team a common goal; and 3) implement the Agile method.

Planning in reverse to build resilience

To plan in reverse is such a broad and powerful concept. It goes beyond this project management example. I mentioned earlier that Stoic philosophers thought a lot about death. You’re going to die one day. That’s your endpoint. Working backward from there forces you to think about our earlier discussion on meaning, the M in the PERMA model, and the old man sitting in a chair reflecting on his life and thinking it was time well spent.

Perhaps the best leadership application of this model is Stephen Covey’s Habit 2: “Begin with the End in Mind.” You know about Stephen Covey, right?

He was one of the most famous American self-help gurus. As of 2021, his book The 7 Habits of Highly Effective People has sold over 40 million copies. He died in 2012, but his seven habits have become the business equivalent of the Bible’s Ten Commandments. They’re still taught in leadership seminars worldwide.

If you haven’t read the book, you should—but after you finish this book and give it a 5-star rating on Amazon. When he explains the begin with the end in mind habit, Covey also invites you to think about the end of your life: “The most fundamental application of [this habit] is to begin today with the image, picture, or paradigm of the end of your life as you frame the reference or the criterion by which everything else is examined.”

I found his explanation of the benefit of thinking this way particularly convincing: “How different our lives are when we really know what is deeply important, to us, and, keeping that picture in mind, we manage ourselves each day to do what really matters most.”

In other words, it clears the mental fog most of us are walking in.

And it gives perspective on the day-to-day issues you encounter. You ask yourself: Does this issue matter to what is deeply important? Asking myself this question has helped me manage stress. When someone says something negative to you, is it worth a large allocation of your mental energy?

Takeaways to improve your mental game

When reality deviates from your plans, consider whether the machine is broken. Don’t let the process become the thing.

Next, take a step back and work backward:

* Frame the discussion with the end goal in mind.
* Ask what needs to happen between now and the end goal, given your new reality.
* Work backward from the launch date through each milestone, step by step. Recognize that each step is uncertain.
* Then, and only then, choose how to respond to today’s setback.

When things get tough, people look to you as a leader to remind the organization of a brighter long-term outlook. And they want you to tell them how the organization will get there. You may have to revise the plan. That’s okay. It’s much better to re-baseline than to hold on to a plan that has become unrealistic.

You can apply this control theory model to your personal setbacks as well. Doing so comes back to a central theme of the psychology of leadership: When you look at the endpoint—perhaps all the way to your deathbed—you become less reactive to day-to-day issues.

One more thing: Should we shoot for the moon to land amongst the stars? Last time I checked, the moon was closer than the stars. I’m just saying.

Principle 12: Sit on It—Learn Strategic Patience

“Never make a decision until you have to.”

—Randy Pausch, author, The Last Lecture

I once worked with a remarkably patient and wise business leader. If someone came to him with an “urgent” problem, he would evaluate whether it was critical and time-sensitive. When needed, he was comfortable taking decisive action. But otherwise, he would take advantage of the option to wait and see how things unfolded. Most of the time, the issues were resolved without his involvement.

Another takeaway from control theory is that if you have a long window to decide, you should keep your options open. Principle 12, “Sit on It—Learn Strategic Patience,” may seem obvious, but it’s constantly violated in business. It speaks to the psychology of decision-making under uncertainty. Most organizations suffer from a false sense of urgency and a bias towards action. Of course, leadership often requires quick decisions. However, sometimes the best decision is to wait and see what happens. I call this “strategic patience.”

Take advantage of the time you have left

In his book Originals, psychologist and famous author Adam Grant praises procrastination. He explains that “leaders who drive change effectively are those who wait patiently for the right moment.” Regarding product launches, he quotes a study by Peter Golder and Gerard Tellis about pioneers (those who are first to market) and settlers (followers who wait for pioneers to create a market and follow with a better product). Golder and Tellis found that pioneers were about six times more likely to fail than settlers.

You can use dynamic programming—the technique used in control theory I mentioned earlier—to model how patient you should be in a given multistage decision. When I worked on applications of dynamic programming in finance, I came across a hypothetical example: deciding when to get married.

Suppose your parents give you ten years to get married, after which they’ll force you to marry the person you’re dating at the time. You rank potential mates on a scale of 1 to 100. The average candidate scores 50, and the population is evenly distributed. To simplify, let’s assume you meet one candidate per year.

Ready? Go!

Year 1, you meet someone you rather like. They’re a 60 out of 100. You should marry that person since they’re above average, right? On the other hand, if you’re patient, you can evaluate as many as nine more candidates. Maybe you could meet a 100 out there (my beautiful, intelligent, funny, and kind wife, Anne, is clearly a 100). To solve for the optimal decision rule, you need to start from, you guessed it, the endpoint.

In year 10, the expected score of the person you’ll meet will be 50. As mentioned, that’s the average of the population. Therefore, in year 9, you shouldn’t settle for anyone below 50. Do you agree? Say you meet a 39 in year 9. Why not take one more chance at meeting a 50? (I’m assuming you’re not very averse to risk. It simplifies the problem.)

Your decision in year 8 gets more complicated because you have two more chances to meet a 50 before your parents’ deadline. Therefore, your threshold should probably be a little higher than 50. Should you settle for a 51 in year 8? In other words, is a 51 better or worse than two more dice rolls?

Using the same logic, your quality threshold should increase as you work backward from year 7 to now. The more chances you have left, the pickier you should be.

It’s easy to solve this problem mathematically. The solution is shown in the figure.

Solution to the marriage problem: Minimum score from years 1 to 10—the Index of Desperation

![](image/MarriageProblem.png)

The line is your decision rule. In any given year except year 10, you should marry only if the person you meet ranks above that line. The line decreases with time. The formula is derived from simple probability calculations assuming the population is equally weighted between 1 and 100.

When I used this example to introduce dynamic programming in quantitative finance presentations, I would refer to the line as the Index of Desperation, which would earn me a few polite chuckles. As time passes, you must lower your standards. Very intuitive. I would add that “transaction costs are not included in the model, but are estimated at 50% of your wealth” in a reference to divorce. I sometimes got a few genuine laughs out of that one. Hilarious, I know.

There are many similar multistage decisions in life and the business world. For example, buying a house or finding a new job. Here’s a simple one: You’re walking around a new neighborhood looking for a restaurant. You want to choose one before you get too hungry, say within an hour. If you’ve just started looking, it would take a really good restaurant to get you to settle. An hour later, you’ll probably settle for any restaurant that’s not Chuck-E-Cheese (better go hungry than suffer the Chuck-E-Cheese mayhem). In business, you may be looking to acquire a company within three years or want to fill an open position with the most qualified candidate within a specific time frame.

In all cases, we intuitively know that our standards should go down as time passes. That’s not the problem. The most common error in judgment with multistage decisions is that most of us don’t take advantage of the time we have left. Our standards go down too quickly. We want to get it done.

Often, issues get resolved before you need to make a tough decision. If you’re patient, problems have a way of working themselves out over time.

There’s a third advantage to strategic patience concerning the psychology of decision-making. I’m still working on it: In meetings with your team, if you don’t express your opinion right away, you get to hear what others think. Thanks to their unbiased input, you can make a wiser decision. Otherwise, once you tell your team where you stand, you’ve made it harder for them to disagree.

I’ve tried, but I still haven’t achieved a high level of strategic patience. I continue to make some decisions too quickly, and I often volunteer my point of view too early in deliberations with my steering committee.

There are plenty of instances when leaders need to make quick decisions. The key is to know when you should take decisive action and when you shouldn’t. Take some time, as appropriate, to assess each situation that requires you to make a high-stake decision through that lens. It helps to insert a cooling-off period between the stimulus and your reaction. Ask yourself, do I need to make this decision now?

Managing your emotions

You’ll find that to exercise strategic patience, you must learn to manage your emotions. We saw that some people are biologically predisposed to experience fewer positive emotions (P) than the average person. You’ll recall that Seligman suggests you can move up the distribution about 10–15% with some techniques like practicing gratitude daily.

The best thing you can do in the long run is to focus on ERMA—engagement, relationships, meaning, and accomplishment. If your mind is set on long-term, meaningful goals, you’re less likely to let emotions drive your need to rush decisions. You’ll remember what’s truly important, and you’ll be prepared to assume the discomfort and stress of waiting it out.

What are the techniques I’ve discovered to improve emotional resilience on the margin? Meditation helps. It teaches you to observe your thinking patterns. That’s easier said than done. It’s a paradox that has occupied philosophers for thousands of years. How do you use your mind to observe… your mind? Here are some tricks:

* Insert a mini-pause between the stimulus (someone said something mean about you, or your socks get wet) and your reaction (you question your entire self-worth or fly off the handle).
* Use the mini-pause to ask yourself, is this worth a strong reaction?
* Recognize when you’re overreacting.
* Before you try to fix the problem, dispassionately tell yourself, “I’m overacting.” Just observe.

My son Charlie’s friend used to suffer from panic attacks. Once, I took Charlie and him snowboarding. They were both 12 years old. It was his friend’s first time snowboarding, minus a couple of perfunctory lessons. After he found himself stuck on the side of the slope for about the tenth time, I noticed that he was about to lose it. The tears, the hyperventilation, were about one second away. I had seen him have panic attacks before. Then, something remarkable happened. He said: “I’m so frustrated right now!”

Immediately, his mind went the other way. He started problem-solving. I was glad he had developed the habit of observing his thinking patterns. I suspect he was coached to do this by a mental health professional. When we finally got to the bottom of the slope, his clothes were drenched in melting snow. He was exhausted, shivering, and in a fair amount of pain. What did he do next? He insisted on going back up the mountain. His goal was to learn to snowboard. Everything else was noise. This kid will go far in life.

Lastly, for the tritest piece of advice of this entire book: Take a deep breath. That’s overused, cliché advice, right? I used to think so until I read James Nestor’s book Breath, in which he explains the science of the connection between breathing and biological functions like blood pressure and heart rate. Nestor explains that how you breathe impacts your brain activity and your mind.

Dr. Zimet points out that almost all pro athletes use breathing techniques. It’s the only physical pathway between what we can’t control (heart rate, sweating, blood pressure) and what we can control (air intake). Swimmer Michael Phelps, the most decorated Olympian of all time, has struggled with anxiety and depression. He credits breathing techniques—outside the pool—as part of a mental health routine that saved his life. And if you’re a basketball fan, here’s a riddle: What do Michael Jordan, Shaquille O’Neal, and Kobe Bryant have in common? They all use, or used, meditation and breathing techniques learned from the same meditation coach, George Mumford. They credit Mumford for improving their game.

We’ve covered the concept of flow at length in this book. In an ABC News interview, Mumford recommends breathing techniques to get into a state of flow: “First, I talk about being a warrior, like a samurai… but also about ‘the zone.’ They know what ‘the zone’ is, they know what ‘being in flow’ is, so when I talk about that, they’re all ears.”

If I were prone to hyperbole, I’d say that meditation and breathing techniques are like performance-enhancing drugs, except legal and without side effects. There’s also a rumor going around that breathing is a critical part of remaining alive.

Takeaways to improve your mental game

Control theory teaches us to use time to our advantage to keep our options open. I call it strategic patience. Don’t rush decisions when you don’t have to. When you need to choose amongst competing options—for example, when hiring someone—your standards should be a function of how much time you have left to decide. Remember that most of us have a bias to action. We underestimate how high our standards should be when there’s more time. When pressed to make a decision that’s not urgent, learn to wait. Sometimes, issues will resolve themselves without your involvement.

Also, when it comes to group decision-making, let your team speak first. Don’t bias the discussions by stating your opinion too early.

Lastly, to help with strategic patience and many other aspects of your life, I encourage you to learn meditation, pay attention to your state of mind, and practice breathing techniques. Remember that these mental tricks work best if you practice them daily.

You read that right: I’m asking you to take a deep breath when you face stressful situations. Groundbreaking advice, I know!

Principle 13: Separate Signal from Noise—Focus on What Matters

“The signal is the truth. The noise is what distracts us from the truth.”

—Nate Silver, author and statistician

Money managers are keenly aware of the need to separate signal from noise. For example, we can’t sell stocks whenever economic news is bad. We need the discipline to determine whether the new information is already reflected in prices. Maybe we should buy stocks when the news is bad because the market has overreacted. Even harder, we need to determine which of the millions of data sources, newsfeeds, and research papers may have relevance to our investment decisions.

That’s essentially the job definition of a portfolio manager: separate signal from noise. And believe me, in the investment world, there’s a lot more noise than signal. Did I mention managing money is hard?

In addition to planning backward and exercising strategic patience, control theory teaches us to separate signal from noise. As a leader, what issues need your attention (signal), and which ones should you ignore (noise)?

This is the focus of Principle 13, “Separate Signal from Noise—Focus on What Matters.”

Do not overcorrect

Here’s a mental map of our journey so far:

Meaningful Goals ![](image/2.png) Execution Plan (Working Backward) ![](image/2.png) Noisy Outcomes ![](image/2.png) What Now? (You Are Here)

When dealing with the noisy outcomes against your plan, you must answer several questions:

* How often should you measure progress?
* How should you calibrate your response?
* When should you take corrective action, and when should you let things play out?

Do you remember when you learned to drive? Within the first few minutes behind the wheel, the most important thing a new driver needs to learn is not to overcorrect. If the car goes off course, you don’t want to steer too hard the other way.

Going back to scientific psychology, earlier I quoted a paper on control theory by Charles S. Carver, Professor of Psychology at the University of Miami. In the same paper, titled “Control Theory, Goal Attainment, and Psychopathology,” Carver explains that separating signal from noise is fraught with psychological biases that make it hard to measure your performance relative to your goal. He gives four examples:

1. Your perception is different from everyone else’s. “You think Susie is attracted to you, but it is clear to everyone else that she is not.”
2. Your perception is divorced from reality. “The gas gauge says ‘empty,’ and you perceive yourself as a mile away from the gas station that is really 10 miles away.”
3. You use the wrong signal. “Having the boss smile at you when passing in the hallway does not necessarily inform you about his opinion of your work.”
4. You’re ignoring the present situation. “When an anger-prone person fails to realize he is displaying anger.”

Carver also describes errors in “calibration.” He warns that perfectionism can become problematic if you stick too closely to your optimal execution path. He warns that too much laissez-faire is also problematic—you must recognize when you’re going off track. Again: signal vs. noise.

Frequent adjustments vs. strategic patience

As a sports psychologist, Dr. Zimet thinks that the best way to apply control theory to athletic goal attainment is with frequent measurements and adjustments. Zimet says that “the most successful athletes” measure their performance relative to their goals regularly, which enables them to “make quick, frequent, small course corrections. This requires self-awareness. Less energy is required to make small adjustments over time than to let things run amok and make a major adjustment.”

But wait, you might say, am I not giving you advice that’s the opposite of the strategic patience I mentioned a few pages ago? And what about Carver’s warning about perfectionism?

First, signals come in different forms. Some arrive at a high frequency and should be dealt with quickly, while others require more information and more time to process.

Second, strategic patience differentiates between urgent and non-urgent decisions. You shouldn’t ignore urgent short-term signals. As for perfectionism, it’s a fair warning, but to Zimet’s point, seeking constant feedback and adjusting along the way is not about overreacting. Rather, it’s about small adjustments for small signals.

None of this advice about frequent adjustments invalidates the key takeaway that you should ignore anything that’s not a true signal. You can’t spend your life making adjustments.

In what follows, I apply control theory to career management, but remember that the same advice applies to managing an organization. Your career is your small enterprise, with its revenues (your salary), costs (what you spend, and what you give up to climb the ladder), stakeholders (your employer, your family, your community), and market value (what you can earn elsewhere). Your current employer is a stakeholder. They don’t own your career—you do! If you’re an entrepreneur, I’m sure this idea comes naturally to you.

Also, as a leader, an important part of your job is to help others progress in their careers. As you climb the ladder, people in your organization will look for you to give them career advice. I will give you some of the best advice I’ve ever received and explain how it relates to psychology and control theory.

In the early years of my finance career, I constantly overreacted to noise, which came in all forms. I would get worried about a negative comment from a superior, a bad meeting with a client, a small trade error, a bad day in the markets for our portfolios, and many more such normal setbacks. In many cases, I was suffering from the bias Carver calls “using the wrong signal.” And there was an asymmetry: I would sweat the minor negative setbacks, but wouldn’t overreact to noise on the upside, such as getting too excited about a good day in the markets. That’s my personality and perhaps human nature.

I also overreacted to bigger issues. Think: Losing a large mandate from a client or not getting a promotion. A couple of decades later, I can say most of these seemingly more significant issues were also noise around an upward-moving career trajectory. It can be tough to distinguish signal from noise in real time; hindsight makes it 20/20.

The easiest way to separate signal from noise is to take a long-term perspective. Frequent measurements of performance against goals and adjustments are useful, but only if you’re also able to put them in the context of the long-term trend before you make major decisions. Here are three questions that may help:

1. Where was your career five years ago?
2. Where will you be five years from now?
3. Does today’s setback change anything to that trajectory?

I’m referring to your personal goals here, but you can apply the same questions to managing your organization toward long-term goals.

If you take this approach to the extreme for your career, and like a Stoic, you put today’s problems in the context of your lifetime and eventual death, you’ll wash away the noise. You’ll end up with pure signal—the things that really matter. Thank you, Stephen Covey. In most cases, you’ll realize that you’re in a good spot and the trend is positive. Of course, the signal can be negative, in which case you’ll know it’s time for action, such as a career change.

Another way to separate signal from noise is to talk to a mentor. Someone with experience and perspective. When emotions run high, it’s easier for someone who’s not involved to provide a clinical assessment of your challenges. This person is less likely to suffer from the psychological biases that Carver mentions.

However, you must work with a mentor who knows what they’re talking about. Even better, work with someone who has power in your organization. Mentors and mentees often begin with good intentions but fail to make a connection because the mentor is too removed or not informed enough on the issues the mentee is struggling with. It’s counterintuitive, but I’ve noticed that the more formal the mentorship program, the less likely it is to succeed.

Let’s keep it simple: Find someone you can talk to about your challenges. And don’t think you’re too senior, smart, or experienced to work with a mentor. You’re not. Some of the best CEOs in the world hire professional coaches or ask board members to mentor them. Besides, all the top athletes in the world use coaches.

For the first ten years of my career, I was lucky to have the perfect mentor in Mark Kritzman. I’ve mentioned him a few times—he’s a Founding Partner of State Street Associates, the research think tank where I worked. Whenever I discussed my career challenges with him, he reminded me to ignore the noise. (And remember: it’s almost always noise.)

Mark teaches a class on financial engineering at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology (MIT). A few years ago, he asked me to give his students career advice. What kind of advice could I give some of the top students in the nation? By making it into this program, they had demonstrated remarkable academic talent. Did they need my advice? MIT is such a prestigious institution. All of them were going to receive multiple job offers. Many had already accumulated a few years of experience at Goldman Sachs, Morgan Stanley, and other Wall Street powerhouses.

I focused on what they wouldn’t learn in school. I wrote the students a letter summarizing real-world advice I had learned from Mark over the years. I boiled it down to five rules for success and added three of my own.

These rules aren’t just for students about to enter the corporate world—they can help at all stages of your career. I received positive feedback when I posted this letter on our corporate intranet. As an accomplished leader, you can use them as a template to give advice to others. Also, the rules go beyond control theory. But notice the advice on “focusing on the trend.” He didn’t call it control theory, but Mark was teaching me one of the model’s central tenets.

Here’s the letter:

![](image/1.jpg)\*\*\*\*\*

Dear MIT Financial Engineering Class,

You don’t need my advice. You have what it takes to succeed in your career. By making it into this program, you have demonstrated outstanding intellect and grit. Many of you have already accumulated professional experience. Employers will be knocking at your door.

In that context, think of what follows as reminders. We all make mistakes in our careers and our lives. To err is human.

During my first ten years in the industry, I was lucky to benefit from the advice of a very wise mentor. He helped me handle difficult situations and guided me through tough decisions. Importantly, he taught me informal “rules of success” for the corporate world. Ten years after we parted ways professionally, we remain friends. I continue to learn from him. In many ways, he is still my mentor.

My mentor is your Professor, Mark Kritzman.

Below are the Top 5 “rules for success” I’ve learned from Mark over the years. I’ve also added three rules of my own.

You might not find this advice particularly insightful, especially if you’ve already accumulated a few years of professional experience. “Duh!” you might think as you read these basic rules. Yet we often forget the basics—simple guiding principles that matter much more to success than the day-to-day ups and downs of our careers. I suppose that’s why self-help books are so popular. Also, some of the rules on this list may surprise you. They’re not obvious or intuitive until one has spent a few years on the job.

When you graduate, you will face perhaps one of the most critical forks in the road you’ll ever face from a professional standpoint. You must recognize that the rules that have made you successful as a student and the rules for success in the corporate world are different. Here goes, in no particular order:

Top 5 Rules for Corporate Success from Mark

1. Stay close to revenues: In many jobs in our industry, this rule means you should always strive to get closer to clients. “You need to stay in front of clients,” Mark would say. And it’s not (just) about getting credit for your work. Staying close to clients means learning their needs and making your work relevant to your firm’s commercial success. Your place in the value chain becomes clearer the closer you are to a revenue stream. This rule comes naturally to extroverts, “MBA-types,” but it’s equally essential for technical experts.

To be clear, many jobs aren’t client-centric, for example, risk-taking roles (trading, security analysis, portfolio management). But, in those positions, too, you should ask, “Where’s the revenue stream, and how can I get closer to it?” In those cases, you should aim to get closer to investment decisions—and maximize your impact on alpha generation.

2. Build your human capital: Jobs in our industry can be risky. No one gets tenure. When times get tough, you may face setbacks due to factors out of your control. This fact of life is one of the reasons why Mark always advised me to build my human capital. He defines human capital broadly: your network of industry contacts, your publications in academic journals, your reputation on the conference circuit, your educational credentials, etc.—anything that differentiates you from your peers and that no one can take away from you.

You may think focusing on human capital-building activities will appear selfish and may be at odds with your employer’s objectives. But, to the extent that you align your efforts to be relevant to your job, employers want you to build your human capital. You become happier at work, better at your job, and more productive. Everyone wins.

For example, at my firm, we encourage associates to take the CFA exams. We pay for their expenses and celebrate those who pass the various levels.

3. Focus on the trend: You will experience ups and downs in your career. You will have bad days. In risk-taking jobs, you will make bad decisions or experience bad luck. You will face setbacks, politics, stressful situations, missed deadlines, conflicts, etc. Many of these issues are just noise! Ask yourself: Will this issue affect my ability to achieve my goals over time? If not, ignore the noise and look at your progress from a longer-term perspective. Is the trend positive? It took me years to internalize this advice. I’m still working on it.

You might wonder what to do if the long-term trend is negative. Even in those cases, it’s better to gain clarity on how things are going for you from a long-term perspective. This way, you can plan accordingly—and find ways to change the trend.

![](image/Trend.png)

4. Don’t get too good at coding: Mark’s advice on computer programming was tongue-in-cheek. Many of you will have successful and fulfilling careers involving coding. By saying, “Don’t get too good at coding,” Mark meant that coding jobs sometimes become execution roles over time. “Data scrubbing” can become repetitive and time-consuming. Ultimately, this advice is not about coding or not coding. It’s about making sure that you progress towards creating, innovating, leading, selling, or risk-taking… not just execution for someone else’s priorities.

Of course, the path toward many of these value-add activities in technical fields involves coding. Most of you will create and innovate from a technical perspective, and doing so will propel your careers. Just don’t get stuck in the mud of a giant technical project with uncertain commercial applications. Such projects usually involve data.

5. Adjust your perspective: Happiness = Reality − Expectations. Your expectations impact your happiness. Manage them carefully. Expectations that are too high (leading to disappointment) are almost as bad as those that are too low (leading to apathy).

There’s nothing wrong with ambition. It’s important to set stretch goals for yourself. But occasionally, it helps to adjust our perspective. Stress and disappointment come from how we react to setbacks, not the setbacks themselves. Resilience is key. I encourage all of you to read about Stoic philosophy. Any book by Ryan Holiday is a good start.

Sébastien’s Top 3 Additional Rules

1. Emphasize communication: As students, you operate in a quasi-perfect meritocracy. You study hard, you get good grades. Aside from team projects, academic life is individualistic. Corporate life does not work that way for many reasons. In corporate life, collaboration and teamwork are essential. While perhaps 90–95% of your success as a student comes from your own intellectual merit, on the job, you will quickly realize that—even for the most technical of roles—maybe as much as half of your success is determined by how well you communicate.

Even risk-takers who live and die by the sword of investment performance must communicate with colleagues to generate trade ideas. When you start a new job, I urge you to write weekly updates for your boss, colleagues, and stakeholders. You don’t need to be self-promotional. Stick to the facts. Also, communicate clearly in meetings and presentations.

2. Pay attention to details: The stakes are high on the job. Many of you will quickly learn that you must always double- and triple-check your work. Your colleagues depend on your work. Your reputation is at stake. In finance, millions, even billions, of dollars are often involved. Attention to detail is much more important than in school. In addition to the disastrous effects of errors, a lack of attention to detail impacts how others perceive you. Over the years, I’ve seen many highly qualified professionals (PhDs, etc.) torpedo their careers with sloppy work.

Sometimes, deadlines loom, and multitasking is unavoidable. Besides, some basic tasks have lower stakes and don’t require perfection. But you must think about quality over quantity. It’s better to do a few things very well and pay attention to details than to take on too many projects and deliver “just okay” work on each of them. Exceptional work is what gets rewarded. On a related topic, I encourage you to read Deep Work by Cal Newport.

3. Take care of yourself: Mind your diet, exercise, and sleep. A career is a marathon. Think of yourself as a “corporate athlete.” Your diet, exercise habits, and sleep are the foundations of everything. Of all the advice I’ve outlined in this letter, I think this is the most important. Diet, exercise, and sleep reinforce each other in a virtuous cycle. Eat well, and you’ll have more energy to exercise. Exercise, and you’ll sleep better. Sleep well, and you’ll have more self-discipline with your diet because your appetite hormones will be in balance. You get the idea…

The key is to prioritize your health. Don’t wait for motivation. Instead, build habits (read Charles Duhigg’s The Power of Habit). Most of us underestimate the value of sleep, but it’s incredibly important. It impacts your energy, mood, defenses against disease, ability to concentrate, and appetite (read Matt Walker’s Why We Sleep).

![](image/Sleepdietexercise.png)

If you take one of these three pillars away, it becomes difficult to maintain the other two. Recently, I’ve also discovered a fourth pillar: mindfulness/meditation. This may not be for everybody, but I’ve learned that training the mind to make room for stillness is also an essential part of the corporate athlete’s training regimen.

As you move into the corporate world, you are about to face important decisions. As MIT students, you are in a good spot. I encourage you to approach this transition with excitement and a positive outlook. Sky is the limit, for all of you.

I wish you all the very best,

Sébastien Page

![](image/1.jpg)

Overreaction vs. underreaction errors

To focus on the trend, you must avoid two types of errors. I call these overreaction and underreaction errors. So far, I’ve emphasized situations when you overreact because you misinterpret noise as a signal. I’m most susceptible to this error. In my letter, I told the students to ignore the noise.

In contrast, an underreaction error occurs when you brush off a signal as noise. Underreaction errors can be more dangerous than overreaction errors. Sometimes, the blinking light on the radar isn’t a weather balloon; it’s an incoming missile.

I learned this the hard way during one of the worst weeks of my career.

When I ran a currency overlay business several years ago, we handled massive client transactions. Every day, we traded billions of dollars in foreign exchange contracts. Most of our mandates were to hedge currency risk for clients. To hedge a risk, you remove the exposure from the portfolio.

Our business was exploding in size and complexity. Over time, we had made minor errors. Nothing serious. For example, sometimes, our portfolio managers would trade the wrong amount because they would rely on bad data from the record-keeper. I wasn’t too worried. Our portfolio managers were diligent—real pros. And we had systems in place to catch these types of errors. Occasionally, I would get a call from our Head of Currency Management, explaining that we were on the hook for a small error but had caught it quickly. Each time, the amount of the loss was small, and we had a plan to make sure the error would not happen again. When you catch an operational error, the first thing you need to do is to put some controls in place to avoid this same type of error in the future. The solution is often to double-check your inputs across separate data sources.

As the pace of these small errors accelerated, I continued to brush them off as noise. They all seemed somewhat different, and our Head of Currency Management was confident the ship was safe. To be clear, it’s the job of record-keepers and money managers to take on these types of risks. The clients are almost never exposed to operational risk. Every financial firm has an error account to buffer profits and losses due to operational mishaps.

However, I was inexperienced. I was a nerdy quantitative researcher who had been asked to expand my responsibilities to run a real business with operational risk while continuing to manage my research team as a player-coach.

One day, I received a call that I should have been expecting.

The error wasn’t so small. We were responsible for a multi-million-dollar loss. My bosses were angry. I didn’t think I would get fired, but I lost sleep that week. The string of small errors hadn’t been noise—it had been a signal all along. I had made an underreaction error. If I had looked carefully at why some of these losses were small, I would have noticed the signal. These losses were small because they applied to small client accounts. I should have inferred that large losses could occur in large client accounts. It sounds simple in hindsight. I should have taken a step back and looked at the machine. Instead, I over-delegated oversight of these risks and failed to ask the right questions.

I hired a consulting firm to review our processes and systems. Their conclusion was interesting. We ran a best-in-class currency management operation. While there were some areas we could improve on the margin, our (my) mistake was that we had underestimated operational risk as a cost of doing business. So, I went to my bosses and told them that when it came to operational risk, they should, as I told the MIT students in my letter years later… lower their expectations.

The mother of all underreaction errors?

Another underreaction error the entire world made was to underestimate COVID and the authorities’ response to it (which some argue was an overreaction). In early 2020, the media focused on comparing the number of COVID cases with what had happened a few years earlier with SARS. It’s hard to believe now, but in the early days of the pandemic, almost everyone interpreted various reports on the virus coming out of Wuhan as noise. The mother of all underreaction errors! I’ll leave it to others to evaluate the Chinese authorities’ response and communication strategy in the early days of the pandemic, but at some point, the signals kept coming. They were there for everyone to see. If we’re being honest, and if we control for our hindsight bias, many of us underreacted.

But there were exceptions, including a few analysts and portfolio managers on our research platform. My colleague Berg Cui was one of them. Berg is a quantitative investment analyst. He holds a Bachelor of Engineering and a PhD in Economics. Not only is he highly qualified in quantitative methods (think: machine learning) and economics, he’s also a gifted communicator. Like me, he’s not a native English speaker. Yet, he uses precise language. When he presents or writes a report, he makes every word count. His quiet, understated personality stands in contrast with his direct communication style. He has the guts to express directional views and doesn’t insert caveats around everything he says or writes (compulsive hedging is a bad habit of many financial analysts, usually reinforced by compliance departments). Hence, he warned us clearly about COVID.

My investment team holds weekly strategy meetings, during which we discuss the macroeconomic environment and debate our tactical positions. To foster an idea meritocracy, I’ve kept the structure of this meeting completely flat—no hierarchy. Any analyst can express their views on current events and capital markets. All are invited to critique the positions in our portfolios. Portfolio managers are more interested in hearing from analysts who disagree with our positions than from those who agree. It’s in these Strategy meetings that Berg repeatedly warned us about COVID in early 2020. He led discussions on what we thought were crazy scenarios, like “what would happen if we had to implement lockdown measures in the United States?”

He was in contact with some of his family members in Wuhan, so he knew this virus was different and dangerous. Before the virus got to the United States, he warned us about a pandemic that would be “as important an event as World War II.” We all thought he was exaggerating.

On January 30, 2020, a few weeks before stuff hit the fan, I was asked to participate in Bloomberg TV’s Daybreak show. The virus was starting to get coverage in the financial media. Authorities in Wuhan had implemented a lockdown on January 23. I expected the show hosts would ask me about it, so I called Berg for his latest insights. He said: “It’s bad. It’s probably everywhere.”

He walked me through his thinking with supporting data. It wasn’t noise. Yet, at the time, like almost everybody else, I thought the base case would be that we’d get the virus under control. Prior virus outbreaks like SARS, Bird Flu, H1N1, and the Swine Flu hadn’t turned into pandemics. Besides, I didn’t want to be alarmist. I’m a wimp when it comes to making bold calls on TV.

So, I described Berg’s view as a tail risk. In the investment world, when you describe something as a tail risk, you think the downside could be devastating, but there’s only a small probability that it will happen. A small probability of a blow-up—the financial equivalent of a car crash or a flood.

The clip is still out there on the internet. Here’s an abstract of what I said:

A tail risk is an event with a small probability, but it’s very consequential. Before the lockdown of January 23rd, 1.8 million people got out of Wuhan. … We’re going to hit the two-week incubation period pretty soon. And two weeks after that, we’re going to get more data on ‘gen(eration) 2’ contagion—people who might have been exposed to the 1.8 million people who got out of Wuhan. … There’s a tail risk out there because this virus is different, and it can be contagious before you even get symptoms.

I was merely repeating what Berg had told me. But I hedged my comments. I included caveats like “no reason to be alarmist” and “the central scenario is that you get a hit to China’s GDP, but you get a V-shaped recovery.” I suppose I wasn’t buying Berg’s view one hundred percent.

So, I made an underreaction error for the world to see on TV. I wish I hadn’t ignored the signal. COVID overwhelmed several hospital systems across the globe. Authorities reacted with drastic, unprecedented lockdown measures. At work, I wish we had aggressively de-risked our portfolios. Markets started crashing about four weeks after my TV appearance. We recovered, however, by taking advantage of another signal: When markets crash, it’s usually a good buying opportunity for the brave willing to ignore the pessimistic media noise.

Underreaction can be worse than overreaction

Before we summarize takeaways on separating signal from noise, there’s one more thing about underreaction errors: you don’t want to make one regarding your career. I hinted at this issue in my letter to the MIT students. Recognize if you need to switch jobs. You must read the signals and discreetly start looking for another opportunity. I’m amazed at how many people don’t try to look elsewhere when the trend in their job is negative and when the signals become obvious.

Here are examples of career-related signals you don’t want to misinterpret as noise: when the company is in trouble and there’s no recovery in sight, when you’re getting repeated and serious negative feedback, or when the culture is toxic. A combination of these signals is more troubling—and indeed, they tend to occur together.

Some people stay in their job despite all three signals flashing red. Why would you do that? Perhaps you’re too risk-averse. Maybe you find it exhausting to lead a double life and interview for another job while working full-time. But it’s a big world out there, full of opportunities. Don’t be like the frog in boiling water.[4](ThePsychologyofLeadership-ebook-050325-22.xhtml#footnote-003) Realize when the temperature is going up before it’s too late.

The exact same advice applies on the other side of the coin, in your role managing employees. Many leaders misinterpret signals as noise when they manage underperforming or toxic employees and take too long to fire them. Firing someone is difficult and emotionally challenging. Most of us wait too long, to the detriment of team morale. If someone isn’t performing or they’re cutting ethical corners, be careful not to brush off the signal as noise.

Companies also often miss turning points in their industries and don’t switch strategies fast enough. Here are examples of companies whose leaders misinterpreted signals as noise: Blockbuster on the transition to streaming, Kodak on digital photography, BlackBerry on smartphones, and Sears on online retailing.

As a rule of thumb, underreaction errors can have worse consequences than overreaction errors. Yet people procrastinate and sometimes ignore problems because approaching them requires effort. Many problems will go away if you just wait (strategic patience). But not all of them. Some problems, like symptoms of cancer or diabetes, start to magnify and will continue to grow until you’re required to give them attention. At that point, it might be too late. If you’re going to err on one side, choose to act rather than wait.

Takeaways to improve your mental game

As a leader, most of the data on performance dashboards—as well as most of the feedback from consumers, employees, and others—that you receive daily is just noise. How do you separate signal from noise?

My advice is to consult with a mentor. Find someone with experience and perspective who doesn’t have skin in the game. Ask them to provide a clinical, unbiased assessment of your challenges.

Also, learn to focus on the trend rather than slight variations around it. Assess your performance and the performance of your business over longer horizons. What’s been the progress over the last three and five years? And do you expect this trend to continue?

Sometimes, a signal looks like noise. Other times, noise resembles a signal. Research in psychology shows that separating signal from noise is fraught with cognitive biases. You must recognize the difference to stay mentally sharp and focused. You don’t want to overreact or underreact. However, beware of underreacting to real threats. When it comes to the psychology of leadership, a little bit of healthy paranoia can be helpful.

But if your boss frowns at you one day, don’t quit your job (overreaction error).

---

1. [4](ThePsychologyofLeadership-ebook-050325-22.xhtml#footnote-003-backlink) From a scientific point of view, this story has been proven false: The frog indeed jumps out when the water gets too hot.

Takeaways on Execution

The first two parts of this book were 1) planning and 2) execution. In the planning stage, we discussed goals. How do you set the right goals for yourself and your organization? I asked you to think about how you define happiness and set big goals. We expanded positive psychology models from your personal goals to your role as an organizational leader.

For execution, we discussed how to manage stress. You can’t go after big goals if you can’t manage your stress. Not all stress is bad, especially if we expand the definition of stress to the scientific psychology model of arousal. I asked you to find the top of your YD curve for optimal performance, like the Japanese dancing mice. Again, we defined stress at the personal and organizational levels. Not enough stress leads to apathy. Too much stress leads to anxiety. Some complicated tasks require lower stress for deeper mental concentration, while simpler ones require higher stress. Archery requires calm. It’s a precision task that necessitates internal quiet. Powerlifting requires high stress and intensity.

Then, we discussed control theory. This model addresses many of the execution challenges you will face on the journey toward your big goals:

* How do you set milestones to measure progress?
* What’s the optimal rate of progress towards your goals?
* How do you optimize multistage decision-making?
* How do you separate signal from noise?

You must set a path for execution. You want to lose 15 lbs.? Fantastic. But how fast should you try to lose the weight to maximize your chances of success? Don’t try to lose 15 lbs. in one day like a professional boxer trying to make weight—that’s dangerous.

Similarly, suppose you, as a leader, want to double your team’s sales. You must set hard but achievable targets that will keep the team engaged. If you push too hard, your team might get discouraged or start cheating.

A key theme of this book is the power of expectations. You must believe in yourself and your organization. When it comes to meaning and accomplishment, the bigger the goal, the better. Let’s change the world. But when it comes to execution, you must manage your and your organization’s expectations realistically.

The optimal path towards big goals is progressing at a rate that matches or exceeds your expectations. Setting unachievable milestones causes frustration. Setting the bar too low makes achievement too easy and, therefore, not meaningful. Who cares if you accomplish something easy? Milestones should be hard enough to be challenging but not so hard that you fail to progress at a satisfying rate.

Set the milestones just right, and you’ll get yourself and your organization in flow. He might not be a perfect role model, but Elon Musk wants humanity to eliminate our dependence on fossil fuels. That’s a big goal. Yet he knows that to pull the plug on all fossil fuel consumption right now would lead to economic disaster. So, he’s setting incredibly stretched yet achievable milestones along the way. Achievable doesn’t mean easy.

Control theory also says that plans should be built in reverse. You start from the endpoint and work backward. This is a powerful and broad concept. It improves strategic planning and tactical decision-making. It applies to technical engineering problems as well as business strategy. It even applies to life goals. The Stoics want you to think about your death. Stephen Covey said, “Begin with the End in Mind.” We have discussed this topic several times in this book: What do you want your obituary to say?

Starting from the endpoint is the best way to optimize multistage decisions. If you have time to decide, don’t rush. Exercise strategic patience. Suppose you’re recruiting someone. You should set a very high bar before you settle early in your search process. Keep the optionality! In a business environment in which decisiveness is prized and everyone’s pressuring you to make a quick decision, strength comes from exercising strategic patience.

While the YD curves optimize stress, control theory optimizes patience.

Another lesson from control theory is that you must learn to put day-to-day challenges in the context of the long-term trend toward your goal. Ignore the noise—the office gossip, the short-term delays, a frown from your boss, a down day in the markets—and keep your eye on the prize. Don’t waste mental energy on small stuff.

However, to separate signal from noise is harder than it seems. It’s a skill you must develop. Mindfulness helps. Insert a micro-pause between the stimulus and your reaction, and yes, take a deep breath. Think about how you think. Use a cooling-off period if you need it. And get a mentor or anyone you can talk to in confidence to give you a clinical, dispassionate perspective. Avoid people who will egg you on.

Once again, the Stoics have it right: Your interpretation of the magnitude of the setback matters more than anything else. Realize that you’ll react differently to the same event whether you’re having a good or a bad day. Even what you ate that day will impact your interpretation! Cutting-edge medical research shows a feedback loop between your gut bacteria and your mood.

Most day-to-day setbacks in projects, relationships, and financial markets are just noise.

But don’t assume all signals come from long-term trends, while noise is exclusively the domain of short-term deviations. Looking at the trend helps separate signal from noise. However, it’s not that simple. There are also short-term signals. Some processes require micro-adjustments. In general, whether for an athlete or an employee, coaching is more efficient with rapid feedback.

Also, micro-adjustments in relationships can be beneficial. Small, respectful feedback between co-workers can build confidence and prevent longer-term conflicts. Of course, you don’t want to micromanage. You must tolerate minor issues while focusing on those that may build resentment over time. If you want to make giving and receiving feedback easier, remember Dr. David Rock’s trick: ask for feedback.

Part III: Unleashing the Power of Personality Psychology

The first two sections of this book focused on groundbreaking research in positive and sports psychology. Here, we venture into another of psychology’s largest and most popular branches: personality.

When we discussed the PERMA model of positive psychology, we learned from the ultra-long-term Harvard study that positive relationships are the most important factor for long-term happiness. We covered your relationships at home, at work, and between the corporation and its stakeholders. Setting big goals focused on relationships is not as straightforward as setting goals for measurable outcomes. Yet, it’s essential. Hence, we discussed corporate peer reviews and the feedback process, customer surveys, and company culture, including core beliefs.

What’s next?

As a leader, you can’t execute your goals if you don’t bring people along. You’re asking people to charge up the hill with you. Will they follow?

You must be a student of people. Every leader needs to be a bit of a psychologist and understand how people are wired. Most of my job as a leader has been focused on relationships: inspiring and helping people find their motivation, consoling those who feel down about their work, and, the hardest part of my job, resolving conflicts. I’m not a trained psychologist, but I wish I were. A lot of conversations you have as a leader amount to providing some form of therapy. It’s surprising that so few corporate leaders have a background in psychology.

How do you get better at relationships? Empathy is the key. You must listen and understand the perspective of others, especially if it’s different from yours. Understand why people think what they think and do what they do. All behavior is purposeful—and if someone’s behavior doesn’t make sense to you, that’s often because you have not taken the time to explore the underlying reasons for it.

The human mind is endlessly fascinating. In the first part of this book, I discussed how our leadership team used the Hermann Brain Dominance model, which describes people’s thinking preferences. It’s a popular tool, but the most ubiquitous model to understand how people think and behave is called the Big Five Personality Traits.

Never mind PERMA, Yerkes-Dodson stress curves, core beliefs, and control theory. The Big Five is, without a doubt, the most popular model from psychology that I cover in this book. You’ll find hundreds of scientific papers on it, including robust empirical studies (if you want to fit in at a university faculty cocktail party, just say “robust empirical study”) and meta-studies, which are studies of studies. Unlike some of the more scientific models we’ve covered, you’ll also find a gazillion pop psychology internet blogs on it. It may be the most popular model in the entire field of psychology.

“Personality” in psychology has a broad definition. The American Psychology Association defines personality as “the enduring characteristics and behavior that comprise a person’s unique adjustment to life, including major traits, interests, drives, values, self-concept, abilities, and emotional patterns.”

Here are the Big Five traits:

1. Extraversion
2. Agreeableness
3. Neuroticism
4. Openness
5. Conscientiousness

In this section, I provide a unique view of the Big Five by applying it to the psychology of leadership. Each of our final five Principles covers a personality trait. I offer examples of celebrities and famous characters who exhibit extremely high or low levels of each trait. Personality psychology is fun, fascinating, and incredibly useful for leadership.

HPOS

Before I describe each trait, let me start with the example of a former colleague at a firm I worked at earlier in my career. This colleague had an extreme, and I mean cartoonish, level of one of the traits. See if you can guess which trait. I’ll use this example to introduce caveats to the Big Five model and explain how the sausage is made, i.e., how researchers in academic psychology have settled on these five traits.

In his famous non-fiction book on investment banking titled Liar’s Poker, Michael Lewis refers to a Wall Street trader as the “Human Piranha.” He explains: “The Piranha didn’t talk like a person. He said things like, ‘If you f\*\*\*in’ buy this bond in a f\*\*\*n’ trade, you’re f\*\*\*n’ f\*\*\*ed.’ … No part of speech was spared. His world was filled with copulating inanimate objects and people getting their faces ripped off.”

I won’t name my colleague, but the best way I can describe his personality would be as the “Human Piranha on Steroids.” Let’s call him HPOS. He was a sales manager. One of the best stories I’ve heard about him took place on our trading floor. Picture a large open floor plan with 300 people crammed together and working at their stations with multiple screens. One day, he was upset about something. He got up and yelled at one of his employees, who was sitting a few desks away:

“Hey! F\*\*\* face!”

Not knowing exactly who he was addressing, ten people—his entire team—got up from their desks: “Yes boss?”

HPOS and I worked well together because he’s an extreme extrovert, and I’m an introvert. HPOS’s professional life is dedicated to spending time with people, meeting new people, selling, or socializing. Despite the swearing (which is just an act), he’s incredibly approachable. He loves being around people. He talks to flight attendants or fellow travelers on airplanes and makes them laugh. I’ll tell you more about him shortly.

Caveats on personality traits

With the HPOS example, I’m caricaturing extraversion. Personality traits aren’t binary; they lie on a continuum. You could place HPOS and me on a scale:

![](image/Extraversioncontinuum.png)

Also, there’s an academic debate as to whether traits can be situation-specific. To me, it’s common sense that personality isn’t set in stone—there are many situations when I turn into an extrovert, and not all of them involve drinking wine. This person-situation debate dates back to academic psychologist Walter Mischel’s 1968 book Personality and Assessment. Mischel argued that personality traits drive less of people’s behavior than most psychologists assumed.

What do you think? Does your personality change with the social situation? Are you a different person at work and at home, for example? The debate between “situationists” and trait psychologists continues to this day. The bottom line is that the Big Five model is useful and backed by serious research, but the traits are not binary nor stable. Your personality traits change depending on the situation.

From the International Encyclopedia of the Social Sciences: “Emerging from two or three decades of debate, most [personality] theories are ‘interactionist’ because they acknowledge the fact that behavior results from an interaction between personality and situational forces.”

I advise you to be careful not to overuse the Big Five. Like with horoscopes, it’s tempting to explain all sorts of behaviors with the traits, even when other factors are at play. A comment like “Well, of course, she would do that, she’s an extrovert!” can start to sound like, “Well, of course, she would do that, she’s a Sagittarius!”

How are traits defined?

Unlike horoscopes, there are robust empirical studies (that term again) behind the Big Five. Lewis R. Goldberg, professor emeritus at the University of Oregon, coined the term “Big Five.” He reviewed the history of this model in a 1993 article titled “The Structure of Phenotypic Personality Traits.” (“Phenotype” is a term used in biology/genetics to represent an individual’s observable traits.) In the very first line of his paper, he sets up a strawman, as intellectuals like to do when they debate each other. On Mischel’s critique that personality traits don’t explain much of people’s behavior, he opens with: “Once upon a time, we had no personalities (Mischel, 1968).”

That’s an exaggeration of the conclusion of Mischel’s book, but isn’t it a brilliant way to start a paper?

To define personality traits, researchers have used factor or “cluster” analysis of words found in surveys, synonym dictionaries, peer reviews, and more. The goal is to find which personality descriptor is most often associated with another. For example, here are some pairs of words that tend to go together when describing someone’s personality:

* sociable and enthusiastic
* calm and confident
* anxious and moody
* reserved and thoughtful

In contrast, the pairs “reserved and sociable” or “anxious and calm” rarely describe the same person. This type of analysis, Goldberg explains, dates to a 1934 article by Louis Leon Thurstone titled “The Vectors of Mind.” Thurstone pioneered factor analysis in psychology and, interestingly, in measuring intelligence. He asked 1,300 raters to circle adjectives that best described someone they knew. Then, he measured how often the same adjectives were used together. When a rater described someone as “calm,” did they usually also choose “confident” as a descriptor? Thurstone found that five factors were enough to create neat groupings or clusters and concluded that “the scientific description of personality may not be quite so hopelessly complex as it is sometimes thought to be.”

Factor analysis allows for positive and negative exposures called “loadings.” In finance, oil producers have a positive loading on the oil factor. These companies make more money when the price of oil goes up. Airlines, in contrast, have a negative loading because they lose money when fuel costs rise with oil prices. Both sectors are sensitive to the price of oil, but in opposite directions. If you want more exposure to the price of oil in your portfolio, you should buy oil producers and sell airlines (who tend to do badly when oil prices go up). It’s the same approach in psychology. The descriptor “sociable” loads positively on the extraversion factor, while “reserved” loads negatively.

With that background in place, let’s move on to the Principles. We start with reading the room, and getting the best out of everybody on your team.

Principle 14: Read the Room—Call on the Introverts

“Stop the madness for constant group work.”

—Susan Cain, author of Quiet: The Power of Introverts in a World That Can’t Stop Talking

The most well-known of the Big Five personality traits is extraversion. Most people have a view as to whether they’re introverts or extroverts.

The first time I met the Human Piranha on Steroids, I was starting my career. My English was mediocre, which made me self-conscious in social situations. I spent my days writing code and running “robust empirical studies,” trying to find new ways to make money in the stock market (and manage risk) using mathematical models. I was, and still am in many ways, a shy nerd.

My models seemed to work well, so my boss asked me to meet with some of our institutional clients to gauge their interest. “I need you to fly to Texas to present your research to a few clients. I’ll ask one of our assistants to book the trip for you. Once you arrive, HPOS will take you to different clients’ offices. To warn you, you’re going to be on a tight schedule,” he said.

“Who’s HPOS?” I asked.

His answer was terse: “Salesman.”

My itinerary showed I should meet HPOS at the Hertz location near the airport at 1 pm. After landing in Texas, I gave the taxi driver the Hertz address on my itinerary.

Then things got weird.

The address was for an abandoned Hertz location. It was about 20 minutes from the airport, much further than you would expect an airport rental car location to be. I paid the taxi driver, and he left immediately. I wondered why HPOS wanted to meet me there. Here I was, in the middle of nowhere, on my first business trip, wearing my cheap suit and holding a heavy bag full of PowerPoint presentations.

I’m not exaggerating: there was no one around, no other building. Just the dilapidated Hertz location. Complete silence.

I had been waiting for at least 45 minutes when my phone rang.

“Sébastien?”

It was HPOS. Someone had given him my cell phone number.

“Where the f\*\*\* are you?!” he screamed.

“I’m at the Hertz location,” I answered.

You can imagine the conversation that followed. We both quickly realized that I was at the wrong Hertz. HPOS was livid. The person who had put the itinerary together was to blame, but HPOS didn’t care. I had never heard such a high ratio of f-words to everyday words in my life—beyond Michael Lewis’ Human Piranha.
