HPOS’s key messages were (minus the swearing): you’re an idiot; aren’t you supposed to be one of these geniuses from the research team; we’re running very, very late to our first meeting; and, stay there, I’ll get to you as fast as I can drive.

Here’s the twist: This was the beginning of one of the most productive professional relationships of my entire career. The meetings that day went well. Despite my imperfect English, I showed off my technical and communication skills. And the clients loved and trusted HPOS (he didn’t swear in front of them). Over the next few years, HPOS took me under his wing, and we built a successful business together.

I mentioned that I don’t like to attend cocktail parties at conferences. As an introvert, if you invite me to a dinner party to meet people, I might ultimately enjoy it, but it will require a lot of my energy. And if you talk to me on an airplane, you can count the seconds before I put on my Bose headphones. I don’t mean to be rude. It’s just that while extroverts like HPOS derive more energy from socializing, for me, it’s a bit draining. I prefer to have only a few very good friends rather than an ever-rotating or expanding social circle. I’ve known my high-school friend Phil for over 35 years. We haven’t lived in the same city since university. But we still talk and text regularly and work on private investing projects together. Thirty-five years of unbroken friendship with Phil is more meaningful to me than a hundred new friends.

With HPOS, our dog-and-pony show worked perfectly with clients. He excelled at the sales and social aspects of the relationship, and I delivered investment insights. He talked about football and why Cheesecake Factory is the best restaurant chain ever, and I talked about “robust empirical studies.” This combination is used throughout the finance industry: an extroverted salesperson works with an introverted quantitative analyst. One of my old colleagues, a salesperson, used to call my team of research analysts the “geeks in tow.”

I don’t mean to stereotype: Not all salespeople are extroverts, and not all analysts are introverts. Yet certain professions will draw on specific personality traits of the Big Five. There’s a reason HPOS gravitated to sales, and I went to analysis. The roles match essential aspects of our personalities.

Principle 14, “Read the Room—Call on the Introverts,” is about getting the best out of everybody, from the most extroverted to the most introverted.

The extraversion scale

Here are the descriptors associated with each side of the extraversion scale. For fun, I’ve added the names of famous personalities on each side. See if you agree with my classification.

![](image/Table03.jpg)

There are important leadership rules to learn from this trait. In her 2013 book Quiet: The Power of Introverts in a World That Can’t Stop Talking, Susan Cain argues that we undervalue introverts. In her Ted talk related to the book, “The Power of Introverts,” which has been viewed over 30 million times, she explains that introverts are routinely passed over for leadership positions, yet they make good leaders because they’re better than extroverts at empowering others.

She also insists that we should build teams comprised of extroverts and introverts (diversity) and that leaders should call on introverts to contribute (inclusion). She makes the provocative statement, “There’s zero correlation between being the best talker and having the best ideas.”

She recommends that people work on their ideas, then discuss them in a well-managed group setting. One of her calls to action is: “Stop the madness for constant group work.”

I agree. Too many meetings! She recommends that everyone find more time for solitude and deep work.

Still, I’m a good talker (despite being an introvert), so it’s hard for me to buy into her zero-correlation claim. All else being equal, people who know what they’re talking about tend to speak with more confidence.

In my experience, the most important rule of thumb related to extraversion that will help you as a leader is to call on introverts. Read their non-verbal cues to know when they have something to say. I’ve led so many meetings when the extroverts dominate the conversation. In my steering committee, we often get stuck in our discussions when dealing with a thorny issue. We start talking past each other and can’t narrow down the options to find a good solution. To move forward, I’ll often call on a steering committee member who’s remained quiet throughout the debate. It’s usually—but not always—one of the members who prefers to sit back, watch, and think. One of the introverts.

There are many reasons someone might not contribute to a meeting beyond being an introvert. Let’s agree with Mischel that personality doesn’t explain everything. And as we will discuss shortly, there are other personality traits. Don’t fall into the trap of explaining too much of people’s behaviors along the extraversion scale.

Lack of knowledge of the topic at hand is an example. Extroverts may remain quiet when they aren’t knowledgeable or involved in the topic discussed. Introverts can dominate conversations too. As I said, don’t stereotype. Extroverts are prone to thinking out loud, but they aren’t all self-promotional jerks who like to hear themselves talk. Introverts aren’t all super nice people who would contribute sooo much to society if only extroverts would shut up (with apologies to Susan Cain).

Also, don’t let personality psychology become a self-fulfilling prophecy for your behaviors. If you categorize yourself as an extrovert, don’t let that drive or excuse all your day-to-day behaviors.

Everyone on my committee is confident in their knowledge and skills. They’re all high-performing, ambitious, experienced pros. And they want to contribute. They care. Therefore, when I call on someone quiet, I’m generally not putting them in an uncomfortable situation. Nonetheless, you should be careful: The goal is not to force anybody to talk when they’d rather not. You’re trying to give people an opportunity to contribute, not trying to put people on the spot (unless, I suppose, they’re not paying attention and you want to get them mentally back into the meeting, but that’s another matter).

Instead, you want to break the flow of a conversation that’s going in circles. You want to get some fresh thinking on the table. To do this, you’ll often need to interrupt one of the extroverts. If it’s a tense or exciting conversation and you don’t want to be rude, that’s not easy. There’s an art to interruption. Here’s a three-step method I’ve found useful:

1. Apologize for interrupting.
2. Summarize the person’s points.
3. Pivot.

I don’t always do this well because I get too involved in the debates myself, but when I mind my manners and use this method, it works.

By summarizing, you’re showing the person that you were listening, and often, you’re helping them make their point.

“So, Susanne, excellent points. Apologies for interrupting, but we only have ten minutes left in this meeting. What you’re saying is ….”

The rest of the team will appreciate that you’ve moved the discussion along, and the person you’ve interrupted should feel heard and be okay with it.

Then comes the pivot. You can throw the ball to someone else, usually an introvert, who’s been quiet all along: “John, what do you think?”

It’s such a simple, open-ended question. In my steering committee, it’s the most powerful question I can ask. In almost all cases, the quiet person will simplify the complex issue to its core and offer helpful insights.

Takeaways to improve your mental game

Recognize who’s an introvert and who’s an extrovert on your team. Deliberately call on introverts. To do so, you may have to stop the extroverts from talking. Follow these three steps: apologize for interrupting, summarize the person’s point, and pivot. You must solicit contributions from those who are reserved without making them feel like they’re being put on the spot. Giving everyone a chance to have the spotlight makes your meeting more productive and keeps everyone mentally alert.

In business, listening is a superpower. In four words, here’s the most critical lesson in leadership I’ve learned over the last few years: talk less, listen more. Maybe Susan Cain’s “power of introverts” boils down to the superior ability of introverts to follow that advice. Introverts often make great leaders because they’re comfortable letting everyone else talk while they listen and process, then summarize well and briefly. I keep forgetting to do this, although I’m improving. I’ve written it down on a yellow sticky note on the edge of my computer screen: “Talk less, listen more.”

And remember, don’t stereotype. Personality traits are nuanced. If you ever meet a human piranha, don’t worry. Most of them don’t bite.

Principle 15: Don’t Run Your Business Like a Democracy—Be Disagreeable, Sometimes

“A genuine leader is not a searcher for consensus but a molder of consensus.”

—Martin Luther King, Jr.

“It’s a fungus, they think. A benign fungus?” says Greg Hirsch, the pushover of the family, talking about a problem with his toe.

To which Tom Wambsgans replies: “Great title for your memoir!”

Do you know someone who’s hard to work with? Someone who enjoys conflict? A self-centered jerk? This person would score low in agreeableness, the second trait of the Big Five. If you have watched the show Succession, from which I took this dialogue, think of Logan Roy, the family’s patriarch. He’s the bully of all bullies. He doesn’t care about hurting other people’s feelings.

If you haven’t seen the show, I encourage you to look up a few YouTube clips. If you don’t mind the swearing, this show has the most brutal, witty, funny, and intelligent dialogue of any show I’ve ever watched. The Roy family is comprised of the most disagreeable people you can imagine.

This next scene occurs when the crass, low-agreeableness Roy family tries to persuade the Pierce family to sell them their media company. The two families are at a cocktail party. The Roys are supposed to be on their best behavior. But they can’t even fake agreeableness.

Mark Pierce, who appears to be in his mid-40s, humble-brags about his education in Africana studies: “Well, the second PhD is much harder. Yeah, five years is an absolute gauntlet.”

To which Shiv Roy replies: “Oh, I’m sure. But just think, once you’re done, you won’t have to waste the 12 seconds it takes to look up something on Wikipedia.”

Principle 15, “Don’t Run Your Business Like a Democracy—Be Disagreeable, Sometimes,” goes against the conventional wisdom that agreeableness is always a positive trait.

The agreeableness scale

Here are the descriptors associated with both sides of the agreeableness scale, and again, some unscientific celebrity diagnoses—a few of which you may disagree with, but that’s okay because I’m agreeable and quite open to your opinion.

![](image/Table04.jpg)

Agreeableness is the willingness to go along and get along—not having to have it your way. Being agreeable means being mentally flexible, adaptive, and open to another person’s perspective.

Notice something different from the extraversion scale? On the extraversion scale, there are advantages to either side. Here, there’s a good side and a bad, almost malevolent side. However, it’s not that black-and-white. You don’t want to be a pushover. If you’re too agreeable, some people will take advantage of you. There are advantages to a bit of disagreeableness.

At my firm, we hire nice people. Extreme low agreeableness, i.e., being a narcissistic jerk, doesn’t work with our culture. You could summarize our recruiting rule of thumb as “we don’t hire a\*\*holes.” But I’ve seen organizations where being a jerk is the norm, and you know what? Some are very successful. But these organizations don’t win “Best Places to Work” awards.

Unlike the Roys in Succession, those who score high in agreeableness are averse to conflict. They’re more likely to be empathetic, flexible, and altruistic. I mentioned that in our peer review process, I ask everyone to score each other on their ability to collaborate. It’s a broad question, and it’s not perfect. I don’t want to measure who’s most popular. I want to measure who makes the most meaningful contributions to the team (in addition to reaching their personal goals). Popularity and team contributions are correlated, but not perfectly. Someone can make a significant contribution by disagreeing with the consensus. Or, as we discussed in the first part of this book, someone may get good collaboration scores merely because they agree with everybody, which is not productive, especially if they’re in a leadership position.

One of the most critical leadership qualities is being comfortable making people uncomfortable. In doing so, you’re not always agreeable. But you’re pushing people to grow, especially if you’re giving them feedback in a respectful way that lets them know they’re valued.

Another question in our peer reviews measures agreeableness better than the collaboration question. It’s more revealing. And it generates a broader dispersion of scores. I ask people to rate each other on the following: This person is open and responsive to differing viewpoints.

This question echoes the definition of agreeableness as “being open to another person’s perspective.” It also measures whether you’re open to the idea that you might be wrong. It’s easy to be open to viewpoints you agree with. The challenge is when you disagree.

Do you consider yourself self-aware?

The correlation between your self-appraisal and peer scores from this question reveals your self-awareness. Research from the early 1960s suggests that most people think they have an above-average sense of humor. Similarly, in one study, 94% of university professors rated themselves as having above-average teaching ability. People tend to view themselves positively, and that’s not a bad thing from a mental health perspective. There’s a strand of research in social psychology on this topic called “illusory superiority.”

In the same vein, everyone thinks they’re agreeable. In other words, many people disagree with the feedback that they’re disagreeable, which is a bit of a paradox. How well would you score on the “open and responsive to differing viewpoints” question? I know when I’m being disagreeable, and I often regret it after the fact, but generally, I consider myself agreeable. I also have above-average writing skills, investment skills, sense of humor, driving skills, and of course, I most definitely have above-average self-awareness and superior humility…

A more accurate assessment of your agreeableness might come from the perspectives of others who know you well. If you don’t use peer reviews, ask people around you for candid feedback. Remember, neuroleadership guru David Rock says you should ask for feedback to reduce the stress for both parties, the giver and the receiver.

However, as I’ve mentioned, you must be careful with all peer review data because they reveal as much about the rater’s biases as the ratee’s performance. It’s been estimated that peer reviews measure only about 20–25% of true performance dispersion across people. Someone may give a low score to a colleague because that colleague disagrees with them. But it’s not about agreeing, it’s about being open. As Stephen Covey would say, listening to understand rather than reply. One of my favorite quotes from Covey is, “Seek first to understand, then to be understood.”

The importance of being disagreeable, sometimes

What you won’t find in Covey’s book is the notion that sometimes it’s necessary to be disagreeable. As a leader, you can’t always agree with everybody.

Is it better for leaders to be agreeable? It can be circumstantial. For example, you don’t want an agreeable leader in a military engagement. The leader needs to be in charge and decisive, and you don’t question that.

But no one’s always right about everything. And corruption can come with that level of power. I’ve never been in the military, but I’m sure that in practice, leaders take inputs from soldiers… to a limit.

In business, agreeableness has its downsides too. Successful leaders can tap into the better qualities of both poles of each of the Big Five traits, depending on the circumstance—and that includes agreeableness. You’re not a leader if all you’re doing is building consensus and trying to make everybody happy. You can’t make everybody happy! You must make decisions between competing priorities and competing advice. Sometimes, your decisions will be optimal for the business, but they will create winners and losers. You may have to fire people.

In my job as a leader, I need to balance this idea that agreeableness has its limits with the need to retain the support of my team. In most companies, leaders can be unpopular with their employees yet survive for a very long time. As long as the team delivers good results and the leader is good at managing up, no one at the top seems to care or even notice that the leader is a tyrant to those below them. There are way too many leaders who are awful at managing down (disagreeable with their employees) yet fantastic at managing up (agreeable with their boss). I’ve worked for one at some point in my career. It wasn’t fun. (Exit interviews can reveal if this is happening, and a good HR department will know what to do with the data.)

Agreeableness and leadership transitions

At my firm, the kiss of death for any senior leader is to lose their team’s support. My boss looks at how my team rates my performance through a formal review process. I do the same with leaders who report to me. We don’t tolerate bad leadership for too long. This culture can be confusing for senior leaders that we hire externally. Many want to make a splash in their first few months and deliver early wins. To do so, they must break eggs, i.e., disagree with the current way of doing things. After all, we hire leaders from the outside to drive change.

But new leaders who break too many eggs fail. When I interview someone for a leadership position and they ask how we’ll measure their success in their first year, I tell them that we won’t. It’s an overstatement that I use to make a point: their number one job in their first year won’t be to hit some Key Performance Indicators. It will be to build productive relationships with peers, Their teams, and senior management. Remember the R in PERMA!

In his excellent book on managing a career change, The First 90 Days, Michael D. Watkins, Professor of Leadership and Organizational Change, argues that senior leaders who take a new job will fail or succeed within their first 90 days. And by the way, about 50% of leadership transitions fail, with the new leader leaving or getting fired. No pressure!

The two times I’ve switched companies were the most stressful and exhausting periods of my career. Watkins uses a powerful analogy: “Joining a new company is akin to an organ transplant—and you’re the new organ. If you’re not thoughtful in adapting to the new situation, you could end up being attacked by the organizational immune system and rejected.”

The potential downside of an agreeable culture is that some leaders feel the need to always build consensus and make everybody happy. A consensus-driven approach can require too many meetings and slow decision-making. Of course, corporate meetings are like children: Other people’s meetings are a waste of time, while the meetings you initiate are always perfect.

In my role, I must balance agreeableness with the courage to make unpopular yet necessary decisions. I struggle with this tradeoff. Am I too soft? Or am I too autocratic? The number one risk with agreeableness is that you let conflict fester because you don’t want to upset either side. Once again, we run into control theory: Leaders must separate signal from noise to choose when to be disagreeable.

As a default position, agreeableness is a desirable trait in a leader. When in doubt, be agreeable. Be more like Keanu Reeves, the Dalai Lama, Mr. Rogers, and less like Charlie Sheen, Joffrey Baratheon (you definitely don’t want to be like Joffrey), and Logan Roy.

Speaking of Joffrey Baratheon, the most-hated character from the popular show Game of Thrones, his innate disagreeableness made him ineffective as King. His clever—yet equally evil—grandfather, Tywin Lannister, gets to the crux of the matter in the following dialogue.

Joffrey says to a member of his council: “I am the King! And I will punish you!”

To which his wise grandfather responds: “Any man who must say ‘I am the King’ is no true king.”

Have you ever found yourself saying “I am the boss” (or something equivalent) during a disagreement at work? I’m going to guess that when you did, you had already lost the argument. Parents know that when you argue with a child, it’s better to explain your rationale than to revert to the classic: “Why? Because I said so. And I’m your parent.”

I’ll concede that sometimes, a reminder of your formal authority is needed, but rarely.

Importantly, someone who’s always disagreeable, like Joffrey, ends up with less influence than someone who’s rarely disagreeable yet can show teeth occasionally. Every leader must be able to lean towards the opposite side of agreeableness.

People who swear constantly, like my ex-colleague HPOS, dilute the effect. In contrast, if someone who almost never swears suddenly drops an f-bomb, you know they mean business. I’m not encouraging anybody to swear in the workplace because it can be perceived as a form of violence. I almost never hear anyone swear at my firm (we’re nice people, remember?). Yet I’ve known leaders who deliberately chose precisely when to drop the rare swear word for maximum effect.

I believe that to be an effective leader, you must be disagreeable about 10% of the time. Use this 10% thoughtfully and deliberately. Personally, sometimes I’m too agreeable, and sometimes I’m too disagreeable. I’d like to improve the balance and pick my battles better.

Later, I’ll show the correlations between the Big Five traits and leadership effectiveness. What do you think is the correlation between agreeableness and leadership success? And does being an extrovert contribute to or hurt leadership success? What about the other traits? Stay tuned.

Takeaways to improve your mental game

Agreeableness is a desirable personality trait. It’s a good default setting for leaders. Getting along helps with collaboration. However, it has potential downsides.

Leaders who are too agreeable aren’t effective when tough decisions need to be made. They will be seen as pushovers, and people will take advantage of them. There are some advantages to calculated disagreeableness. Sometimes, you must be disagreeable to be an effective leader. How often? I would say about 10% of the time. Learn to pick your battles.

Also, you must learn to manage people who are agreeable, disagreeable, and everyone in the middle to channel aggressiveness and competitiveness into your organization’s favor. Disagreeing is often necessary to uncover the best ideas. You must be comfortable with disagreeableness and, unlike the Roys in Succession, learn how to turn it into a positive force to get the most out of the team.

Don’t tell someone they should title their memoir Benign Fungus or that the only reason to earn their PhD is to save 12 seconds looking up the same knowledge on Wikipedia.

Principle 16: Learn to Love Worry—Harness the Positive Side of Neuroticism

“Everything great in the world comes from neurotics.”

—Marcel Proust, author

When you think of neuroticism, a tendency towards anxiety and emotional instability, Woody Allen is a good celebrity caricature. Here’s a quote from him on why he’s remained skinny his entire life: “See, I never gain an ounce, because, you know, my anxiety acts like aerobics.”

He’s joking, but he may be on to something. The brain occupies about 2% of our body mass, but uses 20% of our energy. Chess players can burn up to 6,000 calories a day while playing in a tournament. And there’s a strong link between anxiety and appetite. This link can go in both directions, from appetite loss to overeating.

Most people don’t realize there’s a direct and circular feedback loop between their gut and brain. What you eat impacts your mood, and your mood impacts your gut bacteria and digestion. I digress, but it’s unfortunate that the medical community emphasizes drugs for stomach, appetite, and mental health issues over diet as the first line of defense.

Here’s a more existential quote from Allen: “I think there is too much wrong with the world to ever get too relaxed and happy. The more natural state, and the better one, I think, is one of some anxiety and tension over man’s plight in this mysterious universe.”

High neuroticism is a propensity to worry, to be anxious, concerned, fearful, and risk-averse. On the other side of the scale, people who score low in neuroticism tend to be implacable, unaffected, emotionless, disengaged, and low energy.

High neuroticism is statistically associated with mental health issues such as anxiety and depression. But as with the other traits, there’s nuance. Some of the descriptors of low neuroticism are undesirable: disengaged, low energy.

Principle 16, “Learn to Love Worry—Harness the Positive Side of Neuroticism,” teaches us that a little bit of neuroticism is necessary and the absence of neuroticism is not a good thing.

The neuroticism scale

At this point on our journey, you, dear reader, can probably guess roughly where I land on the neuroticism scale.

In a few pages, I’ll reveal my scores for each trait of the Big Five model…

For now, let me mention my friend Troy. Of everyone I’ve ever known, Troy scores the lowest on neuroticism by a wide margin. That’s my unscientific assessment, although anyone who knows him would agree. Troy has lived his whole life in Newport Beach, California. He’s a surfer, a high-powered lawyer, and a successful entrepreneur. Despite running a business in a hard-charging industry (commercial real estate law), he’s incredibly relaxed. I’ve never seen Troy get stressed. Ever. We call him “Teflon Troy.” He’s always cheerful, social, and relaxed. If we’re running late to go somewhere for dinner, Troy doesn’t care. He doesn’t sweat the small stuff. Sometimes, that’s an issue. We never know whether he’s going to show up on time. He’s punctual at work, but on weekends or vacation trips, asking, “Where’s Troy?” has become our running joke.

Extremely low neuroticism has its downsides, but I think Troy is at the optimal point. He’s not a disengaged or low-energy person. He’s serene, relaxed, and cheerful. You can’t insult Troy. First, you don’t want to insult him because he never insults anybody himself unless it’s in jest (he has rapid-fire wit). And even if you get out of line, Troy will laugh it off.

Have you ever heard of the dead body test? It’s a great discussion topic over cocktails. It’s clearly hypothetical because, of course, all of us would call the cops, but it goes like this: If you ever had to hide a dead body, which of your friends would you call for help? It’s a question of how close, loyal, and able to keep a secret your friends are. Troy would be at the top of my list. Bottom line: I’ve never met anybody like him.

Here are the descriptors associated with both sides of the neuroticism scale and some celebrity guesses.

If you search on Google for “neurotic celebrities” or “celebrities with anxiety,” you’ll notice that almost all celebrities self-report as high in neuroticism. That includes Oprah, Stephen Colbert, Taylor Swift, Scarlet Johansson, and Beyoncé. Is it trendy to say you’re neurotic? Are celebrities trying to signal they’re just like the rest of us? I suppose there’s a link between high neuroticism and making the career choice to become a Hollywood star, but I haven’t found scientific research on this topic.

Radio host Howard Stern—one of the best interviewers of celebrities and self-described neurotic—often comments that celebrities seem driven by a deep sense of insecurity and an almost unnatural yearning to be loved. Put it this way: It’s nearly impossible to find celebrities with low neuroticism. Still, I gave it a shot.

![](image/Table05.jpg)

As a leader, you can improve your management of individuals and teams by understanding where people fall on this scale. Learn to read personality traits! If someone scores high on neuroticism, allow them to vent their anxiety—just listen. If someone scores low, remember that they might need a gentle push once in a while to perform at their best. Use the YD curves we discussed earlier to calibrate people’s goals based on their stress tolerance.

And as with all the personality traits, you should build diverse teams in which personalities balance each other out. Don’t put all the low-neuroticism people on the same project. If you do, you might experience delays. And if you stack a team with high-neuroticism people, be careful because members might egg each other on and work themselves into a panic.

Let me give you a few examples to help you learn to read people’s level of neuroticism.

Years before the artificially intelligent chatbot ChatGPT was released, in 2016, IBM’s supercomputer Watson analyzed dialogue from Star Wars to assess the main characters’ personalities. Watson used the Big Five model. I don’t know if that’s a window into the future of artificial intelligence, but merging human psychology with AI seems a bit scary. Is that how we end up in the Matrix?

Reporting on the IBM study, Althea Serad, writer at independent fantasy and science fiction website Epicstream, explains why Watson classified C-3PO as high in neuroticism: “C-3PO is the most neurotic, obviously. There’s no question about this. C-3PO couldn’t stop worrying about anything and everything throughout the entire original trilogy.”

Earlier, we discussed the importance of separating signals from noise. Neurotics see many signals that are, in fact, noise, while people low in neuroticism miss important signals and interpret them as noise.

IBM researcher Vinith Misra points to the Jedis’ levelheadedness as an example of low neuroticism. “Yoda is one of the least neurotic characters,” he concludes.

Neil Armstrong provides another example of low neuroticism. The first manned moon landing in 1969 almost failed. During the last few moments, everything went awry. Armstrong and Buzz Aldrin received unexpected error messages from the onboard computer due to a data overload. The computer bug occurred at the same time as the radio connection between the astronauts and mission control deteriorated to almost zero. Then, the Lunar Module veered off course. Oh, and it almost ran out of fuel—Murphy’s law.

There was a point in the mission when the option of aborting the landing was taken off the table. From Controller Steve Bales: “In the middle of landing, it was almost as dangerous to try to abort with a bad computer as it was to carry on with the landing. So balancing risk versus risk, we decided that the safest thing would be to continue to land.”

Armstrong took over manual control of the landing. With nerves of steel, he landed the Lunar Module. It’s an incredible story. Real-time data reveals that Armstrong’s heartbeat was around 75 for most of the mission until it understandably spiked at 150 when the Module touched down. Most adults have resting heart rates between 60 and 100.

Armstrong must have scored low in neuroticism. In addition to his calm under pressure, here’s another hint: he didn’t fit the Hollywood-type mold. After the mission, although he was the most famous man in the world, Armstrong shunned the attention. For the rest of his life, he lived on a farm in Ohio and taught aerospace engineering at the University of Cincinnati. From an article written about him back in 1999: “Armstrong still refuses all interviews, makes only occasional public appearances, and goes out of his way to avoid any form of civic honor. ‘I don’t want to be a living memorial,’ he once grumbled. He has even refused to let a hospital take his name.”

The same article reveals that Buzz Aldrin followed a different path:

Buzz Aldrin, who stood beside Armstrong on the Moon 30 years ago became a Nasa PR spokesman, and quickly spiraled into depression, a spell in a psychiatric ward, divorce and finally alcoholism, before emerging as a teetotal author, product endorser and after-dinner speaker.

It appears Aldrin suffered the downsides of neuroticism for most of his life.

What are the benefits of neuroticism?

Should we all strive to be more like Neil Armstrong and less like Buzz Aldrin? More like Yoda and less like C-3PO? It’s not that simple. Many people consider Woody Allen a creative genius, and celebrities like Oprah are admired by millions of people. I’ve made it my mission in this book to avoid facile generalizations. I hope you’ll agree that I’m giving you both sides of the models, whether it’s disagreements on academic research or nuances in their interpretation or usefulness. Psychology requires nuance. I explained why leaders who can’t dispense the occasional dose of disagreeableness—and maybe the occasional swear word—can’t be true leaders. Likewise, a little neuroticism can be a good thing. If you score high on this scale, you can succeed as much, if not more, as your thick-skinned colleagues who score low.

First, there may be a positive relationship between neuroticism and creativity. Research has shown that anxious people tend to have a highly active imagination—which is why they tend to perceive threats before others or even when there are none. Again, we encounter the idea of the importance of separating signal from noise. Adam Perkins, lecturer in the neurobiology of personality at King’s College London, links a tendency to perceive threats, which he calls a “built-in threat generator,” to a “highly active imagination.”

However, Scott Barry Kaufman, a cognitive scientist and author, claims that the relationship between neuroticism and creativity is weak. Kaufman explains that another of the Big Five traits has a stronger correlation with creativity: openness to experience. (We’ll discuss this trait shortly.)

In this academic debate, I’m going to take the risk of conflating correlation with causation and side with Perkins. I submit that creativity is indeed one of the upsides of neuroticism. My view is based on a simple correlation: People in creative roles tend to score higher in neuroticism.

Second, neuroticism leads to vigilance and can be a source of motivation. I’m a little bit of a worrier. That makes me attentive, focused, even driven. People who are low in neuroticism can be satisfied with suboptimal circumstances.

This effect has roots in human evolution. It’s a survival tool that makes you better at detecting threats. The adjectives attentive and focused support the YD Law model that we discussed earlier: You need some anxiety or stress to operate at an optimal level.

Do you want your surgeon or your money manager not to be worried when there are real risks involved? In these roles, it’s better to risk worrying over nothing than to ignore something that could be important.

We saw that archery requires calm concentration, but the optimal performance point is not zero stress. Even Armstrong’s heartbeat rose to 150 when he landed the Lunar Module. If it hadn’t, perhaps the landing and the course of human history would have turned out differently. And I’m going to bet that even though I put them in the left column earlier, Snoop Dog and Seth Rogen have higher levels of neuroticism than their image suggests. As mentioned, neuroticism and creativity are correlated.

Also, based on years of counseling them, Dr. Zimet says that pro athletes tend to score high on neuroticism. It gives them drive. He says most athletes spend hours practicing not only because they love their sport or want to win but also because they’re terrified of losing. The same goes for most top students who spend hours studying before tests. Many of them are motivated by fear of failure.

Takeaways to improve your mental game

Some neuroticism is essential to reach peak performance. Anxious people tend to have a highly active imagination. They are intuitive innovators and problem-solvers. They come up with ingenious ideas before others. And they have a keen radar for sensing impending danger.

Remember that high performance has an anxiety cost. You must understand, accept, and harness the anxiety, both personally and within your organization. The best tool to do this may be the YD curves we discussed earlier, which map performance to stress levels and help you find the optimal point. The need for a dose of neuroticism is situation-specific. Heightened anxiety while driving will keep you alive. Being a perfectionist and highly neurotic about gardening will keep you in the yard forever. You must balance both sides of the brain and call upon one or the other as each situation demands.

And when all else fails, like Yoda try to be.

Principle 17: Be Like Jim Morrison and the Pope—Balance Openness and Structure

“It’s like some venereal disease or something. I just regard it as beneath contempt.”

—Charlie Munger, famous investor, talking about Bitcoin

In the early years of my career, I traveled the world to meet with the most powerful people in money management: the CEOs and Chief Investment Officers of the world’s largest pension plans, sovereign wealth funds, endowments, and insurance companies. My role was to provide advice and research to help them and their investment teams decide how to allocate billions of dollars across financial markets.

I repeatedly flew across the United States and to Canada, Europe, Japan, Australia, Singapore, China, Hong Kong, the Middle East, and South America. In each country, a salesperson would shepherd me from meeting to meeting. It was exhausting. To make it interesting, one day, I decided to ask each salesperson in each country I visited to try to find a local dish that I wouldn’t be game to at least try. I would say, with bravado: “I’ll bet you $10 that you can’t find anything that people eat here that I will not eat.”

I was showing openness, also known as openness to experience, the fourth of the Big Five traits. By participating in this food challenge, I was expressing a preference for variety, adventure, curiosity, and a willingness to try new things.

I’ve eaten organ meats, unusual sea creatures, and the like. And I’ve tried a few insects. You get the idea. But for the record, a friend once asked me to eat part of a scorpion that had been marinating for about a decade in a bottle of alcohol of dubious origin. We debated it for a couple of hours, but I chickened out. In my defence, so did everyone else.

Ultimately, this challenge is easier than you might think. The vital stipulation is that locals must eat the food. And over time, I’ve noticed that the more unusual the food, the more chefs camouflage the flavor with spices, oils, and deep-frying.

How do you feel about the challenge? If you’d never do such a thing, you may score low in openness. Conversely, if you think it might be fun to try—if only for the adventure and dinner conversation—you might score high in openness.

This test is merely anecdotal—not enough to fully describe openness.

Openness to experience is a longing, desire, or pull for novelty or newness. Wanting to try different things, experiment, see something you’ve never seen, or do something you’ve never done. Always trying something new on the menu at the restaurant.

On the other side of the scale, someone with low openness follows routines. There’s a pattern to their day. They’re comfortable mastering something they’ve done a thousand times.

Dr. Zimet, as a sport psychologist, explains that being low in openness helps elite athletes: “They need to spend thousands of hours perfecting the same movement. There’s a routine that comes with high-performance sports. Someone high in openness will get bored with repetitive training.”

Principle 17, “Be Like Jim Morrison and the Pope—Balance Openness and Structure,” invites you to learn when to show openness and when to bear down and follow the usual order.

The openness scale

As we did for the other traits, here are common descriptors for both sides of the openness scale and some celebrity guesses. While personality psychologists tend to focus on openness to experience, in the context of the psychology of leadership, I will emphasize how this scale influences one’s openness to change and new ideas (dogmatic and narrow in thinking vs. open-minded and likes to try new things).

![](image/Table06.jpg)

Looking at the celebrity guesses, on the low openness side, you’ll notice people who represent institutions built on convention and rituals: the Queen and the Pope. Their job is to perpetuate tradition and resist change as much as possible. Howard Stern doesn’t travel and almost always eats the same thing every day. Warren Buffett is also a man of habit. He has lived in the same modest house (for a billionaire) for 60 years, which he purchased for $31,500 and would be worth about $250,000 now. He eats, in his words, “like a six-year-old.” He loves ice cream and drinks five Coca-Colas daily. Also, he eats breakfast at the same McDonald’s every morning, where, according to Bob Bryan of Business Insider, he follows a ritual: “If stocks are up, he gets a bacon, egg, and cheese biscuit. If they’re down, he opts for a cheaper breakfast of two sausage patties. If the market is flat, he goes for the sausage McMuffin.”

Buffett’s low openness, in my opinion, has helped his success as an investor. He doesn’t chase fads. Instead of investing with the crowd, he has remained disciplined—you could even say dogmatic—about a set of simple rules for so-called value investing.

This style of investing goes back over 80 years to Benjamin Graham. Unsurprisingly, Buffett and his late partner, Charlie Munger, haven’t jumped onto the cryptocurrency bandwagon. Charlie Munger described crypto as “rat poison.”

In contrast, so-called “growth” investors are well served by their high level of openness. These investors like to imagine what the future will look like. They look for disruptors rather than disrupted companies. Think social media vs. newspapers, electric vs. gasoline cars, and ride-sharing companies vs. yellow taxis. Amazon, Netflix, Google, and Tesla are all growth companies. Growth portfolio managers want to be on the right side of change.

The founder of my firm, Thomas Rowe Price, is often credited with pioneering growth investing. Notice how this quote from him speaks to openness: “It is better to be early than too late in recognizing the passing of one’s era, the waning of old investment favorites, and the advent of a new era affording new opportunities to the investor.”

Which is the superior style of investing, the lower-openness value style (à la Buffett), or the higher-openness growth style? Both. It depends on the portfolio manager’s personality and skills. Value and growth investing are different games. “Core” or diversified investors need to be flexible between value and growth, depending on the conditions and opportunities. These managers are the biathletes of the investment world.

Openness is a good quality in a leader. You must drive change within your organization and adapt to trends in your industry. However, you need balance. You must focus on a key set of priorities if you want your organization to remain competitive. A few things done very well are better than many things done half-assed. You may subscribe to the build fast and fail fast school of thought common in technology startups and companies like Amazon, and that’s okay as long as you’re prepared to shut down underperforming initiatives.

Openness is a fuzzy trait

Remember that the Big Five are not set in stone—personality can be situational. Charlie Munger didn’t show much openness when he talked about certain growth stocks and new technologies, but he was a voracious reader and open to different philosophies. As for my earlier personal example, I illustrated my high openness through my exotic food challenge. Yet, at work, I eat the same salad for lunch every day. During workdays, I’m usually “stressed,” at the top of my YD curve, so I use food as fuel more than enjoyment. Also, it’s easier to control my calorie intake with a daily routine, and I reduce decision fatigue by not wasting mental energy deciding what to eat.

Take a minute and ask yourself in which situations you display high openness and in which situations you display low openness. I bet you can find examples of both. Maybe you’re dogmatic in your political views, but you’re game to try the occasional recreational drug? Perhaps you’re not interested in traveling, but you frequently try new hobbies, such as photography, chess, or wine tasting?

Openness is the loosest of the traits. It’s definitely observed in the English language, but doesn’t appear in all cultures.

Perhaps the biggest academic knock against openness comes from a fascinating 2010 study in neuroscience titled “Testing Predictions from Personality Neuroscience: Brain Structure and the Big Five.” This paper shows evidence that the volume of different regions in your brain predicts your personality after controlling for sex, age, and whole-brain volume. In theory, it means that I can scan your brain and tell you whether you’re an introvert or an extrovert, whether you’re high or low in neuroticism, and so on. The only trait that I wouldn’t be able to predict just by looking at your brain is openness.

Openness may be more situational than the other traits, but it’s important nonetheless. As a leader, you will be well served by understanding it in yourself and your team members. I would not hire someone low in openness in a think tank and would be hesitant about assigning someone high in openness to a long-term, routine project.

Also, you must show openness if you want to be a champion of diversity and inclusion. Here, I refer to all aspects of diversity, with cognitive diversity and team performance as the ultimate goals. You can’t call yourself a leader if you’re not open to new ideas. And you can’t call yourself a leader if you’re biased against hiring people who don’t look and think like you. With respect to inclusion, you must encourage people who think differently to participate in problem-solving, even if it doesn’t come naturally to you.

In that sense, it’s a good thing that openness is less innate than the other traits. You can learn it, practice it, and get better at it over time. When you collaborate with someone, you must adjust your approach based on your understanding of the person’s openness. Most of the time, you need to sell an idea to someone. “In life, we’re always selling,” my father used to say. No matter how smart you are, soft skills are essential for career success. In reviewing the Big Five model, I aim to help you improve these soft skills.

How can a better understanding of someone’s openness make you a better leader? Some of my colleagues almost always say “no” to a new idea that requires their buy-in. That’s okay. I’ve learned that I can avoid unnecessary conflicts by recognizing that it’s merely how they process new ideas. Skepticism is their default position. So, I give them time and answer their questions patiently. Interestingly, those who start negatively on a new idea almost always become my strongest advocates after their questions are answered.

Some people are too open. They chase too many new ideas rather than executing well on a handful. Are you trying to do too much—launch too many products, make too many enhancements to your process, and expand in too many new markets? It’s an excellent question to ask yourself as a leader: Is your level of openness so over the top that you’re chasing too many rabbits? Focus requires you to keep your openness within bounds.

In addition to a lack of focus, the other issue with extreme openness may be a lack of healthy skepticism. I mentioned colleagues whose default position is always “no.” I also have colleagues whose default position is always “yes.” Be careful when you seek honest feedback from yes-people. Leaders must encourage loyal opposition. You must allow people to respectfully disagree. The ability to test your ideas with the devil’s advocate will only make them stronger. You’ll start thinking about angles you hadn’t thought about. Putting your cherished idea on the chopping block is uncomfortable, but what’s the worst that can happen? If it gets chopped, it probably wasn’t worth pursuing in the first place. If it survives, you now have more confidence in it. And you’ve benefitted from input that will make it even better. The best negotiators, salespeople, and debaters show high openness because they listen carefully to the other side.

As I mentioned earlier, fund manager Ray Dalio emphasizes radical truth and radical transparency. Dalio doesn’t couch his philosophy in the Big Five terminology, but he argues for extreme openness to new ideas and challenging the status quo. However, in his book The Fund, Rob Copeland offers a caveat: When it really matters, Copeland claims that staffers better agree with Dalio or risk getting fired.

Other thought leaders point to the need for high openness to foster an “idea meritocracy” (Dalio’s term) and produce accurate forecasts. In his book Think Again, Adam Grant argues that people should welcome differing viewpoints with curiosity rather than the usual fight-or-flight response. Phil Tetlock and Dan Gardner, in Superforecasting: The Art and Science of Prediction, explain that the most talented forecasters are those who work in teams, admit their mistakes, and change course when needed.

Agreeableness vs. openness: what’s the difference?

You might argue that I’m conflating agreeableness and openness. Disagreeable people tend to say no and be skeptical of your ideas. Is a lack of openness the same as being disagreeable? Perhaps there’s a correlation. However, as I describe yes-people and no-people based on openness to new ideas, I’m implicitly holding agreeableness constant. For the same level of agreeableness, openness can vary.

Here’s a thought experiment: Can a highly disagreeable person score high in openness? I think so. Guess who I’m trying to describe, with the hint that we talked about this person in an earlier chapter: He’s an imaginative person who’s open to new ideas—extreme fruit-based diets, Japanese culture and aesthetics, and new technologies—but who’s disagreeable and hard to work with.

That’s Steve Jobs.

What about the other side of the scale? Can people with low openness be agreeable? Low openness makes it hard to be agreeable if it means changing your routine to please someone. Still, I can think of plenty of people around me who aren’t particularly adventurous yet are quite agreeable. Can you? For a famous example, consider that extremely low openness was part of Queen Elizabeth II’s job definition. Yet the show The Crown suggests that she was agreeable. She had to conform to royal traditions and work with hard-headed prime ministers without expressing her opinion. Early in her tenure, she was too agreeable. People around her, her handlers, made decisions for her. Until she put her foot down. I don’t know what’s fact vs. fiction in The Crown, but my point remains valid.

Takeaways to improve your mental game

You can learn, practice, and improve your openness. Doing so will boost your creativity and reduce your potential bias against hiring people who don’t look and think like you. You must also encourage everyone—especially those who think differently—to participate in debates.

On the other hand, focus is as critical as openness. Don’t chase too many new ideas at once. Instead, execute a handful of them exceptionally well. Be skeptical of fads.

As a leader, read the level of openness in those around you. Some people will be more attached to tradition than others. Adjust how you inspire, convince, influence, or sell them. Give people with low openness time to process your change agenda. Rein in those with high openness before they chase too many rabbits. And build cognitively diverse teams by combining contributors with low and high openness.

Lastly, don’t listen to Warren Buffet—six-year-olds should not drink Coca-Cola.

Principle 18: Eat Your Vegetables—Unleash the Power of Good Habits

“Willpower is a learnable skill.”

—Charles Duhigg, author of The Power of Habit

Descriptors of conscientiousness, the last but not least of the Big Five, include self-discipline, attention to detail, and dutifulness. Think of people who are organized, punctual, and thorough. A-students, if you will. Research shows that conscientiousness explains five times as much variation in GPA (Grade Point Average) across students as does intelligence.

Several years ago, I learned this the hard way. The toughest exam I ever took was not in one of my father’s classes. It wasn’t any of the three notoriously grueling, 4.5-hour Chartered Financial Analyst (CFA) certification exams. It was in an accounting class during the first year of my Master’s in Finance program.

I prepared as I always did: I ignored the upcoming exam until the night before, then panicked and crammed. This method is frowned upon, but it has always worked for me. Most of the top students cram for exams. The stress motivated me (think back to our YD Law). When I sat down to take the exam, the knowledge was fresh—I recognized problems I worked on only a few hours before. When teaching CFA (Chartered Financial Analyst) prep classes, I would tell the students that knowledge has a half-life. The closer they studied to the exam, the more they would retain for the test. I would bet that if a student studies two hours six months before a test and another studies the same materials for one hour the day before the test, they’re about equally ready.

I never liked accounting; I preferred mathematical topics. I studied advanced math and physics in Cégep, a mandatory two-year college education unique to Québec, Canada. To me, accounting is more about memorization than logic. Accounting is a set of rules you must learn, whether intuitive or not. You must have a passion for tedious work. If I show you a box of tangled electric wires, what’s your reaction? Do you feel the urge to untangle them? Or does your head start to hurt just looking at the cables? If you get excited about the flow state you’ll get in while you untangle the wires, I bet you’d love accounting. When I see such a box of wires, I feel the urge to spray lighter fluid and light a match to it. (With apologies to accountants—I respect and admire your detailed work. It’s just not for me.)

That evening, as I was cramming, I realized something was wrong. I couldn’t get my head around some of the problems. Normally, that wouldn’t be an issue. I would push through and get the typical a-ha moments. But the box of wires was too big. Hundreds, even thousands, of wires (accounting rules) tangled together. Too much material to learn in too little time. We were expected to analyze income statements and balance sheets for two separate companies and, under various accounting standards, including international rules, create a new set of books assuming the companies merged. Basically, simulate a merger on paper by creating new financial statements from scratch. For example, we had to calculate how pension liabilities would be treated under various conventions. And if you got the first few steps wrong, all the other numbers would be wrong.

Over the years of studying business, I had slowly accumulated a deficit in accounting despite getting decent grades. But this was a Master’s level class. Without the basics at my fingertips, I was doomed. The bill was coming due: My accounting knowledge deficit was finally catching up to me. Cramming doesn’t create durable knowledge: You commit knowledge to short-term memory and then forget it after the test. So, I had years of cramming to blame.

I had never been so nervous before a test—I believe it’s the closest I ever came to having a panic attack in my entire life. I remember rhetorically asking a friend before the test whether you could die from being too nervous. As a student, despite the cramming, I took tests seriously. My sense of self-worth was tied to my grades, and as I’ve mentioned before, well, I’m competitive (but it’s okay if you sneeze harder than me).

I had to leave several questions blank. Leaving questions blank is the kiss of death for any exam. Answering something is always better than nothing. I failed the test by a wide margin. It was a shock because I had never failed an exam. I was starting the semester near the bottom of the class. Fortunately, I managed to climb back up near the top by the end of the semester. And I learned a valuable lesson in conscientiousness.

Principle 18, “Eat Your Vegetables—Unleash the Power of Good Habits,” teaches you to maximize the positive side of consciousness. I encourage you to increase conscientiousness while avoiding the pitfalls associated with extreme levels.

The conscientiousness scale

Below are the descriptors and my celebrity guesses for both sides of the conscientiousness scale.

![](image/Table07.jpg)

Conscientiousness is a tendency towards order, systematization, logic, reason, neatness, and structure. You want a conscientious person to write code or work in a lab. Think of someone tidy, neat, and organized. Someone who does things exactly as they’re supposed to.

Someone low in conscientiousness is freewheeling, less bound by rules, and easy-going. Think of someone who shrugs their shoulders at a lot of things. Their desk is usually messy. Of the five traits, conscientiousness is the most preferred in the workplace. People who score low in this trait tend to miss deadlines, show up late and unprepared at meetings, and hand over poor-quality work.

Teams benefit from a culture where people show up on time for meetings, follow up on their promises, and pay attention to detail—as long as the process does not become “the thing,” as Jeff Bezos said. Remember that you lead by example, so keep your desk clean and be punctual!

Of course, you need to watch out for perfectionism. Sometimes leaders need to abandon conscientiousness when fast decisions need to be made and there’s no time to sweat the details, but in business and in life, details matter more often than not.

Importantly, conscientiousness is the most important trait you can work on for self-improvement. Small changes in your daily habits can lead to big results over time. I’ll give you several tricks or hacks to become more conscientious, but remember that the advice I give for self-improvement in this chapter easily translates to managing individuals and teams.

Essentially, conscientiousness is a super-trait. If you’re going to pick one of the Big Five to improve on, this is it. Research shows conscientiousness correlates with academic and career success, while at best, the evidence is mixed for the other four traits of the Big Five model. Some people think CEOs don’t deserve their pay. I don’t know, but I can say that every CEO I’ve met is extremely conscientious. CEOs have their sh\*t together. They start their day early, work hard, are dependable and organized, and get things done. The best athletes are also known to be highly conscientious. You may think their success is all about innate talent, but it’s not. Michael Jordan, Simon Biles, Tom Brady, Serena Williams, and Tiger Woods… all practiced harder than everyone else. CEOs and top athletes are disciplined. They’re focused.

Here are a couple of quotes from Michael Jordan:

The moment you get away from fundamentals—whether it’s proper technique, work ethic, or mental preparation—the bottom can fall out of your game, your schoolwork, your job, whatever you’re doing.

I’ve always believed that if you put in the work, the results will come. I don’t do things half-heartedly, because I know that if do, then I can expect half-hearted results.

That’s conscientiousness!

And Tom Brady? He was recruited as the 199th pick in the NFL draft. Yet he became the GOAT (Greatest of All Time) by outworking everybody. His self-discipline is legendary. Here’s a glimpse at his diet:

* 80% fruits, vegetables, whole grains, nuts, seeds, and legumes.
* 20% protein (chicken, red meat, seafood).
* Eat until you’re 75% full.
* No dairy or nightshades.
* Halve your body weight in pounds and drink that many ounces of water.
* Max two cups of coffee /no caffeine after noon.
* No food within three hours of going to bed.

Here are some of his views on hard work and self-discipline:

If you want to perform at the highest level, then you have to prepare at the highest level.

You can’t go out and practice average on Wednesday, average on Thursday, okay on Friday and then expect to play well on Sunday.

The more you study the lives of highly successful people in sports, business, and other fields, the more you’ll find that conscientiousness is the key factor to their success. Think of Angela Duckworth’s work on grit as a success factor. Or Admiral McRaven’s famous book Make Your Bed, which emphasizes self-discipline in all aspects of your life, starting with making your bed in the morning.

What’s more, conscientious people tend to live happier lives and have better relationships. And they’re healthier.

So, conscientiousness is awesome, right? It looks that way.

To have low conscientiousness certainly doesn’t look appealing. Research has linked low conscientiousness with criminal behavior, drug addiction, imprisonment, homelessness, and unemployment.

The good news is that you can learn conscientiousness. Recall that the Big Five aren’t as innate as you might think. There’s no statistical evidence that conscientiousness is heritable. The best way to develop your conscientiousness may be to build habits. Charles Duhigg, in The Power of Habit, explains that you can master new habits through the powerful cue-behavior-reward feedback loop. The cue is the event or situation that triggers your habit. The behavior is the habit you want to create. The reward is something you give yourself every time you perform the desired behavior. Here’s a powerful secret to building habits: It doesn’t matter how big the reward is, as long as there is one.

A personal example of the power of habits

I’ve mentioned that my day job is intense. I work long hours every day of the week, as I have for my entire career. I used to work a fair amount on weekends, too, but these days I only do so when absolutely necessary. My job takes all my mental energy on the professional side.

So, people often ask me how I find the time to write books. Did you know that 81% of Americans want to write a book?

First, I like to write. It doesn’t feel like work. Writing is an engaging hobby, like golf, exercising, gardening, or fixing cars. Also, for this book, I followed Duhigg’s process. My habit was to write at least 1,100 words per weekend. I didn’t allow myself any exception to this rule. I spent two to three hours on Saturday mornings and one to two hours on Sunday mornings, usually before the kids got up and begged me to make crepes. That’s it. Little by little. As I write this, I’m almost done with my first draft of the book. Here’s a chart of the number of words I’ve written over the last 38 weeks and the cumulative number of words:

![](image/Bookprogress.png)

The cue to write was simple: It’s Saturday or Sunday morning, therefore, I need to write. The reward was updating a spreadsheet with the number of words I had written. Just updating the numbers was deeply satisfying. I’m not kidding when I say the reward can be almost anything—a checked box in your workout app, a cookie, a high five… Alcoholics Anonymous uses key chains that mark someone’s number of days sober. I’ve found that maintaining streaks is profoundly motivating. For more than ten years, I’ve exercised every day for a minimum of 30 minutes except for one day when I had back spasms and couldn’t move, and about three days each following appendix removal and deviated septum surgeries. The critical part of the habit-creation process is consistency with the reward. Adding one day to your streak is a reward.

James Clear’s Atomic Habits is one of the highest-rated and most popular self-improvement books of all time. It has 120,000 Amazon ratings, with an average of 4.8/5, and has sold over 15 million copies.

Clear explains the power of small habits: “It is so easy to overestimate the importance of one defining moment and underestimate the value of small improvements on a daily basis.” To which he adds a metaphor that resonates with me, as a money manager: “Habits are the compound interest of self-improvement. We often dismiss small changes because they don’t seem to matter very much in the moment.”

When I gave the MIT students advice, I asked them to look at the trend in their career, rather than obsess over daily ups and downs. The control theory model we discussed supported this notion. Clear concurs: “Success is the product of daily habits. … You should be far more concerned with your current trajectory than with your current results.”

Clear delivers pure wisdom and practical advice. No hyperbole is needed. If you make improvement a daily habit, the sky’s the limit (oops, hyperbole). The next chart shows how the math works. It’s like compound interest in a retirement account. This is just an illustration, but suppose you get 1% better at something every day for a year. At the end of the year, you’ll be 37 times better. And if you get 1% worse every day? You’ll end up at -97% below your initial capability, close to zero. This negative result is important. It means that small bad habits (think: eating a bit too much) can ruin your life over time.

Try to get a little more conscientious every day for the next year. You’ll create a virtuous circle, becoming more conscientious about becoming more conscientious. Getting better at getting better. Find a way to keep track in a spreadsheet or an app. Give yourself the daily reward by checking a box for starting your workday 30 minutes earlier, meditating 20 minutes, helping your kids with homework, exercising, eating within your calorie limit, or sleeping eight hours. Start small, maybe with a single habit. Then, add habits, maybe once a month. If you miss a day, don’t worry; keep going and keep tracking. The key is to unleash the power of compounding.

![](image/Compounding.png)

The downsides of conscientiousness

Are there downsides to conscientiousness? You bet. Here are some of the caveats to the conscientiousness is awesome mantra: High levels of it have been associated with difficulty improvising and changing course when difficulties arise, lack of creativity, perfectionism, workaholism, and from a leadership perspective, lack of delegation (micromanaging).

Overly conscientious leaders tend to lower team accountability because they carry too much on their shoulders. They may exhibit intolerant or harsh attitudes towards people who don’t meet their standards. Also, extreme levels of conscientiousness have even been associated with obsessive-compulsive behaviors and burnout.

There isn’t much serious research on the benefits of low conscientiousness, but you can think of the other side of some of these issues: the ability to improvise, to be spontaneous, and to be creative. Also, procrastination can be good because it gives you more time to think about the problem you’re trying to solve, hence the idea of sleeping on it. Sometimes, you procrastinate because you need time to work through an issue or incubate an idea.

The downsides of perfectionism

I’ve mentioned before that I manage a high-performing team. Conscientiousness runs high in our division. Everyone works hard, pays attention to details, and delivers high-quality work. Each member of my team was an A-student. But perfectionism can be an issue. In the fast-paced business world, you often need to deliver work that’s 80% perfect in 20% of the time it would take to strive for perfection.

Suppose I want to investigate the relationship between oil price spikes and subsequent stock market returns. You could write a PhD thesis on this topic. You could build a theoretical model, identify regime changes, test different econometric methodologies, and control for a host of variables such as GDP growth, inflation, interest rates, and changes in the U.S. dollar. But what if I need an answer for our Asset Allocation Committee meeting the following day? In that case, you can download the data and run a simple correlation analysis in about 30 minutes. The answer will come with caveats, but it will be good enough for a qualitative analysis of the current environment. The study will be simple, hence easy to communicate. Moreover, the data will be recent, reflecting recent market conditions. A lot of conscientious high-performers struggle with these types of requests. They don’t like to cut corners.

This example illustrates how perfectionism often gets in the way of decision-making. People high in conscientiousness are deeply concerned about making mistakes, which can cause them to freeze up when an ambiguous decision needs to be made.

To be clear, much of our work requires in-depth analysis with attention to detail. The key is to know when it’s appropriate to use the 80–20 rule and when it’s not. I need precision if I ask for a performance attribution to show our board of directors or a client. No cutting corners. If someone trades billions of dollars, buying and selling stocks, there’s zero room for error. And anything related to accounting, of course, is the realm of conscientious analysts.

The constant challenge in business is to filter tasks and prioritize when to go in-depth and when to deliver something quickly. Most conscientious people, especially at the beginning of their careers, have only one speed, one way to approach their work: with extreme professionalism and dedication to exactitude. Over time, they must learn that sometimes, for lack of a better expression, they need to wing it.

I learned this lesson early in my career. During an internship in money management, my boss asked me to model the cash going in and out of our institutional client accounts. These clients often deposited millions of dollars in retirement contributions, only to withdraw the funds a few days later to pay benefits. The cash constantly moved in and out of the stock and bond markets, creating friction and missed opportunities to stay invested. My boss wanted to extend credit to these clients when they needed cash so they would remain invested. He asked me to model the benefit to our clients and the risks involved with increasing leverage (i.e., allowing overdrafts) in these accounts. With his help, I built an incredibly complex simulation, projecting future market returns and simulating the cash movements. It was my first big project, and I wanted to do it perfectly.

But it was impossible to account for every detail. I got lost in the weeds. My spreadsheet became so complex that my boss had to create a simpler version. In theory, my model was better, but it was difficult to explain and involved assumptions on top of other assumptions.

I had a good relationship with my boss, and I was young and brash, so I insisted that my model was better. With less than six months of experience in the corporate world, I didn’t care about the politics of challenging an experienced professional who happened to be my direct supervisor. He decided to teach me a valuable lesson. He said: “Okay, why don’t we each present our approach to the head of our division and let him decide?”

You can imagine what happened. I came across as dogmatic about my ideas, and the simpler model prevailed. It was easier to understand and precise enough to get a broad idea of the proposal’s benefits. My approach may have been closer to perfection, but it was unnecessarily complex and introduced assumptions on small effects irrelevant to the big picture.

Perfectionism also becomes an issue when it prevents companies from bringing products to market on time, ceding market share to competitors. Successful technology startups and established companies like Amazon, Google, and Tesla get around this issue by releasing beta versions of their products and following the fail-fast-learn-fast approach. Minimum viable products (MVPs) are designed to get around perfectionism by allowing for an early collision between ideas and consumers. With an MVP, you’re not looking for the best features. You’re looking for the minimum set of features that will make the first version of your product viable. Improvements will come later, with the benefit of real-world experience.

Tesla’s release of self-driving capabilities has been controversial because it doesn’t work perfectly. I drive a Tesla. I feel safe using self-driving (also known as “autopilot”), but I need to pay attention and be ready to take over at any time. Why would Tesla release a product that’s not perfect? Because they want to be first to market. Tesla is a technology company that makes a car, as opposed to a car company that deploys technology as an add-on. Perhaps more importantly, they record all the data from drivers using the system—representing billions of miles driven. These data feed into their artificial intelligence/machine learning software. Every mile driven makes autopilot incrementally better. Think about how much of a competitive advantage they have over a company that may still be waiting for their system to work perfectly before releasing it to consumers.

Takeaways to improve your mental game

Cognitive diversity is a good thing, and pairing people with high and low levels of conscientiousness increases team performance. Some team members will be more comfortable with imprecise, fluid, and fast work, while others will be happy to handle more detailed parts of the project and take on responsibility for error-checking.

I encourage you to develop personal and organizational habits that improve conscientiousness, while managing the potential downsides. Conscientiousness comes from a collection of small things: showing up on time, creating routines and checklists for quality control, rewarding excellence, keeping track of business win streaks, and more. At the same time, you must watch for perfectionism and workaholism and continue to foster flexibility and creativity. Like so many of the principles in this book, once again, we learn that the psychology of leadership is nuanced. Mastering the mental game of leadership requires self-awareness, awareness of others, and, more than anything else, balance.

Please give yourself a small reward for reading this principle. A nice coffee? A piece of chocolate? A short walk outside?

Takeaways on Personality Psychology

Personality psychology is nuanced. It’s a world of grey areas. The definitions of good or bad traits are open to interpretation, often a matter of degree. Nothing is constant, either. People change. In the short run, our personality changes depending on the situation. In the long run, it changes in more profound and permanent ways. If traumatic enough, a single event can change your personality permanently. With decades of research and thousands of studies on the Big Five, we’re still scratching the surface.

Now that we’ve reviewed the Big Five traits (extraversion, agreeableness, neuroticism, openness, and conscientiousness), let’s summarize how the traits compare to each other in terms of heritability (i.e., how much you can develop each trait vs. being born with it), and how each of them predicts leadership success.

Earlier, I mentioned the person-situation debate when I talked about my ex-colleague, the Human Piranha on Steroids, and his cartoonish level of extraversion. We may exhibit different levels of the traits depending on the situation. I act like an introvert at cocktail parties at industry conferences, but I turn into an extrovert when I’m with close friends.

A related question is the degree to which the traits are hereditary, i.e., part of your DNA, or whether we learn them over time. A meta-study of 134 studies on this subject, published in 2015 and covering over 100,000 people, suggests that your personality traits are about 40% genetic (hence heritable) and 60% environmental. The following chart shows estimates of the genetic component of each of the Big Five. The lines indicating the confidence intervals tell you that roughly 95% of the estimates fall within that range.

You can learn, practice, develop, and improve your personality traits. People change. You can change. The five traits have about the same level of heritability, although openness (41% heritable) may be harder to develop than conscientiousness (31% heritable) on the margin. Remember that there’s no good or bad trait. You might want to add more or less of any of them to the extent that it helps you balance your personality and be a better leader, develop better relationships, and achieve your goals.

Percentage heritability of Big Five traits

![](image/Heritabilityofbigfive.png)

Source: Vukasović, T., & Bratko, D. (2015). Heritability of personality: A meta-analysis of behavior genetic studies. Psychological Bulletin, 141(4), 769–785. doi.org/10.1037/bul0000017.

I found the result that openness is the most heritable trait surprising. As I mentioned, it doesn’t show up as much as the other traits in brain imaging studies. Why is it the most heritable trait but doesn’t show in brain imaging? As with so many things about the human mind and so many of the models we’ve covered in this book, we must admit that there’s still a lot we don’t know.

Conscientiousness is awesome

It’s encouraging that conscientiousness is the least heritable trait. In leadership studies, which I’ll discuss shortly, it shows up as a desirable trait. As long as you don’t go into extremes, and keep perfectionism and workaholism in check, you should and can develop your conscientiousness, perhaps using some of the advice on developing habits I mentioned earlier.

But here’s an important caveat: It’s hard to tweak your personality. Just because a trait is 69% not heritable, doesn’t mean it’s easy to change. You may have developed low conscientiousness over decades. It may be a lot of work to change. You’ll have to decide if it’s worth the effort. If you’re an entrepreneur, consider that a study from a startup incubator concludes that no one below the 30th percentile in conscientiousness can run a successful business. You can be below the median and still succeed. But if you’re in the bottom 30%? You’re probably too disorganized and undisciplined to succeed.

A meta-squared study (a study of meta-studies—I wonder if anyone’s ever run a meta-cubed study: a meta-study of meta-studies of meta-studies?) of more than 100 years’ worth of research—representing over 1.1 million participants—reveals that conscientiousness is the top predictor of job performance. Makes sense. You don’t need to study 1.1 million people to intuit that character traits like rule-following, self-discipline, focus, tenacity, and being organized predict job performance.
